The current condition is examined in relation to the challenge, a next target condition is established, and experiments are used to learn how to close the gap. Once that condition is reached or the learning changes what is understood, another target condition can be set. This creates a chain of manageable advances rather than a single large implementation project. The relationship matters: a local improvement should be connected to the organization's direction, not pursued simply because it is an attractive technical change.
Study instruction:
- "Map one departmental or organizational vision to a near-term process condition that would represent a meaningful step toward it."
Key point:
- The vision supplies direction; the target condition supplies the next practical destination.
- Progress is built through successive learning steps.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
5. Q: How does a target condition align people and reduce unproductive debate?
A: Show answer
A target condition turns a discussion from a contest between personal proposals into a shared challenge. When the team agrees that a process must, for example, produce a stable cycle with a defined sequence and a controlled buffer, participants can evaluate ideas against that condition rather than against personal preference. This does not eliminate disagreement; it changes the subject of disagreement from competing opinions to evidence about how the condition can be achieved. The condition also makes trade-offs explicit. Cost, safety, quality, capacity, and timing become constraints to solve within, rather than reasons to abandon the direction prematurely. Alignment therefore comes from a common description of success, not from requiring everyone to prefer the same countermeasure.
Study instruction:
- "Reframe a current disagreement as a question about the process condition that all participants need to achieve."
Key point:
- A shared target condition depersonalizes the challenge.
- Different ideas can be tested against the same required condition.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
6. Q: What is the difference between a target condition and a countermeasure?
A: Show answer
A target condition describes the state to be achieved; a countermeasure is an action or change intended to move the process toward that state. For example, a target condition might specify that a part should pass a reference point every 16 seconds with a stable sequence. Moving a parts tray closer, changing replenishment timing, or redesigning an operator's work may be possible countermeasures. If the target condition is written as 'install a new conveyor' or 'introduce kanban', it has already prescribed a solution and may conceal the real need. Keeping the two separate allows the team to learn which action works, observe its effect, and change it if necessary while retaining the intended condition. Countermeasures should be selected and tested after the target is clear.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three solution-based statements as descriptions of the desired process state, then list possible countermeasures separately."
Key point:
- A target condition defines where the process should be; a countermeasure defines an action to try.
- Do not embed a preferred solution in the target condition.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
7. Q: What is the relationship between an initial challenge and a fully detailed target condition?
A: Show answer
In uncertain situations, an initial challenge may be a deliberately broad description of the desired next state. As the team observes the work and learns about constraints, it can add detail until the condition becomes specific enough to guide daily improvement. The chapter uses the term challenge for the initial, less-detailed state and target condition for the fuller description. This distinction helps avoid false precision. A team developing a new product or reorganizing an unfamiliar process may not yet know customer needs, technical limits, or the true current condition. It should state the direction clearly but leave uncertain details open. As experiments reveal facts, the team can refine the description without casually changing the underlying challenge or its achieve-by date.
Study instruction:
- "For a project with significant uncertainty, write a basic challenge and identify which details can only be established after observation or testing."
Key point:
- Begin with enough definition to move, not with invented precision.
- Add detail as learning clarifies the process and obstacles.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
8. Q: Which parts of a target condition should be measurable?
A: Show answer
At least some aspects should be measurable or directly observable so that the team can determine whether the condition has been reached. Measures may include cycle time by step, fluctuation between successive cycles, sequence adherence, number and location of operators, buffer quantity, lot size, or the location where one-piece flow is expected. Not every important feature can be reduced to a number. A target condition may also describe qualitative characteristics such as a reliable handoff, clear responsibility, or a safe method. The practical test is whether the team can observe the process and make a defensible judgment about whether the desired condition exists. Measures should illuminate process behavior rather than substitute for understanding it.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed target condition, identify at least two measurable indicators and one qualitative feature that can be checked at the worksite."
Key point:
- A target condition needs observable evidence of attainment.
- Use measures to reveal process behavior, not to replace direct understanding.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
9. Q: What information should a manufacturing-process target condition contain?
A: Show answer
The chapter groups the information into four categories. First are process steps, sequence, and times: the work elements needed for a complete cycle, their order, duration, and responsible operator. Second are process characteristics, such as operator and shift count, planned one-piece-flow locations, buffer locations and quantities, lot size, changeover time, and the leveling pattern. Third are process metrics used during operation, including actual cycle times and cycle-to-cycle fluctuation. Fourth are outcome metrics used periodically, such as output, productivity, quality, cost, and variation in shift output. The first three categories support daily improvement because they describe how the process should function and where it differs. Outcome measures help assess whether the improvement effort is producing the broader result.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a target-condition sheet using the four categories and separate real-time process measures from periodic outcome measures."
Key point:
- Describe work sequence, process characteristics, process metrics, and outcome metrics.
- Process measures guide improvement; outcome measures assess results.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
10. Q: Why must a target condition be based on a detailed grasp of the current condition?
A: Show answer
A target condition that is disconnected from the actual process can be either too easy to matter or too ambitious to achieve within the available time. Direct observation reveals the work sequence, actual cycle variation, material movements, interruptions, quality losses, and workarounds that may not appear in reports. Analysis then helps distinguish recurring process characteristics from isolated events and assumptions. Understanding the current condition also helps the team choose a meaningful next step rather than copying a generic lean technique. The chapter recommends beginning with direct process analysis and using data from the work itself. The more unfamiliar or variable the situation, the more cautious the team should be about specifying details before seeing how the work really operates.
Study instruction:
- "Observe the process at the point of work and record actual sequence, timing, interruptions, material flow, and abnormal conditions before proposing a target."
Key point:
- The target condition must be grounded in observed reality.
- Reports and opinions alone may conceal important process behavior.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
11. Q: How much detail should be included when the current condition is not yet fully understood?
A: Show answer
Use a basic but thoughtful description and avoid specifying details that are still conjecture. The chapter advises erring slightly toward a less detailed target condition when the current state is unclear, then adding precision as the team learns about obstacles. This is not permission to make the target meaningless. The direction, key performance characteristics, and achieve-by date should be clear enough to guide action. What remains uncertain should be explicitly treated as something to investigate. An early experiment can itself be a way of analyzing the current condition: attempt to operate toward the target and observe what actually happens. The target is then fleshed out with evidence rather than rewritten to fit the first obstacle encountered.
Study instruction:
- "Mark each element of a draft target condition as known, estimated, or unknown; leave unsupported detail open for investigation."
Key point:
- Do not confuse detailed wording with reliable knowledge.
- Refine the target condition as evidence accumulates.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
12. Q: What makes a target condition a useful stretch rather than an easy task or an impossible wish?
A: Show answer
A useful target condition lies beyond current capability but remains potentially achievable through focused learning, resourcefulness, and improvement within the time and resource constraints. If the team can see exactly how to achieve it from the outset, the work may be routine implementation or a rearrangement of existing methods rather than a meaningful improvement challenge. If it is so ambitious that the team cannot make credible progress, it may create frustration or encourage superficial compliance. The chapter describes the motivating value of a challenging but achievable stretch: when people overcome uncertainty through learning, they experience a genuine sense of accomplishment. The appropriate degree of stretch is a judgment developed through repeated practice and knowledge of the process.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the proposed condition with current capability and explain what is known, what is uncertain, and why the challenge is demanding but plausible."
Key point:
- The route should not be obvious at the outset.
- A useful stretch is demanding without being detached from evidence and constraints.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
13. Q: Why should a team avoid using return-on-investment calculations to choose the target condition itself?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns against allowing a conventional cost-benefit calculation to decide the desired process state before the team understands what is needed. An ROI screen can favor familiar, easily quantified changes and reject a necessary challenge because its benefits or route are not yet known. The target condition should first express the process state needed to move toward the vision; the team then works to achieve it within budget, safety, and other constraints. This does not mean ignoring cost or approving unlimited spending. Resource limits remain real design constraints, and the team must develop economical countermeasures. The distinction is between choosing the destination based only on a forecast return and solving creatively within the resources available to reach a needed condition.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the business need and required process condition from the later evaluation of alternative countermeasures, costs, and constraints."
Key point:
- Define the needed condition before evaluating how to achieve it.
- Cost discipline belongs in developing countermeasures, not in replacing the improvement challenge.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
14. Q: What time horizon is appropriate for a target condition?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes several horizons. A one-year condition may align with annual planning or a long project, but it is generally too distant to guide effective daily learning on its own. For production processes, the author suggests three months as a practical maximum and recommends breaking longer horizons into interim conditions. For people learning the improvement kata, one to four weeks often provides useful practice with complete improvement cycles. The shorter horizon makes feedback faster and helps teams avoid planning an entire route through uncertain territory. A longer horizon requires a more developed plan, while a short horizon can often begin with a clearly defined next step and rapid observation.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a target-condition horizon appropriate to process complexity and learning speed; break a long-range goal into nearer conditions."
Key point:
- Short horizons create more frequent learning cycles.
- Longer horizons need interim target conditions and more deliberate planning.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
15. Q: How should a team choose the first step toward a target condition?
A: Show answer
The team should identify a next step that is clear enough to begin learning, without delaying progress while it tries to prove which obstacle is the single largest. The chapter calls the delay caused by prolonged debate over the supposedly biggest problem Pareto paralysis. Data collection and Pareto analysis can be useful, but they become counterproductive if the team treats perfect prioritization as a prerequisite for action. A modest step may be direct observation, a short measurement exercise, a small trial, or a focused check of one suspected obstacle. Once the team acts, the process reveals more information and the next obstacle becomes easier to see. The first step matters chiefly because it starts the learning cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Write one immediate action that can be completed quickly and that will produce useful information about the gap to the target condition."
Key point:
- Do not wait for a perfect ranking of all obstacles before beginning.
- Choose a useful first step that starts learning.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
16. Q: Why might the next step be observation or data gathering rather than a countermeasure?
A: Show answer
A team often does not yet know why the process is failing to meet the target condition. In that situation, installing a fix would be premature. The next step may instead be to watch the work, time a sequence, trace a material movement, check machine availability, verify a work instruction, or run a small experiment that tests an assumption. The chapter notes that many improvement steps are efforts to see more clearly rather than immediate corrective actions. This is consistent with scientific problem-solving: define what is known, identify uncertainty, test one idea, and learn from the result. Observation is not inactivity; it is purposeful work when it reduces uncertainty relevant to the target condition.
Study instruction:
- "For one unresolved obstacle, state what fact is missing and design a direct observation or test to obtain it."
Key point:
- Do not prescribe a fix before understanding the obstacle.
- Information-gathering can be the most valuable next step.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
17. Q: What does process stability mean in the context of a target condition?
A: Show answer
Process stability means that the work can be performed with sufficiently predictable capability, availability, and flexibility to support the intended operating pattern. In the chapter, a common initial challenge is to reduce excessive fluctuation in workstation cycles and output cycles. A process may meet the required average output while individual cycles vary widely because of interruptions, material shortages, equipment losses, or uneven work content. Such variation can create extra staffing, quality problems, expediting, and difficulty sustaining later improvements. The Lean Enterprise Institute describes basic stability through the four Ms: manpower, machines, materials, and methods. Stability is therefore not merely a smooth-looking chart; it is a reliable foundation for flow, takt, pull, and leveling.
Study instruction:
- "Assess manpower, machines, materials, and methods for recurring causes of interruptions or inconsistent cycle performance."
Key point:
- Basic stability is a foundation for sustained improvement.
- Averages can conceal cycle-to-cycle instability.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
18. Q: How can a team measure the stability of a production line using a stopwatch?
A: Show answer
Select a repeatable reference point at the end of the line and record the elapsed time between successive pieces passing that point for approximately 20 to 40 consecutive cycles. Then move upstream and measure each operator's complete work cycle using a consistent reference point in that operator's sequence. Record each observation individually, including unusually long or short cycles, and graph the sequence for each position. The chapter emphasizes observing actual cycle-to-cycle behavior rather than relying only on shift totals. Measurement should be conducted safely and without interfering with work. The observer should note relevant conditions such as changeovers, material replenishment, machine stops, rework, or assistance from other operators so that variation can later be investigated.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a safe time study of 20 to 40 successive cycles, using consistent reference points and recording every cycle rather than only an average."
Key point:
- Measure successive cycles at defined reference points.
- Record context and variation so causes can be investigated.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
19. Q: Why does the chapter warn against using average cycle time to judge process stability?
A: Show answer
An average compresses a sequence of observations into one number and can hide the very variation that the team needs to understand. For example, cycles of 12, 16, 20, and 16 seconds average 16 seconds, but the process is not producing a consistent 16-second rhythm. If only the average is reported, interruptions and uneven work may remain invisible. A run chart or sequence of individual observations shows whether cycles are clustered, drifting, interrupted, or alternating between unusually fast and slow performance. Averages can still be useful for capacity or summary analysis when used appropriately, but they should not replace the direct view of successive cycles when the target condition requires stable repetition.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a set of individual cycle times with its average and explain what the average hides about process behavior."
Key point:
- A correct average does not prove a stable process.
- Use the distribution and sequence of cycles to identify variation.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
20. Q: How is takt time calculated, and what does it represent?
A: Show answer
Takt time is the effective available production time divided by customer demand for the same period. If a process has 26,100 seconds of effective operating time per shift and customers require 450 units in that shift, takt time is 26,100 divided by 450, or 58 seconds per unit. It represents the average pace at which a unit must be completed to match demand, not necessarily the cycle time that the process should be scheduled to achieve. Effective time excludes planned nonproduction periods such as breaks, meals, meetings, cleanup, and planned maintenance. The chapter's example does not subtract unplanned downtime or changeover time in this calculation; the process design must account for such losses separately.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate takt time from available operating time and demand for one consistent period, and state which planned breaks have been excluded."
Key point:
- Takt time translates customer demand into a production rhythm.
- Use matching time and demand periods and clearly define effective time.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Takt Time
21. Q: Why is takt time not automatically the same as the planned cycle time?
A: Show answer
Takt time is derived from customer demand, whereas planned cycle time is the operating pace at which the process is intended to run. A process may need to run faster than takt to compensate for planned changeovers or other known time losses that must be accommodated within the shift. The planned cycle is therefore a process-design decision informed by takt, not simply a copy of it. The chapter's example uses a planned cycle that is 15 percent faster than takt as a possible target condition, but this is illustrative rather than a universal rule. The appropriate gap depends on process characteristics, demand, planned losses, capacity, and the improvement challenge.
Study instruction:
- "For a process with a known takt, list the planned losses that affect its operating cycle and explain how the planned cycle is set."
Key point:
- Takt expresses customer pace; planned cycle expresses the intended process pace.
- The required gap must be justified by actual process conditions.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Takt Time
22. Q: How should a process distinguish planned losses from unplanned downtime when setting its cycle target?
A: Show answer
Planned losses, such as scheduled breaks, meetings, planned maintenance, and product changeovers, should be made explicit in the process design and time assumptions. Unplanned downtime is different because it is a problem to expose and reduce, not a loss that should automatically be built into a faster planned cycle. The chapter describes Toyota's practice of making up unplanned downtime through overtime when necessary rather than speeding up the planned cycle in advance, because that keeps equipment and process problems visible. This is a description of the practice discussed in the chapter, not a rule that every organization must adopt. Any implementation must consider labor agreements, safety, local law, fatigue, and the organization's operating policy.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each time loss as planned or unplanned and identify how it should be reflected in the takt calculation, process design, and problem log."
Key point:
- Do not normalize recurring unplanned downtime by hiding it in the target pace.
- Operating policies and labor constraints must be checked before applying overtime practices.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Takt Time
23. Q: How can the gap between planned cycle time and takt time become a useful improvement challenge?
A: Show answer
Once the process can operate consistently at its planned cycle, the team may define a further condition that moves planned cycle time closer to takt. A smaller gap can indicate that the process is using its available time more effectively while remaining synchronized with demand. Achieving that condition may require reducing changeover time, equipment downtime, scrap, absenteeism, or other losses. The chapter gives a 15-percent-faster-than-takt example to illustrate how a specific process condition can direct improvement; it does not prescribe that percentage for every factory. The team should choose a gap that is demanding but supported by the current condition and should not compromise safety, quality, or sustainable work.
Study instruction:
- "After confirming stable performance, identify one source of planned cycle-time allowance and define a measurable next condition for reducing it."
Key point:
- First stabilize the process; then consider narrowing the planned-cycle gap.
- The gap is a challenge to investigate, not a universal fixed percentage.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Takt Time
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
24. Q: How should a supervisor verify whether the stated obstacles to a slower target cycle are real?
A: Show answer
The supervisor's explanation is a useful hypothesis, but it may reflect expectations or assumptions rather than the obstacles that will actually occur. The chapter describes a practical test: if safe and operationally acceptable, establish a controlled temporary buffer and run the process at the proposed slower cycle. Observe what happens, record where work accumulates or stops, and identify the actual causes. This method makes the process reveal its constraints instead of relying only on opinions. The trial must be planned so that product quality, traceability, safety, and customer commitments are protected. The buffer is an experimental aid, not a permanent substitute for resolving the obstacle.
Study instruction:
- "Turn a supervisor's predicted obstacle into a controlled, safe test with a defined observation point and recorded result."
Key point:
- Treat opinions as hypotheses to test at the worksite.
- A controlled trial can expose actual constraints more reliably than discussion alone.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
25. Q: What is one-piece flow, or 1×1 flow?
A: Show answer
One-piece flow means that a unit moves directly from one process step to the next without waiting in an intervening batch or buffer inventory. The work is arranged so that the steps are connected and the unit progresses through the sequence at the intended pace. One-piece flow can reduce waiting and make interruptions visible, but it also increases the need for reliable processes and a clear response when an abnormality occurs. It is not achieved simply by moving machines closer together; work content, cycle balance, quality, material supply, staffing, and equipment reliability must support the flow. The chapter treats 1×1 flow both as a desired process condition and as a way to reveal what must improve.
Study instruction:
- "Map the movement of one unit through adjacent process steps and identify where it waits, accumulates, or is handled in batches."
Key point:
- A unit moves directly between steps without intermediate batch inventory.
- Flow requires process capability and a response to abnormalities.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
- Toyota: Toyota Production System
26. Q: Why can buffers between workstations hide problems that a one-piece-flow target would reveal?
A: Show answer
Buffers allow downstream work to continue when an upstream station is delayed, so the line may still achieve its output even though individual steps are unreliable. This can make the process appear flexible while concealing recurring equipment, material, quality, or work-balance problems. A one-piece-flow condition removes some of that protection and makes a stoppage or imbalance visible immediately. Visibility is valuable only when the organization has a disciplined way to respond, investigate causes, and restore the process. Removing buffers without that response capability can create disruption rather than improvement. The correct approach is to treat flow as a challenge and reduce buffers in a controlled manner as process capability improves.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one buffer that protects downstream operations and trace which upstream problems it may currently conceal."
Key point:
- Buffers can protect output while hiding process instability.
- Reduce buffers only with a clear abnormality-response and problem-solving routine.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
27. Q: Why does the correct number of operators depend on the intended process condition?
A: Show answer
Operator count should be evaluated against the work content, planned cycle time, work sequence, and required flexibility of the process. A line with extra operators and buffers may continue producing when one person is delayed, but it can also conceal imbalance and make workarounds normal. A tightly staffed one-piece-flow arrangement exposes a problem quickly because the steps depend on one another. The chapter's comparison is not a simplistic instruction to remove people. It is a challenge to design work so that the required number of operators can perform the intended sequence reliably, with an explicit method for responding to abnormalities. Staffing decisions must also respect safety, ergonomics, skill, fatigue, and applicable labor requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an operator balance chart and compare each person's work content with the planned cycle and the required abnormality response."
Key point:
- Staffing is part of the process design, not an isolated head-count target.
- A leaner configuration must remain safe and capable of responding to problems.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
28. Q: What does the loudspeaker brass-insert example teach about variable work content?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's example, cabinet panels of different sizes require different numbers of brass inserts. The insertion time therefore varies, causing downstream operators to move between neighboring value streams to stay busy. Although the workaround appears helpful, it creates additional variation and obscures the process problem. A target condition requiring a consistent cycle across cabinet sizes makes the real challenge explicit: develop a method that handles the varying insert work without shifting instability into other lines. The example shows why local utilization is not the same as whole-system improvement. The team must understand the source of variable work content and develop a repeatable method that preserves quality and a stable flow.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a product family with different work content and identify how product variation affects cycle time and downstream workload."
Key point:
- Local workarounds can transfer variation to neighboring processes.
- A target condition should expose the underlying variation to be solved.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
29. Q: What is heijunka, and what does a heijunka box do?
A: Show answer
Heijunka is production leveling: arranging the mix and quantity of production into a planned pattern over time rather than releasing uneven batches directly to the process. A heijunka box is a visual device that organizes production kanban by product type and time interval. Its rows or slots can represent products, while columns represent repeated withdrawal intervals. The pattern helps the process see what should be produced next and at what frequency. In the chapter, the deeper purpose is not merely to display a schedule. The pattern becomes a target condition: the team tries to operate according to it, observes where it cannot, and works on the obstacles that prevent consistent adherence.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a simple leveling pattern for two or more products and specify the time interval, sequence, and intended quantity."
Key point:
- Heijunka levels product mix and volume over time.
- The leveling pattern can serve as a process target condition.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
30. Q: What is the difference between leveling the production mix and leveling the production quantity?
A: Show answer
Leveling the mix means arranging different product types in a repeatable sequence so that the process does not make long, uneven runs of one item followed by another. Leveling the quantity means limiting how much of each item is produced in one pass through that sequence, based on the intended lot size and average demand for the period. Together, the two dimensions smooth both what is made and how much is released at each interval. A pattern may be designed to reduce changeover losses or distribute demand more evenly. The specific sequence and lot size should reflect customer demand, process capability, and the desired replenishment rhythm; they should not be copied mechanically from another factory.
Study instruction:
- "Create a sample sequence and calculate the maximum quantity for each product per pass using the chosen planning period and demand data."
Key point:
- Mix leveling controls product order; quantity leveling controls the amount per pass.
- Both are needed to create a repeatable production pattern.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
31. Q: Why might an assembly process deliberately defer an extra kanban request to the next pass through the leveling sequence?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives an example in which eight containers of an item are requested even though the leveled pattern calls for a maximum of seven in the current pass. Producing the eighth immediately may transmit a short-term demand spike to upstream processes, where it can be amplified into additional inventory, schedule changes, and expediting. If customer demand over time is expected to average out, the extra quantity may be absorbed in a later pass. This decision depends on the actual customer-service requirement and the ability to hold finished goods or otherwise respond to demand variation. A leveling rule must not be applied blindly when a genuine urgent order or service commitment requires a different response.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a demand spike and determine whether it can be absorbed in a later cycle without breaching customer lead-time or service commitments."
Key point:
- Leveling protects the wider value stream from unnecessary short-term demand spikes.
- Customer commitments and available finished-goods protection still govern exceptions.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
32. Q: How can production leveling reduce the bullwhip effect?
A: Show answer
The bullwhip effect occurs when demand variation becomes amplified as information and replenishment decisions move upstream through a supply chain. If an assembly process reacts to every short-term order spike by changing its schedule or increasing batch production, upstream processes may interpret the temporary increase as a sustained demand signal. They then build more inventory or change capacity and schedules, creating further instability. Leveling smooths the mix and quantity released by the downstream process, reducing the size of the variations transmitted upstream. It can therefore reduce the inventory and lead-time burden needed to protect each process from downstream fluctuations. Leveling does not eliminate genuine demand uncertainty; it creates a more stable replenishment pattern for the system to manage.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a one-day order spike through at least three upstream processes and identify how a leveled release would change the signal."
Key point:
- Uneven downstream schedules can amplify variation upstream.
- A stable release pattern can reduce the need for protective inventory and expediting.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
33. Q: How is sequence attainment different from schedule attainment?
A: Show answer
Schedule attainment asks whether the required shipments or output were achieved by the deadline, regardless of how the process was operated. Sequence attainment asks whether the process actually followed the intended leveling sequence. A factory can ship everything on time and still have poor sequence attainment if it repeatedly changes the planned order, skips items, expedites parts, or runs around shortages. The chapter's example shows how a team initially reported a high percentage by measuring shipments rather than adherence to the pattern. The two measures answer different questions: one concerns delivery outcome, the other concerns process behavior. Both may be useful, but substituting one for the other hides the obstacles to a stable production pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Write operational definitions for schedule attainment and sequence attainment, then test them against a day when shipments were on time but the sequence changed."
Key point:
- On-time output does not prove that the intended process pattern was followed.
- Use separate measures for delivery results and process adherence.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
34. Q: What should a team do when it cannot follow the heijunka sequence on a particular day?
A: Show answer
The team should first identify the specific obstacle that prevents the planned sequence from being run. If the process cannot follow the pattern, it may temporarily deviate to protect a genuine customer or operational requirement, but it should record the deviation and pursue the cause rather than treating the workaround as the new normal. The chapter's routine is to load the leveling device according to the intended pattern, ask whether the process can run that way today, and, if not, investigate one problem while temporarily adjusting the sequence. The team should return to the intended pattern as soon as it can do so responsibly. Repeated deviations become a prioritized learning agenda.
Study instruction:
- "For one recent sequence miss, document the trigger, immediate response, root-cause hypothesis, owner, and next test."
Key point:
- A deviation should trigger learning, not silent abandonment of the pattern.
- Return to the target sequence while addressing the obstacle.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
35. Q: Why should a heijunka pattern not be treated as a rigid straitjacket?
A: Show answer
The pattern is a desired operating condition, not an instruction to ignore safety, quality, equipment failures, material shortages, or urgent customer needs. Its purpose is to expose the reasons the process cannot run in a stable, leveled manner. If the pattern is nearly always achievable, the team may set a more challenging condition, such as smaller lots or a tighter sequence, so that improvement continues. If it is repeatedly missed, the right response is to understand the obstacles rather than conceal deviations or punish people for reporting them. A pattern used this way is a learning instrument. A pattern displayed only for audits or tours, but not used to guide actual production and problem-solving, has lost its improvement purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Review a leveling board against actual production records and identify whether it is being used to guide work or merely displayed as evidence of a tool."
Key point:
- A leveling pattern is a challenge for improvement, not an inflexible rule detached from reality.
- Visible deviations are useful only when they lead to problem-solving.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
36. Q: What is the difference between a push system and a pull system?
A: Show answer
In a push system, each process receives a schedule based largely on forecasts or planned future requirements and produces what it believes the next process will need. In a pull system, the downstream process signals its actual consumption or requirement to the upstream process, which then replenishes what was withdrawn. The signal may be a kanban card, electronic message, empty container, or another controlled device. Pull links the processes through a defined customer-supplier relationship and is intended to reduce overproduction. Neither method guarantees improvement by itself. A pull system requires sufficiently capable processes, clear replenishment rules, controlled inventory, and reliable signals. If those conditions are absent, the system may become a poorly controlled stock arrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Map a material route and identify where production is triggered by a forecast schedule versus actual downstream withdrawal."
Key point:
- Push is driven by planned release; pull is triggered by downstream need.
- A pull signal must be linked to clear rules and reliable process capability.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Pull Production
- Toyota: Toyota Production System
37. Q: What information should a kanban system specify for each item?
A: Show answer
A kanban needs enough information to authorize and control the movement or production of a defined quantity. Depending on the design, this includes the part name and number, the supplying process or external supplier, pack-out quantity, the storage address, and the consuming process address. The chapter emphasizes two addresses: where the item belongs in the supermarket and which machine or process is authorized to make it. Those addresses are not merely clerical fields. They define a more systematic relationship between the supplying and consuming processes. If locations, quantities, or authorized sources are ambiguous, the signal can produce wrong parts, duplicate replenishment, missing stock, or untraceable workarounds.
Study instruction:
- "Review one kanban card or electronic signal and verify that its item identity, quantity, source, storage location, and consuming point are unambiguous."
Key point:
- A kanban is an authorization and information signal, not just an inventory label.
- Accurate item and location data are essential to controlled replenishment.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Pull Production
38. Q: Why does assigning a specific machine to a kanban item expose process problems?
A: Show answer
When a supplying department can run every part on any available machine, it can work around equipment problems by moving production between machines. That flexibility may preserve output in the short term, but it increases the number of variables and makes it harder to understand why a particular process fails. A kanban design that assigns each part to a defined machine or route removes some of that freedom and reveals whether the designated machine is reliable and capable. The supervisor may object that the route is too restrictive, but that objection identifies a problem to investigate. Machine assignment is therefore part of the target condition and a diagnostic device, not simply a scheduling restriction.
Study instruction:
- "Compare actual machine routings with the intended kanban routing and record every bypass, including the reason and effect."
Key point:
- Uncontrolled routing can conceal machine and process weaknesses.
- A defined route makes reliability and capability gaps visible.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
39. Q: Why does a pull system often reveal obstacles soon after it is introduced?
A: Show answer
A pull system limits the ability to produce or move material independently of downstream consumption. That exposes shortages, unreliable equipment, long changeovers, poor quality, inaccurate inventory, and weak replenishment routines that a push schedule or excess stock may have hidden. The chapter describes early pull-system failures as expected learning signals, not proof that the concept is inherently unsuitable. The system does not create the underlying machine or material problem; it makes the problem more visible because the previous workaround is no longer available. Teams must investigate the failure, improve the relevant process, and then test the pull arrangement again. This requires a manageable scope and a reliable response routine.
Study instruction:
- "When a pull loop fails, distinguish the signal failure, inventory-record issue, supply-capability issue, and actual customer-demand change before changing the design."
Key point:
- Pull exposes pre-existing weaknesses that schedules and buffers can hide.
- Failure is useful only when it is investigated and followed by another test.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Pull Production
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
40. Q: Why is it risky to introduce kanban across an entire factory at once?
A: Show answer
A factory-wide launch can expose more shortages, routing conflicts, machine failures, data errors, and process-instability issues than the organization can investigate at one time. If many processes are unstable, the inventory needed to protect every new pull connection may be excessive, and the new system may quickly revert to expediting or schedule-based workarounds. The chapter recommends introducing pull on a small scale, learning about the affected processes, and expanding as capability improves. A pilot should have clear boundaries, accountable owners, accurate item data, defined replenishment rules, and a process for responding to shortages. Expansion should follow demonstrated learning rather than a calendar-driven rollout alone.
Study instruction:
- "Select one contained process pair for a pull pilot and define readiness checks, failure signals, owners, and criteria for expansion."
Key point:
- Start with a manageable loop rather than a simultaneous factory-wide conversion.
- Expand when the processes and management routines can sustain the next scope.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Pull Production
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
41. Q: Does introducing kanban automatically reduce inventory?
A: Show answer
No. A kanban system organizes and controls replenishment of inventory; it does not automatically eliminate the inventory or correct the processes that require it. The amount held is determined by demand, replenishment lead time, lot size, reliability, and the chosen protection against variation. A poorly designed pull loop can simply become a more orderly store of stock. In the chapter's improvement logic, kanban is used to make the process relationship visible and to create successive conditions under which the supermarket can be reduced. Inventory falls sustainably when the causes that require it are understood and improved, not merely because cards or bins have been introduced.
Study instruction:
- "Compare inventory before and after a kanban introduction and determine whether the reduction came from process improvement or from an arbitrary stock-limit change."
Key point:
- Kanban controls replenishment; it does not itself remove the need for inventory.
- Sustainable inventory reduction follows improvements in the processes that drive stock.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Supermarket
42. Q: Why might Toyota remove a kanban card after a pull loop has operated reliably?
A: Show answer
Removing a card reduces the quantity authorized to circulate in the loop and therefore lowers the inventory protection available between processes. If the loop has been stable for a period, reducing one card creates a more demanding target condition. The resulting shortage or interruption, if one occurs, can reveal a remaining obstacle that was previously buffered by the extra stock. The team then works on that obstacle and tries again. This is a controlled learning method, not a recommendation to cut stock indiscriminately. The reduction must be monitored so that customer service, quality, safety, and material traceability remain protected. The long-term intent is to reduce dependence on the kanban loop as processes move closer to continuous flow.
Study instruction:
- "For a stable replenishment loop, identify the evidence needed before reducing one card and define how the trial will be monitored and reversed if necessary."
Key point:
- A card reduction is a deliberate next challenge after stability is demonstrated.
- Inventory protection should be reduced through controlled experiments, not arbitrary cuts.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Supermarket
43. Q: What does the statement 'the purpose of kanban is to eliminate kanban' mean?
A: Show answer
The statement expresses a direction of continuous improvement. Kanban is useful where processes cannot yet be connected directly in one-piece flow, so a controlled supermarket and replenishment signal regulate the gap. As reliability, changeover capability, quality, and process balance improve, the amount of stock and the need for separate replenishment signals can be reduced. Kanban is therefore a temporary means of managing and learning about disconnected processes, not necessarily the final state. The chapter also notes that kanban remains useful as a tool for making progress: it defines successive, increasingly challenging conditions on the route toward flow. The objective is not to remove cards prematurely but to reduce the underlying need for them.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which process obstacles currently require a supermarket and describe what would have to improve before the loop could shrink or disappear."
Key point:
- Kanban is a means toward better-connected processes, not an end in itself.
- Reduce the need for kanban by improving the processes it links.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kanban
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
44. Q: How should lean tools such as takt time, 1×1 flow, heijunka, and kanban be used within the improvement kata?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that these tools should support a repeatable improvement routine rather than be treated as stand-alone solutions to deploy. A tool can define a target pattern, make a gap visible, and focus the team on the next obstacle. The improvement kata supplies the behavior for moving toward that condition: understand the challenge, grasp the current condition, set a next target condition, and experiment to overcome obstacles. Installing a kanban board or leveling box without that learning routine may only rearrange inventory or display a schedule. The same principle applies to other techniques: their value depends on how they help people observe, learn, and improve the actual process.
Study instruction:
- "For each lean tool used in your area, state the target condition it is meant to support and the obstacle-learning routine that follows when it does not work."
Key point:
- Tools are subordinate to the improvement routine.
- A tool installation is not evidence that process capability has improved.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
- Toyota: Toyota Production System
45. Q: What is the difference between a written standard and standardized work?
A: Show answer
A written standard describes how a process is intended to operate: the prescribed sequence, method, timing, and relevant conditions. Standardized work, as discussed in the chapter, is the actual condition in which the process is operating in accordance with the standard. Displaying or filing a standard does not prove that the work is being performed that way. A supervisor or team leader must observe the process and compare actual work with the defined pattern. Differences reveal a gap that may require clarification, training, problem-solving, or improvement of the standard itself. Standards should be treated as a reference for understanding and improving work, not simply as documents to demonstrate compliance.
Study instruction:
- "Select one work standard, observe the corresponding process, and record the differences between the documented sequence and actual practice."
Key point:
- A standard describes intended work; standardized work describes actual adherence to it.
- The document alone does not demonstrate that the process is stable or controlled.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
46. Q: Who should use work standards, and why does the chapter emphasize the team leader's role?
A: Show answer
The chapter's example explains that work standards are primarily a reference for the team leader, who needs to compare expected work with actual work and respond to gaps. The standard is not simply a document posted over an operator as a disciplinary instruction. A team leader uses it to understand whether the process is operating as intended, identify abnormalities, support the operator, and guide improvement. Operators also need clear, usable instructions and should contribute to improving the work, but management must not confuse posting a document with managing the process. The orientation and practical use of the standard should make it accessible to the people responsible for observing and improving the work.
Study instruction:
- "Review who uses each standard in your area, what decisions it supports, and whether it is positioned to help the team leader observe the process."
Key point:
- Standards are reference points for plan-versus-actual comparison.
- Leadership responsibility includes observing gaps and helping improve the process.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
47. Q: How should an abnormality be interpreted when actual work differs from the standard?
A: Show answer
An abnormality shows that the process has not yet reached or maintained the intended condition. It should trigger a response that protects quality and safety, makes the deviation visible, and helps the team understand why it occurred. Treating every deviation solely as a discipline failure can discourage reporting and leave the process causes unresolved. At the same time, an abnormality should not be ignored or accepted as normal. The team should determine whether the standard is appropriate, whether the operator has the necessary materials and training, whether equipment is capable, and whether the method can be followed under actual conditions. The chapter reframes abnormalities as evidence of a gap to work on through improvement kata.
Study instruction:
- "For a recent deviation, identify the immediate containment action, the actual cause to investigate, and whether the standard itself needs review."
Key point:
- A deviation is a signal that the intended condition is not being achieved.
- Respond to protect the process and learn, rather than hiding the abnormality.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
- Toyota: Toyota Production System
48. Q: How is a target condition established from the current condition and the organization's direction?
A: Show answer
Begin by clarifying the relevant vision, challenge, departmental need, or customer requirement. Then observe and analyze the actual process so that its capability, variation, constraints, and workarounds are understood. Use that evidence to define a near-term operating condition that moves the process toward the direction. Early conditions may focus on stability and may arise directly from process analysis before they are tightly linked to departmental metrics. As the process improves, target conditions should align more explicitly with departmental and organizational needs. The process should not be left without a next challenge after a departmental target is achieved; without continued striving, it can slip back into old patterns.
Study instruction:
- "Create a short chain showing the organizational direction, current process evidence, and the next target condition that connects them."
Key point:
- A target condition is built from both direction and direct knowledge of the current state.
- Continue setting new conditions after earlier milestones are reached.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
49. Q: What is a sensible progression of target conditions for many manufacturing processes?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a common, but not universal, progression. First, establish a stable process with a repeatable one-piece flow where appropriate, the planned cycle, and a suitable number of operators. Next, work toward a leveled product mix and smaller lots. Then connect processes through kanban where continuous flow is not yet feasible. Further improvement may align the process more closely with departmental targets, narrow the gap between planned cycle time and takt, reduce batch size toward one piece, and move toward the longer-term vision. The sequence depends on the actual condition and constraints. A process that cannot reliably produce good parts or operate when needed should not jump straight to a sophisticated pull arrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare your process with this progression and justify which stage is appropriate based on observed capability, availability, flexibility, and demand."
Key point:
- Build stability before expecting flow, leveling, and pull to work reliably.
- Treat the progression as a guide, not a mandatory universal rollout sequence.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
50. Q: Why can a numerical inventory-reduction target create operational problems?
A: Show answer
Inventory is an outcome of process performance and customer-demand conditions. If management orders stock to be reduced without improving the processes that require it, the system may lose the protection needed to absorb long changeovers, unreliable equipment, quality problems, or uneven demand. The chapter describes a factory that reduced finished-goods inventory to one day but then incurred expensive expedited shipping because the assembly processes could not support that low level. In another example, a Toyota executive called for more inventory because the process was not yet capable of operating with less. The lesson is not that high inventory is desirable; it is that inventory should be changed in step with process capability and customer requirements.
Study instruction:
- "For an inventory target, identify the process losses and demand variability currently covered by stock before deciding how much can safely be removed."
Key point:
- Inventory levels cannot be set independently of process capability and demand.
- Sometimes temporary protection is necessary while the underlying process is improved.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Supermarket
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
51. Q: When can reducing inventory too quickly or creating a long FIFO route make the process less stable?
A: Show answer
A large reduction in stock can remove the time and protection needed for upstream and downstream processes to recover from variation. Similarly, a long FIFO route may allow conditions to change while parts are moving through it, so the sequence that was valid at the start may no longer fit the situation at the end. The chapter warns that an ambitious flow design with a lead time longer than the system can reliably manage may create disorder rather than better flow. FIFO lanes are useful only when their capacity, sequence, and lead time are understood. The team should establish shorter, manageable conditions, verify process stability, and increase flow step by step instead of jumping directly to an idealized future state.
Study instruction:
- "Map the proposed FIFO route, calculate its lead time and capacity, and identify how much process or demand change can occur while items are in the lane."
Key point:
- More flow or less stock is not automatically better if the system cannot sustain it.
- Break ambitious changes into smaller conditions supported by process evidence.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: First In, First Out (FIFO)
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
52. Q: What does the file-cabinet redesign example show about using a target condition to resolve engineering disagreement?
A: Show answer
The engineering team debated several alternatives, including adding a second bending-and-welding line and making both assembly lines capable of producing all cabinet sizes. Each proposal attracted valid objections about capital cost, ergonomics, and different work content. Discussion moved in circles because the group was comparing solutions without agreeing on the required future process arrangement. Once a target condition was drawn, the question changed from which proposal was best to how the desired arrangement could be achieved within budget and time. Engineers then developed creative ideas, such as reusing idle equipment and simplifying transfers. The condition was not fully achieved, but the shared challenge produced progress and learning that the unstructured debate had not.
Study instruction:
- "For a design decision with competing proposals, define the required process state first and then assign teams to develop feasible countermeasures within explicit constraints."
Key point:
- A target condition gives engineering debate a common design objective.
- Partial achievement can still produce meaningful learning and progress.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
53. Q: How can target-condition thinking apply outside manufacturing?
A: Show answer
The improvement kata can be applied wherever work is performed through a process and the future route is uncertain. A service team might define a target condition for how a customer request is received, assessed, assigned, and resolved. A product-development team might define a near-term condition for validating a customer need or completing a prototype test. A finance or administrative process might define a stable sequence, clear handoffs, and a predictable turnaround time. The details differ from factory measures, but the logic remains: clarify the challenge, observe the current process, describe the next desired state, and experiment to overcome obstacles. Measures should fit the work and should not be forced into manufacturing-style cycle times when those are not meaningful.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a nonmanufacturing process and define a near-term target condition using observable handoffs, decision rules, quality criteria, and response time."
Key point:
- Target-condition thinking applies to processes beyond the factory floor.
- Adapt the measures to the nature of the work rather than copying factory metrics.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
54. Q: How should a team respond if it does not fully achieve its target condition by the due date?
A: Show answer
The team should assess what was achieved, what remains different from the intended condition, what was learned about the obstacles, and whether the original condition was based on sound evidence. The chapter cautions against casually changing the target or date whenever the work becomes difficult, because that can replace problem-solving with goal adjustment. However, new facts may reveal a genuine change in customer need, safety requirement, or operating context that requires a formal reconsideration. The team should distinguish an avoidable failure to pursue the condition from a justified change in direction. In either case, the review should produce a clearer current condition and a sensible next target or experiment.
Study instruction:
- "At a missed target date, prepare a review of actual results, unresolved obstacles, assumptions disproved, and any evidence-based reason to revise the direction."
Key point:
- Do not move the goal merely to avoid confronting obstacles.
- Use the review to improve understanding and define the next responsible step.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
55. Q: How can managers keep improvement focused on the current target condition?
A: Show answer
Managers should ask whether a proposed action is expected to move the process toward its current target condition. If it is not connected, it may be a worthwhile idea but should not automatically consume the team's limited attention and resources now. This discipline prevents improvement activity from becoming a collection of unrelated projects, tool installations, or personal preferences. It also helps the team recognize when a proposed countermeasure addresses a real obstacle and when it is simply familiar or fashionable. The manager's role is to maintain focus, support observation and experimentation, and ensure that actions remain within safety, quality, and operational constraints. Focus is not rigidity: new evidence can justify revisiting priorities through a deliberate process.
Study instruction:
- "Review the team's improvement-action list and classify each action as directly linked, indirectly linked, or unrelated to the current target condition."
Key point:
- Prioritize work that advances the current process challenge.
- A useful idea can be deferred when it does not address the present target condition.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
56. Q: How can a team distinguish a genuine process improvement from a transfer of waste to another area?
A: Show answer
A change is not a true improvement merely because one workstation becomes faster or appears cleaner. The team should examine effects on the entire process and value stream, including downstream workload, quality, lead time, inventory, ergonomics, and the work of other operators. The chapter's examples show how operators moving between neighboring lines can keep people busy while spreading variation, and how dropping parts into a container may simply transfer work to the paint line. A target condition should describe the system behavior required, and the team should observe whether the proposed change improves that behavior without creating a new bottleneck or hidden cost elsewhere.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed local improvement, map the upstream and downstream effects and verify whether the change removes waste or merely relocates it."
Key point:
- Evaluate improvement across the process and value stream, not only at one workstation.
- A local gain can create downstream instability or additional work.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Value Stream Mapping and Continuous Flow
57. Q: What role does the manager or team leader play in developing a target condition?
A: Show answer
The manager or team leader helps connect the process challenge to the organization's direction, ensures that the current condition is understood, and supports the team in defining a specific next condition. The leader should ask questions that direct attention to facts, obstacles, and learning rather than supplying every solution. Once the condition is defined, the leader helps maintain focus, makes abnormalities visible, protects time for observation and experiments, and ensures that safety and quality are not sacrificed to meet a target. The chapter's discussion of work standards also places the team leader in a key role: comparing actual work with the intended pattern and helping the team understand the gap.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a leader's improvement discussion and assess whether the questions promote evidence-based learning or simply direct people to implement a preferred solution."
Key point:
- Leaders create the conditions for focused learning and problem-solving.
- Coaching should develop the team's capability rather than replace its thinking.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
58. Q: How should sequence attainment, cycle variation, and outcome measures be used together?
A: Show answer
These measures provide different views of performance and should not be collapsed into a single score. Cycle variation shows whether the process repeats predictably at the workstation or line level. Sequence attainment shows whether production followed the intended leveled pattern. Outcome measures such as output, productivity, quality, cost, and on-time delivery show the results produced by the system. A process can meet its output target while remaining unstable or repeatedly breaking the leveling sequence. Conversely, sequence adherence may improve before all customer or cost outcomes are visible. Reviewing the measures together helps managers distinguish process improvement from short-term performance achieved through overtime, expediting, extra staffing, or other workarounds.
Study instruction:
- "Build a review sheet with one process-stability measure, one sequence-adherence measure, and two outcome measures; define what each can and cannot tell you."
Key point:
- Process and outcome measures answer different questions.
- Use a balanced set of indicators to avoid mistaking workarounds for improvement.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
59. Q: What would you investigate if a line meets its shift target but its cycle times fluctuate widely?
A: Show answer
First verify the measurement method and observe the actual work sequence at the line. Record successive cycles and note interruptions such as material replenishment, machine stops, changeovers, rework, operator assistance, and walking. Compare the work content and timing of each station with the intended cycle. Check whether buffers or extra staffing are allowing the line to meet output while individual stations remain unstable. Then select one concrete obstacle for observation or a controlled experiment. Do not immediately increase the pace or remove operators, because the underlying cause may be an unreliable machine, poor material presentation, unbalanced work, or an unsafe method. The target condition should define the required stable pattern and guide the investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Use a cycle-by-cycle observation sheet to link each unusually long or short cycle to the event that occurred during it."
Key point:
- Shift output can conceal unstable individual cycles.
- Find the causes of variation before changing staffing or pace.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
60. Q: What would you do if a proposed target condition requires more inventory than the current management target allows?
A: Show answer
Clarify why the proposed condition requires the inventory and what process risk the stock is intended to protect against. Compare the requirement with actual demand variability, replenishment lead time, machine reliability, changeover performance, and customer-service obligations. The chapter's Detroit example shows that a rigid low-inventory target can increase expedited freight when the process cannot yet support it. A temporary higher buffer may be justified while the team works toward a more capable process. The decision should be transparent, time-bound where appropriate, and linked to a plan for investigating the causes of the inventory requirement. Management should not treat a numerical stock limit as more important than reliable delivery and sustainable process improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an evidence-based explanation of the inventory needed today, the process losses it protects against, and the improvement condition that could reduce it later."
Key point:
- Inventory is a process outcome and may be necessary temporarily.
- Any temporary buffer should support learning and a plan to improve the causes behind it.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Supermarket
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
61. Q: How should a team handle an urgent customer order that conflicts with the leveling pattern?
A: Show answer
The team should determine whether the order is a genuine exception with a customer commitment that cannot be met through the normal pattern. If a deviation is necessary, it should be authorized under the organization's escalation rules, recorded, and communicated to affected upstream and downstream processes. The team should then examine why the exception occurred and whether demand volatility, inadequate finished-goods protection, long replenishment lead time, or an inaccurate leveling assumption is responsible. The chapter notes that leveling may rely on finished-goods inventory to absorb order spikes and that exceptionally large spikes may require investigating the customer-demand pattern itself. The goal is to protect the customer without allowing emergency scheduling to become routine.
Study instruction:
- "Create an exception path for urgent demand that defines approval, customer impact, schedule changes, communication, and post-event review."
Key point:
- Customer-critical exceptions may justify a controlled deviation.
- Recurring emergencies indicate a demand or process condition that needs improvement.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Heijunka Box
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Pull Production
62. Q: Why does the chapter say that a target condition is a challenge rather than a prediction?
A: Show answer
A prediction claims that a particular future result or route is expected to occur. A target condition states the desired future process state even though the team does not yet know exactly how to achieve it. That uncertainty is deliberate: it creates the need to observe, test, and learn. If the team already knows every step required, it may be implementing a known solution rather than improving the process. The target condition should still be grounded in the current condition and constrained by a realistic time horizon, budget, quality, and safety requirements. It is a challenge to develop capability, not a forecast that success is guaranteed.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed improvement, separate what the team wants to be true from what it currently predicts will happen and from the actions it plans to test."
Key point:
- A target condition defines a desired state under uncertainty.
- Do not disguise a prediction or implementation plan as a challenge.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
63. Q: How can a target condition create a learning environment rather than a compliance exercise?
A: Show answer
A learning environment treats the target as a reference for experimentation and reflection. People are expected to report gaps, test ideas, and explain what happened rather than conceal problems to make the dashboard look successful. The target must be specific enough to observe, while the route remains open to discovery. Leaders review evidence from the work, support small experiments, and use deviations to identify the next obstacle. A compliance-only approach, by contrast, may reward the appearance of adherence, encourage workarounds, and turn standards or boards into display items. The chapter's examples of staged heijunka boards and posted standards illustrate why visible tools are insufficient unless they are actively used to understand and improve the process.
Study instruction:
- "Review a target-condition board or standard and determine whether it is used in daily observation, problem-solving, and learning or mainly for reporting and display."
Key point:
- A learning system makes gaps visible and safe to discuss.
- Tools and documents have little value when separated from actual work and experimentation.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Standardized Work
64. Q: What are common mistakes when defining or using target conditions?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include confusing an outcome target with a process condition, embedding a preferred solution in the target, specifying detail that is not yet understood, setting a challenge that is either trivial or impossible, choosing a horizon that is too long, and delaying the first step while searching for the largest obstacle. Other errors include treating takt, flow, heijunka, or kanban as stand-alone implementations; measuring schedule attainment instead of sequence attainment; reducing inventory before improving the processes that require it; and displaying standards without checking actual work. These errors share a common pattern: they replace direct observation and learning with assumptions, tools, or numerical outcomes. A disciplined review should test whether the condition is observable, relevant, challenging, and connected to a practical learning cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Audit one existing improvement project against these mistakes and identify the single most important correction to its target-condition practice."
Key point:
- Most failures come from confusing tools, outcomes, or solutions with the desired process condition.
- Direct observation and disciplined learning correct these errors.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Basic Stability
65. Q: How can an organization know whether target-condition thinking is becoming part of everyday management?
A: Show answer
Look for observable management behaviors rather than the number of improvement boards or lean tools installed. Teams should be able to explain their current challenge, show evidence about the current condition, describe the next target condition, and identify the obstacle they are investigating now. Leaders should regularly observe work, ask focused questions, review experiments, and respond constructively when the target is not met. Measures should show whether process stability, sequence adherence, quality, delivery, or other relevant outcomes are improving over time. It is also important to see whether people continue setting new conditions after reaching earlier ones. A true management habit is demonstrated by repeated learning cycles embedded in normal work, not by a one-time transformation event.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a short gemba review and ask a process owner to explain the challenge, current condition, target condition, present obstacle, and next experiment using evidence."
Key point:
- Look for repeated scientific problem-solving behavior, not tool counts.
- The management routine should be visible in daily work and follow-up.
Source links:
- Rother, Toyota Kata, Chapter 5
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
- Toyota: Toyota Production System
Rows: 65
Table 6
Problem Solving and Adaptation Through Target Conditions
1. Q: How does problem solving and adapting move an organization toward a target condition?
A: Show answer
