Moving toward a target condition requires more than defining a desired result and issuing a plan. The organization must work through the obstacles that become visible as the process changes. The route is uncertain because each action interacts with people, equipment, information, suppliers, customers, and other parts of the system. The improvement kata therefore combines a clear challenge with small experiments, direct observation, learning, and adjustment. Teams compare what they expected with what actually happened, investigate abnormalities, and use that knowledge to choose the next step. The objective is not perfect prediction; it is disciplined progress through uncertainty while developing the capability to solve new problems.
Study instruction:
- Explain the relationship between a target condition, obstacles, experiments, learning, and the next step. Use one work process to illustrate the complete sequence.
Key point:
- A target condition gives direction; iterative learning reveals the route.
- Problem solving is a continuing process of adaptation, not a one-time solution.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
2. Q: What is the difference between a target condition, an outcome goal, and an implementation plan?
A: Show answer
A target condition describes the desired way a process should operate at a defined point in the future, including relevant process characteristics and measures. An outcome goal states a result, such as completing a quantity of work by a deadline, but may not explain how the process should function. An implementation plan lists actions that someone intends to perform. These are related but not interchangeable. A target condition provides a description against which the current process can be compared; the plan is a provisional prediction about how to move toward it. If a plan is treated as fixed, new information may be ignored. A useful target condition makes the process observable enough to support frequent learning.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an outcome-only goal as a process-based target condition by specifying steps, sequence, timing, process characteristics, process measures, and the intended outcome.
Key point:
- A plan is a hypothesis, not a guarantee.
- A target condition describes the desired process as well as the intended result.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
3. Q: Why should teams assume that the path to a target condition is unclear?
A: Show answer
Even a carefully designed plan cannot anticipate every interaction and response in a changing work system. Demand, material availability, equipment behavior, human actions, quality conditions, and dependencies may differ from assumptions. The chapter describes this uncertain route as a gray zone: the destination is understood, but the exact path is not. Assuming that the route is unclear prepares a team to notice deviations instead of treating them as surprises or evidence of poor discipline. It also reduces overconfidence in plans and encourages short feedback loops. The team should still plan, but it should expect to revise its understanding as direct evidence emerges.
Study instruction:
- Identify five variables that could change between planning and execution in a process you know. Explain how each could affect the route to the target condition.
Key point:
- Uncertainty is a normal feature of improvement work.
- Planning should prepare a team to learn and adapt, not create an illusion of certainty.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
4. Q: How does interconnectedness make process improvement difficult to predict?
A: Show answer
A process is part of a wider system. A change to one step can alter queues, workloads, material movements, quality, information needs, or the timing of downstream work. These effects may be delayed or may appear in a different part of the value stream from where the change was introduced. For example, speeding up one workstation can increase work-in-process inventory or overload the next process. A local improvement may therefore worsen overall performance if its effects are not observed across the system. Teams should define the boundary of the process, identify important upstream and downstream relationships, and check both the intended effect and possible side effects after each experiment.
Study instruction:
- Map the main upstream and downstream dependencies of a process and predict at least two unintended effects a local change could create.
Key point:
- A process change can produce system-wide effects.
- Evaluate improvement against the whole flow and customer need, not only one local metric.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
5. Q: Why is a plan better understood as a prediction than as a fixed sequence of instructions?
A: Show answer
A plan expresses what the team currently expects to happen if it takes a particular action under assumed conditions. Because those assumptions may be incomplete, the predicted result may not occur. Treating the plan as a prediction makes it possible to compare expectation with reality and learn from the difference. Treating it as a fixed instruction encourages people to continue even when evidence shows that the conditions have changed. A plan remains useful because it clarifies the proposed step, expected result, timing, and responsibility. Its value depends on testing it and updating it when the observed process contradicts the assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Take a recent improvement plan and label its assumptions, predicted effects, evidence to collect, and conditions that would cause the team to revise it.
Key point:
- A plan should be specific enough to test and flexible enough to change.
- A deviation between prediction and observation is information, not automatically a failure of discipline.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
6. Q: What does the staircase analogy explain about moving toward a target condition?
A: Show answer
The staircase represents progress through a sequence of small steps rather than one large leap from the current condition to the target. Each step is an experiment: the team predicts what will happen, tries the action, observes the result, and uses what it learned to choose the next step. The next step may differ from the original plan because the previous experiment may reveal an obstacle that was not visible beforehand. The staircase is therefore not a predetermined project schedule. It is a practical way to move forward while continually adjusting to the current condition. The team keeps the destination in view but decides the next move from the evidence available now.
Study instruction:
- Draw a staircase from a current condition to a target condition. Label each proposed step as a testable experiment rather than an irreversible project milestone.
Key point:
- Progress is built from learning steps.
- The next step should be informed by the latest understanding of the process.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
7. Q: How does the flashlight analogy help explain experimentation in uncertain conditions?
A: Show answer
The flashlight analogy describes a team that knows where it wants to go but cannot see the entire route. The beam illuminates only the area immediately ahead. Taking a step forward reveals more of the path and may expose an obstacle that could not be identified from the starting point. The team cannot obtain reliable knowledge of the whole route simply by discussing it from a distance. It must act, observe, and update its understanding. The analogy does not justify acting recklessly; it supports small, controlled steps that extend visibility while limiting the consequences of being wrong.
Study instruction:
- Apply the analogy to a process change. State what is currently visible, what remains uncertain, what small step would reveal more, and how the risk of that step will be contained.
Key point:
- Direct action and observation extend what the team can know.
- Use controlled steps to discover the next part of an uncertain route.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
8. Q: What are the four stages of the PDCA cycle, and what should happen in each stage?
A: Show answer
Plan defines the proposed action, the prediction about what it will do, and the expected result. Do tests the hypothesis, preferably on a manageable scale, while the team observes the process and records relevant conditions. Check compares the actual result with the prediction and examines what was learned, including unexpected outcomes. Act determines what follows: retain and stabilize a change that works, adjust it, or begin another cycle when the hypothesis is not supported. PDCA is iterative rather than a one-pass checklist. The cycle is useful only when the comparison produces learning that informs the next decision.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page PDCA record for a small improvement. Include the prediction, test conditions, observed result, comparison, learning, and next action.
Key point:
- PDCA links planning to testing, evidence, and action.
- The cycle should repeat as learning changes the team's understanding.
Source links:
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
9. Q: Why does Toyota place Go and See at the center of PDCA?
A: Show answer
Go and See means that people responsible for understanding a process should observe the actual work and conditions directly rather than relying only on reports, assumptions, or second-hand explanations. A process can change after a single observation, so an earlier visit may no longer represent the current situation. Direct observation helps reveal sequence, delays, workarounds, abnormal conditions, and interactions that may be hidden in summary data. It also allows the observer to ask operators what is happening and verify whether documented procedures reflect actual work. Go and See should be repeated throughout the cycle, not treated as a one-time diagnostic visit.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a short observation at the place where work occurs. Record the actual sequence, interruptions, handoffs, and differences between the documented method and the observed method.
Key point:
- Direct observation keeps problem solving connected to current reality.
- Go and See is needed during planning, testing, checking, and follow-up.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
10. Q: What is the difference between facts and data when investigating a process?
A: Show answer
Data are recorded measurements or information, while facts in the chapter's usage are verified conditions observed in the actual process. Data can be useful, but a report may be incomplete, delayed, aggregated, or based on assumptions about how work is performed. The Portugal assembly example illustrates how capacity calculations identified one presumed bottleneck while direct observation showed a different current bottleneck. This does not mean data should be ignored. It means that data should be interpreted and validated against the process. Reliable problem solving combines measurement with firsthand observation and checks whether the information represents the present condition.
Study instruction:
- Choose one performance report and verify its key assumptions through direct observation. Note where the report accurately reflects the process and where it needs context or correction.
Key point:
- Data is valuable but should not substitute for seeing and verifying the process.
- Combine measurements with firsthand evidence before concluding what the problem is.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
- ASQ: Statistical Process Control
11. Q: What does testing over talking mean in improvement work?
A: Show answer
Testing over talking means that opinions and theoretical arguments should not substitute for an experiment when a hypothesis can be tested safely and practically. People may debate whether a proposed change will work, but discussion alone cannot establish how the real process will respond. A small test can provide evidence about feasibility, side effects, timing, and obstacles. The principle does not mean that consultation or analysis is unnecessary. Teams still need to understand risks, obtain authorization, and define a prediction. It means that once a safe test is designed, learning should come from observing what happens rather than prolonging debate based only on belief or authority.
Study instruction:
- Identify a disputed improvement idea and formulate a small test that can produce evidence within a short period. State the safeguards and the result that would challenge the current belief.
Key point:
- Evidence from a controlled test is stronger than unsupported opinion.
- Use discussion to design a sound experiment, then learn from what actually happens.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
12. Q: Why must an improvement hypothesis be capable of being refuted?
A: Show answer
A useful hypothesis makes a prediction that could prove incorrect. If a team defines a test so vaguely that every outcome can be interpreted as success, it cannot learn whether its explanation was sound. Refutability requires stating what should happen, under which conditions, and what observation would show that the prediction was not supported. This keeps the team alert to contrary evidence and discourages confirmation bias. A hypothesis that is not refuted may still need further testing; one successful trial does not prove that a change will work under every condition. The purpose is to improve understanding, not to defend an idea.
Study instruction:
- Write a testable prediction for a process change and specify in advance what result would count as evidence against it.
Key point:
- A meaningful experiment allows the original prediction to be challenged.
- Define expected and disconfirming evidence before running the test.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- The W. Edwards Deming Institute: PDSA Cycle
13. Q: How should a team distinguish an experiment from an intellectual discussion or a routine implementation?
A: Show answer
An experiment begins with an explicit prediction, a defined action, observable conditions, and a planned comparison between expected and actual results. An intellectual discussion may generate ideas but does not test them. A routine implementation may roll out a change without specifying what is being learned or how unexpected effects will be recognized. A genuine PDCA test is designed so that the team can examine cause and effect and decide what to do next. It is usually limited in scope initially, monitored closely, and documented. A larger implementation should follow evidence and appropriate approval, not merely confidence or enthusiasm.
Study instruction:
- Review a proposed change and identify whether it includes a hypothesis, a bounded test, observation criteria, and a decision rule. Add any missing elements.
Key point:
- An experiment is a structured test that produces learning.
- Implementation without a prediction and review can conceal whether a change caused the result.
Source links:
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
14. Q: Why should experiments normally begin on a small scale?
A: Show answer
A small-scale test limits exposure while allowing the team to learn about the process. If the prediction is wrong, the effect can be contained before it reaches customers, disrupts a large operation, or commits substantial resources. Small tests also make it easier to observe conditions closely and isolate the effect of a proposed change. The appropriate scale depends on the risk: a low-risk workstation adjustment may be tested locally, while a safety-critical or regulated process may require formal risk assessment, authorization, and validation before any trial. Small scale does not mean informal or uncontrolled. The test must still protect people, product quality, legal obligations, and downstream users.
Study instruction:
- Design a small-scale trial and define its scope, duration, participants, approval, stop conditions, customer protection, and evidence to collect.
Key point:
- Small tests reduce the cost of learning from an incorrect prediction.
- Scale the experiment according to risk and required safeguards.
Source links:
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
15. Q: Why does Toyota describe problems and unexpected results as opportunities for learning?
A: Show answer
An unexpected result can expose a limit in the current process, reveal a false assumption, or show that the team's understanding is incomplete. A result that matches expectations may confirm what the team already believed, while a refuted hypothesis can reveal a new obstacle and point toward a more suitable next step. This is the reasoning behind the phrase that problems are jewels: they provide information that can guide improvement. The phrase does not mean that defects, injuries, or customer harm should be welcomed. It means that abnormalities should be surfaced, contained, understood, and used to strengthen the system rather than hidden or stigmatized.
Study instruction:
- Take an unexpected process result and write down what assumption it challenged, what new information it revealed, and what follow-up experiment could test the revised understanding.
Key point:
- An abnormality can reveal a boundary in current process capability.
- Treat problems as information while still containing risk and protecting customers.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
16. Q: What does the statement 'No problem is a problem' mean in the improvement kata?
A: Show answer
It means that an apparent absence of problems may indicate that people are not seeing, reporting, or responding to abnormalities. If a process is assumed to be perfect, the organization may stop learning and become less capable of adapting. A low number of reported issues could mean fewer abnormalities, but it could also mean that employees have stopped raising them or that the reporting mechanism is ineffective. Leaders should investigate the meaning of the signal before celebrating it. The chapter's point is not to manufacture problems; it is to create a system in which real deviations become visible early enough to understand and address.
Study instruction:
- Choose a process with very few reported issues. Test whether the low count reflects genuinely stable performance, weak detection, under-reporting, or barriers to escalation.
Key point:
- A low problem count is not automatically proof of a healthy process.
- Visibility and honest reporting are prerequisites for continuous learning.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
17. Q: How can an organization create a no-blame approach without weakening accountability?
A: Show answer
A no-blame approach separates investigation of a process failure from personal condemnation. It starts by asking what conditions, standards, tools, information, workload, training, or system design made the abnormality possible. It assumes that people are generally trying to do their work, while remaining willing to examine conduct and decisions when evidence warrants it. No-blame does not mean ignoring negligence, deliberate misconduct, or repeated failure to follow agreed responsibilities. Leaders should be demanding about understanding and correcting the process, and clear about expected behavior and follow-through. The distinction is between learning-oriented investigation and reflexive punishment, not between accountability and permissiveness.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent deviation and separate system factors, individual actions, management decisions, and any evidence of deliberate noncompliance. Identify the appropriate response to each.
Key point:
- No-blame is not no accountability.
- Investigate system conditions rigorously while addressing conduct fairly and proportionately.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
18. Q: Why should problem solving focus on the process rather than immediately blaming an individual?
A: Show answer
Many recurring problems are shaped by the system in which people work: unclear standards, poor layout, unreliable equipment, inadequate materials, conflicting priorities, insufficient training, or unrealistic workload. Blaming an operator before examining these conditions can hide the actual cause and make the same failure recur with another person. A process focus asks what made the error possible and what change would make the desired work easier and more reliable. This does not remove individual responsibility. It ensures that responsibility is assessed after the process and evidence are understood, rather than assumed from the fact that a person was closest to the event.
Study instruction:
- Use a cause-and-effect review to examine work design, materials, equipment, methods, information, environment, and management conditions before deciding whether individual conduct contributed.
Key point:
- Recurring failures often require changes to the work system, not only reminders to workers.
- Understand the process before assigning responsibility.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
19. Q: What does 'Be hard on the process, but soft on the operators' mean?
A: Show answer
The phrase calls for rigorous examination of the work system while treating the people doing the work with respect. Leaders should challenge unclear standards, unstable methods, unnecessary movement, poor material presentation, and conditions that make errors likely. They should listen to operators because those closest to the work often know where the process becomes difficult or abnormal. A respectful approach encourages people to report problems instead of hiding them. Being soft on operators does not mean accepting unsafe conduct or ignoring agreed standards. It means starting with curiosity and process analysis, then addressing responsibilities fairly using evidence.
Study instruction:
- Observe a task and ask operators what prevents them from following the standard consistently. Compare their answers with the actual work conditions and identify a process improvement.
Key point:
- Respectful treatment makes problems more visible.
- High standards for the process can coexist with respect and clear expectations for people.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
20. Q: How should leaders use the question 'What is preventing the operators from working according to the standard?'
A: Show answer
The question directs attention to barriers between the documented method and actual work. Leaders should first confirm that a clear, current, practical standard exists and that operators have been trained to use it. They should then observe the task and examine equipment condition, material availability, layout, timing, workload, conflicting instructions, and process dependencies. Operators should be invited to explain workarounds and recurring difficulties without being led toward a predetermined answer. The findings may show that the standard is unrealistic, outdated, poorly communicated, or difficult to follow under actual conditions. The response should address the verified barrier and then check whether the change improves performance.
Study instruction:
- Select one standard-work requirement and test it at the workplace. Document each barrier that prevents consistent execution and validate the findings with the people doing the task.
Key point:
- A standard should be usable in the real process.
- Ask about barriers before assuming that nonconformance is simply a motivation problem.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
21. Q: What can an andon signal teach an organization about abnormalities?
A: Show answer
An andon signal makes an abnormal condition visible and calls for assistance while the issue is still occurring. In the chapter's example, an operator uses an andon pull to request help for a problem such as a cross-threaded bolt or a task taking too long. The value lies not in the signal count itself but in the timely response, investigation, and learning that follow. Toyota's official description of jidoka similarly explains that an operator can stop a line when an abnormality occurs and use an andon display to call the responsible person. An andon system is therefore a response mechanism, not merely a visual indicator or performance statistic.
Study instruction:
- Trace one abnormality signal from detection through response, containment, cause investigation, corrective action, and confirmation that recurrence risk has been reduced.
Key point:
- An abnormality signal is useful only when it triggers timely support and learning.
- Visual escalation connects process detection with problem solving.
Source links:
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
22. Q: Why might a decline in andon pulls be a warning rather than an improvement?
A: Show answer
A decline may indicate fewer process abnormalities, but it may also indicate that operators are reluctant to call for help, that the cord or reporting method is not being used, or that the culture has shifted toward hiding problems. The chapter describes a Toyota plant where a fall in andon pulls prompted leaders to investigate whether employees were failing to report problems. The response was to reinforce the expectation to call for help and examine whether process buffers could be adjusted if genuine abnormalities had decreased. The lesson is to interpret a metric in context and verify the underlying process before declaring success.
Study instruction:
- Build a balanced interpretation of a falling incident or escalation count using corroborating measures such as quality, rework, downtime, operator feedback, and response records.
Key point:
- A falling signal count can reflect under-reporting as well as better performance.
- Validate the meaning of a metric before rewarding or acting on it.
Source links:
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
23. Q: Why is checking only the final outcome insufficient for effective PDCA?
A: Show answer
A final outcome shows whether the overall result was achieved, but it may not reveal where the process deviated or what caused the shortfall. If a morning routine takes 64 minutes instead of the 60-minute target, the total tells the person that the result was late but not which activity consumed the extra time. By the time the result is measured, the context of earlier steps may have disappeared and there may be no opportunity to adjust the remaining work. Effective learning requires checks during the process at intervals that preserve the cause trail and allow timely adaptation.
Study instruction:
- Compare an end-of-process measure with intermediate checks for one workflow. Identify what each measure can reveal and when the information becomes available.
Key point:
- Outcome measures show the final result but may conceal process causes.
- Frequent process checks make timely learning and adjustment possible.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
24. Q: What information should a useful target condition contain?
A: Show answer
A useful target condition generally describes the process steps, their sequence and expected times, relevant process characteristics, process metrics, and outcome metrics. The description should be detailed enough for people to see whether the process is operating as intended and to identify abnormalities at specific points. It should also make clear the time horizon and the conditions under which the target is expected to be achieved. A target condition is not a guarantee that the process will behave exactly as described. It is a testable picture of the desired operating state that helps the team recognize gaps, define experiments, and learn.
Study instruction:
- Draft a target-condition sheet for a real process. Include step sequence, expected duration, quality or flow characteristics, process measures, outcome measure, and review horizon.
Key point:
- Specific process detail enables early detection of deviations.
- A target condition must be observable enough to guide experiments.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
25. Q: What is the difference between process metrics and outcome metrics?
A: Show answer
Process metrics measure how the work is unfolding at intermediate steps, while outcome metrics measure the final result. For example, the number of units completed every 30 minutes can be a process metric, while the total units completed by the end of a shift is an outcome metric. Process metrics help identify where and when the work departs from the expected pattern. Outcome metrics show whether the overall objective was achieved. Neither category is sufficient alone: process measures without outcome context can encourage local optimization, while outcome measures alone can reveal problems too late. A balanced target condition uses both and makes their relationship explicit.
Study instruction:
- Classify the measures in a current performance report as process or outcome metrics. Explain how each measure supports a different management decision.
Key point:
- Process metrics support timely diagnosis; outcome metrics assess the final result.
- Use both to connect local process behavior with customer or business outcomes.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
- ASQ: Statistical Process Control
26. Q: How do short PDCA cycles improve the ability to adapt?
A: Show answer
Short cycles reduce the time between taking an action and learning what happened. When a deviation is detected soon after it occurs, the relevant conditions are easier to observe, the cause trail is still available, and the team may still have time to adjust the remaining work. Short cycles also make it possible to test one change, observe the effect, and revise the next step without waiting for a long project phase to finish. The cycle should be as short as practical without compromising safety, measurement quality, or the time needed for an effect to become visible.
Study instruction:
- Choose a process and determine the shortest useful interval for checking it. Explain what information would be lost if the interval were longer and what risks arise if it is too short.
Key point:
- Short feedback loops preserve context and enable course correction.
- Set review frequency according to process speed, risk, and the time needed for effects to emerge.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
27. Q: Why does improvement need to reach the detailed process level?
A: Show answer
Broad goals such as reducing cost, improving customer satisfaction, or increasing output are achieved through specific work activities. The chapter calls this the organizational fractal or detail level: the scale at which people can observe, test, and change the actual conditions that determine results. A vision such as ending hunger depends on details such as fuel, road access, and reliable deliveries. Similarly, a production target depends on work sequence, equipment availability, operator movement, and material presentation. Management by objectives and high-level incentives may describe desired results but cannot replace detailed process understanding. Improvement must reach the level where the work is performed.
Study instruction:
- Break one strategic objective into process-level conditions and identify the smallest observable work elements that influence its achievement.
Key point:
- Strategic outcomes are produced through detailed operational conditions.
- Improvement becomes actionable when it reaches the level of actual work.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
28. Q: How can the morning-routine example demonstrate process-level learning?
A: Show answer
If the only measure is the total time from waking to entering the car, a result of 64 minutes against a 60-minute target reveals a four-minute gap but not its location. A more detailed target condition assigns expected times to each step. Intermediate checks can then show that making breakfast took four minutes longer than expected. This identifies where to investigate and may allow the person to adjust later steps to preserve the overall outcome. The extra time does not yet prove why breakfast took longer, so the next action should be observation of that routine rather than an immediate assumption about the cause.
Study instruction:
- Create a timed sequence for a recurring personal or work routine. Compare expected and actual time at each step and identify the first deviation that needs investigation.
Key point:
- Detailed step timing turns a final shortfall into a traceable process question.
- Finding where a deviation occurs is not the same as knowing its root cause.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
29. Q: How can intermediate production checks help a factory recover from a shortfall?
A: Show answer
If a target is 32 boxes over two shifts and management checks only at the end of each shift, several small delays may have accumulated before the shortfall becomes visible. The reasons may be difficult to reconstruct, and there may be little time to recover before delivery. If each box or kanban card represents approximately 30 minutes of production time, checking at that interval can provide an early warning. A shortfall then points to a recent period that can be investigated while the conditions remain visible. The measure is useful only if it is tied to a defined expected rate and a response process.
Study instruction:
- Design an interval-based production check. Specify the expected quantity, actual quantity, tolerance or alert condition, person responding, and method for recording the cause.
Key point:
- Early indicators preserve the context needed to investigate production loss.
- A process metric must trigger a timely response, not merely populate a board.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
- ASQ: Statistical Process Control
30. Q: What role must support functions play when operations use frequent process checks?
A: Show answer
Frequent checking creates value only when the organization can respond to what the checks reveal. If logistics, maintenance, quality, planning, or supervisory support arrives only after a problem has already delayed the process, the information may be too late to help. Support functions need clear response expectations, escalation routes, authority to act, and access to the relevant process information. The chapter contrasts scheduled response to an expected need with a pickup that occurs only after a pallet is finished. In the latter case, the timing is reactive rather than experimental. A functioning improvement system connects process operators with support resources at the moment an abnormality is detected.
Study instruction:
- Map one operational support request from signal to resolution. Identify expected response time, ownership, escalation, and what happens if the support function cannot respond.
Key point:
- Measurement without response capability does not create adaptiveness.
- Design the support process alongside the operating metric.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
31. Q: How can a visual management board become a tool for learning rather than justification?
A: Show answer
A board should show the expected condition, actual performance, deviations, current obstacles, responsible response, and follow-up. If comments are used mainly to explain after the fact why a target was missed, the board becomes a record of excuses rather than a trigger for action. A learning-oriented board is reviewed during the work period, while the process context is still available. The team uses it to identify abnormalities, ask what is happening, request support, and test countermeasures. The visual format alone does not create improvement; the behavior and response routine around the board determine whether it supports PDCA.
Study instruction:
- Review an existing performance board and assess whether it enables real-time response. Add fields for expected timing, actual condition, abnormality, owner, immediate action, and learning.
Key point:
- A board is a communication and response mechanism, not a decoration.
- The management routine around a visual tool matters more than copying its appearance.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
32. Q: Why should frequent process checks not be used primarily to police employees?
A: Show answer
If employees believe checks are designed to catch them working slowly or to punish deviations, they may alter behavior when a manager approaches, conceal problems, or report only what appears acceptable. This creates an artificial picture of the process and prevents leaders from seeing normal operating conditions. The chapter argues that the proper intent is to find problems because they provide opportunities to learn and improve the system. Leaders should explain the purpose of checks, ask neutral questions, and respond constructively to abnormalities. Standards and accountability remain necessary, but monitoring should not turn into surveillance that suppresses honest reporting.
Study instruction:
- Observe how employees respond during a management walk. Identify behaviors that may indicate fear or metric gaming and propose changes to the leader's questions and response.
Key point:
- The intent behind measurement influences the information people provide.
- A learning culture requires truthful visibility, not performance theater.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
33. Q: Why does Toyota seek rapid PDCA cycles?
A: Show answer
Rapid cycles reveal the next obstacle sooner. Since the team cannot fully understand the next challenge until it takes a step, delaying that step also delays learning. Short cycles allow people to test a manageable change, see the result, and make another adjustment while the process context is still fresh. The chapter notes that some cycles may take only minutes. The appropriate speed depends on the process and the risk of the change; speed should not be confused with haste or inadequate preparation. The aim is to reduce unnecessary waiting between hypothesis, action, observation, and learning.
Study instruction:
- Measure the elapsed time from identifying an obstacle to testing a response and reviewing the result. Identify delays that do not add necessary safety, quality, or decision value.
Key point:
- Fast feedback accelerates learning and reveals subsequent obstacles.
- Make the cycle short while retaining adequate control and observation.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
34. Q: Why should a PDCA experiment generally change one factor at a time?
A: Show answer
Changing one factor at a time makes it easier to connect the observed effect to the action taken. If several variables are changed simultaneously, a favorable or unfavorable result may be difficult to explain because the team cannot tell which change produced it. Single-factor experiments support clearer cause-and-effect learning and make the next decision more reliable. There are situations where designed multi-factor experiments are appropriate, particularly when interactions between factors are important, but those require a deliberate experimental method. The chapter's practical guidance is to isolate one problem and one change where feasible, then shorten the cycle so learning does not become unnecessarily slow.
Study instruction:
- Take a proposed improvement with multiple changes and separate it into testable steps. State when interaction effects might require a more formal experiment.
Key point:
- Isolating a variable improves interpretability.
- Do not change several things at once unless the experiment is deliberately designed to test their interaction.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
35. Q: Why is a provisional step often better than waiting for a perfect solution?
A: Show answer
A provisional step can remove or test one obstacle quickly and reveal what should be learned next. Waiting for a fully engineered solution may leave the team operating under the same unknown conditions for weeks. A temporary solution can also expose design requirements before expensive equipment or permanent fixtures are built. The chapter's examples include temporary point-of-use material delivery and a provisional fixture for shipping mixed cylinder sizes. A provisional step must still be safe, compliant, reversible where possible, and clearly identified as temporary. It should have an owner, review date, acceptance criteria, and a decision about whether to modify, standardize, or remove it.
Study instruction:
- Identify an obstacle that is delaying a permanent solution. Design a safe temporary test and specify its limits, owner, expiry or review date, and learning objective.
Key point:
- A controlled provisional step can create learning earlier than a perfect design.
- Temporary measures need boundaries, ownership, and a review decision.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
36. Q: How did temporary logistics changes help the factory test a 16-second cycle-time target?
A: Show answer
Observers timed 20 successive cycles and found that the operator periodically left the line to obtain trays of parts, making the cycle unstable. A long-term logistics concept for point-of-use delivery would take weeks to design and implement. The chapter recommends not waiting for the full system: if feasible, bring parts to the operator temporarily so the team can remove that variable and observe the next obstacle. The temporary arrangement is not assumed to be the final design. Its purpose is to create a faster, controlled test and improve understanding before investing in a permanent material route.
Study instruction:
- Time repeated cycles, identify the interruption that causes variation, and propose a temporary material-delivery test. Define how to monitor safety, replenishment, and cycle stability.
Key point:
- A temporary change can isolate a suspected cause while permanent design continues.
- Use the test to learn what the final logistics system must achieve.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
37. Q: What does the hydraulic-cylinder fixture example teach about flow and customer demand?
A: Show answer
The supplier shipped cylinders in pallets separated by size, while the customer needed only two cylinders of any size at a time. This mismatch created opened pallets and aging inventory at the receiving factory. A proposed 1×1 flow would ship the pairs actually needed, but a new mixed-size fixture would take weeks to design. The improvement-kata response is to create a provisional fixture quickly, even if it includes temporary waste, so the team can test the flow and learn before committing to a costly permanent design. The example shows that improvement should be judged against the customer's actual consumption pattern, not merely the supplier's convenient batch size.
Study instruction:
- Map the supplier-to-customer flow and compare shipment batch size with actual demand. Identify inventory, handling, and responsiveness effects of a provisional smaller-batch trial.
Key point:
- The customer's consumption pattern should inform the flow design.
- Prototype the new flow before investing in a permanent fixture.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
38. Q: What lesson does the flow-rack story provide about premature permanent design?
A: Show answer
The author describes racks built quickly and beautifully to an initial design, only for production trials to reveal changes in operator task allocation, part locations, reach heights, and layout. Because the racks were fabricated as finished equipment, later modifications became costly and slow. The better approach would have been to use simple provisional racks during the unstable trial period, adjust them as the process changed, and build permanent fixtures only after the design requirements became clearer. The story illustrates how premature commitment can convert learning into rework. A polished solution is not necessarily a good early experiment.
Study instruction:
- Before commissioning permanent equipment, list which process assumptions remain uncertain. Decide what can be tested with temporary materials and what must be fixed for safety or compliance.
Key point:
- Stabilize the process concept before investing in final fixtures.
- Prototype and learn before optimizing the permanent design.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
39. Q: How should teams keep an open mind during PDCA cycles?
A: Show answer
Teams should treat each experiment as an opportunity to discover what is actually happening, not as a chance to prove that their preferred explanation is correct. Bias can influence what people notice, which evidence they collect, and how they interpret unexpected outcomes. To remain open-minded, define the prediction in advance, record observations even when they contradict expectations, ask operators for alternative explanations, and distinguish what was observed from what was inferred. Avoid selecting only favorable evidence or changing the success criteria after seeing the result. Open-mindedness is a disciplined practice of allowing evidence to change the team's understanding.
Study instruction:
- Before a test, write down your expected result and likely assumptions. Afterward, record observations separately from interpretations and ask what evidence would challenge your explanation.
Key point:
- Scientific improvement requires willingness to revise beliefs.
- Document unexpected results rather than explaining them away.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- The W. Edwards Deming Institute: PDSA Cycle
40. Q: What explains Toyota's ability to achieve challenging target conditions more reliably?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the advantage is not simply greater discipline, fewer problems, sudden innovation, or airtight planning. It is the ability to detect small process-level problems early, while their context is still visible and corrective action is still possible. Short PDCA cycles generate more frequent learning and allow teams to adjust to obstacles that could not have been predicted. The organization therefore executes more effectively under uncertainty. This capability depends on people being willing to surface abnormalities, leaders responding constructively, and management routines staying close to actual work rather than relying exclusively on distant outcome reports.
Study instruction:
- Compare a project managed through milestone-only reviews with one using frequent process-level PDCA. Identify how each detects deviations and how quickly it can adapt.
Key point:
- Adaptiveness comes from early detection and repeated learning.
- Execution capability depends on how an organization responds to the unpredictable.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
41. Q: Why do organizations often respond incorrectly when a plan fails late?
A: Show answer
When problems remain hidden until the final outcome, managers may conclude that the plan was poor, employees lacked discipline, or individuals made mistakes. They may respond by creating a new plan, adding pressure, issuing more countermeasures, or replacing people. Those actions may not address the actual process conditions that caused the failure. The chapter describes a pattern in which everything is reported as fine until the end, when a large problem appears. Without intermediate checks, the organization loses the evidence needed to understand the sequence of small deviations. The corrective response should therefore reconstruct the process and strengthen early detection, not merely intensify the original management approach.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a delayed project and reconstruct the sequence of small deviations, when each became visible, who knew, and why the information did not trigger an earlier response.
Key point:
- Late discovery encourages superficial explanations and blame.
- Improve the information and response system before simply rewriting the plan.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
42. Q: What are Toyota's five questions for moving toward a target condition?
A: Show answer
The five questions are: (1) What is the target condition? (2) What is the actual condition now? (3) What obstacles are preventing progress, and which one is being addressed now? (4) What is the next step? and (5) When can the team go and see what has been learned from that step? Together they create a compact routine for orienting the team, understanding reality, focusing on an obstacle, defining a test, and scheduling the learning review. They are used after a target condition has been established, during the PDCA phase of the improvement kata. Before a target condition exists, the order of target and current-condition questions is reversed.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the five questions and use them during a process observation. Ensure each answer is based on current evidence and leads logically to the next question.
Key point:
- The questions form a repeatable routine for scientific problem solving.
- The sequence connects direction, current condition, obstacles, action, and learning review.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
43. Q: How should the target-condition question be answered in a coaching or improvement discussion?
A: Show answer
The answer should describe the desired operating condition clearly enough that a person can compare it with the current process. It should identify the relevant process behavior, sequence, timing, quality, flow, and outcome measures rather than relying on a broad aspiration such as 'be more efficient.' A target condition should be challenging but meaningful and connected to a larger direction or customer need. It is not a detailed prediction of every action required to get there. The team should be able to tell when the target condition is being approached, what abnormality would matter, and what evidence will show whether the desired condition has become stable.
Study instruction:
- Replace a vague improvement statement with a specific target condition and define the observable indicators that would demonstrate progress.
Key point:
- A useful target condition is specific, observable, and connected to purpose.
- Describe how the process should operate, not only what result is desired.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
44. Q: How should a team determine the actual condition now?
A: Show answer
The team should observe the process directly, establish the current sequence and performance, and distinguish verified facts from assumptions. Useful information may include timing, work-in-process, defects, interruptions, handoffs, equipment condition, material availability, and operator workarounds. Measurements should be collected at a level of detail that helps explain the gap between current and target conditions. Averages or summary reports may hide variation, so teams should inspect the conditions under which different results occur. The current condition is not a static profile; it can change as demand, staffing, equipment, and other circumstances change. It must therefore be refreshed as the work progresses.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a current-condition map based on observation and measurements. Record when and where the data were collected and identify variation that an average would conceal.
Key point:
- The current condition must reflect what is happening now.
- Use direct observation and relevant measurements to establish a credible baseline.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
- ASQ: Statistical Process Control
45. Q: How should obstacles be identified and prioritized?
A: Show answer
Obstacles are conditions that currently prevent the process from reaching the target condition. They should be identified from the gap between the observed current condition and the desired operating state, not generated as a generic list of possible solutions. The team should distinguish a confirmed obstacle from a suspected cause and decide which obstacle to address now. A useful first choice is one that is relevant to progress, sufficiently understood to test, and suitable for a controlled experiment. Safety, customer impact, legal obligations, and dependencies may affect priority. The team should avoid attempting to solve every obstacle simultaneously because doing so makes learning and cause-and-effect interpretation more difficult.
Study instruction:
- List the obstacles supported by current evidence. Select one for the next cycle and explain why it is the most appropriate one to address first.
Key point:
- Prioritize a specific current obstacle rather than launching many countermeasures at once.
- Selection should consider impact, evidence, testability, and risk.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
46. Q: What makes a next step suitable for the next PDCA cycle?
A: Show answer
A suitable next step is a concrete action that addresses the obstacle currently being investigated and can produce useful information. It should be small enough to test, have a clear prediction, identify who will perform it, and specify when and how the result will be observed. It should not be a vague intention such as 'improve communication' or a large bundle of unrelated actions. The next step may be provisional and may not solve the entire problem. Its purpose is to move the process closer to the target condition while revealing what the team needs to learn next. Appropriate safeguards and authorization must be defined before execution.
Study instruction:
- Convert a broad action item into one testable next step with an owner, prediction, scope, evidence, safeguards, and review point.
Key point:
- A next step is an experiment, not merely an activity on a task list.
- Make the step specific enough to test and review.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
47. Q: Why must the team decide when it will go and see what was learned?
A: Show answer
A scheduled review closes the feedback loop and prevents an experiment from becoming an unmonitored action. The review time should be soon enough to preserve process context but late enough for the expected effect to become observable. At the review, the team should compare actual results with the prediction, examine unexpected conditions, and decide whether to adapt, repeat, standardize, or investigate further. Without a defined review point, people may forget to collect evidence, continue a temporary change indefinitely, or assume that an action succeeded because it was completed. The commitment to go and see makes learning part of the work rather than an optional retrospective.
Study instruction:
- Add a review date and time to every improvement test. Define what evidence will be available and who must attend or contribute to the review.
Key point:
- Every test needs a planned observation and learning review.
- Completion of an action is not evidence that the intended effect occurred.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- ASQ: Plan-Do-Check-Act Cycle
48. Q: Why does Toyota emphasize understanding the current situation before choosing a countermeasure?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes problem solving as deeply understanding the work system and the conditions that produced the problem. If the cause is not yet clear, applying multiple countermeasures may treat symptoms, introduce new variables, or make it impossible to know which action helped. Direct observation, careful questioning, and reconstruction of the process should therefore precede solution selection. Once the mechanism is understood, a suitable countermeasure may become much more obvious. This approach can require patience, especially when managers are accustomed to rewarding rapid action, but it reduces guesswork and improves the chance that the change addresses the actual cause.
Study instruction:
- Take a recurring defect or delay and document the observed sequence that produces it. Do not propose a countermeasure until the causal mechanism is supported by evidence.
Key point:
- The first task in problem solving is to grasp the situation.
- Premature countermeasures can obscure causes and create additional variation.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
49. Q: What does the turbine-blade coating example reveal about root-cause investigation?
A: Show answer
Some precision-cast turbine blades were dented when they contacted neighboring blades on a spray-coating conveyor. Initial suggestions included spacing blades farther apart or adding protective shields. One engineer instead observed the process for several hours and noticed that, at a 90-degree conveyor turn, some blades rotated slightly and unscrewed their hooks. The loosened hooks allowed the blades to swing into neighboring parts. Once this mechanism was understood, the countermeasure was clear: prevent the hooks from unscrewing. The example shows why observation of the actual process can outperform rapid brainstorming based on assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the example as a cause-and-effect chain: conveyor turn, blade rotation, hook movement, swinging, contact, and damage. Identify the observation that revealed the mechanism.
Key point:
- The right countermeasure followed from understanding the physical cause.
- Observe the process long enough to see intermittent failure mechanisms.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
50. Q: What should a team do when the right solution is not yet obvious?
A: Show answer
It should return to the process and gather more evidence rather than choosing a countermeasure merely to demonstrate action. The team may need to observe additional cycles, compare normal and abnormal conditions, inspect equipment, talk with operators, examine timing, or trace material and information flow. It should state what remains unknown and formulate the next observation or experiment to reduce that uncertainty. If the issue has safety, customer, or compliance implications, immediate containment may be required even while root-cause investigation continues. Containment protects the process; it should not be confused with proof that the underlying cause has been eliminated.
Study instruction:
- Write a problem statement that separates known facts, suspected causes, unknowns, immediate containment, and the next evidence-gathering action.
Key point:
- Lack of a clear solution may signal insufficient understanding.
- Contain immediate risk while continuing to investigate the underlying mechanism.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
51. Q: Why is firefighting not a substitute for systematic problem solving?
A: Show answer
Firefighting restores operation temporarily, often under pressure, but may leave the cause untouched. If organizations reward only the person who gets production moving again, they may neglect the investigation needed to prevent recurrence. Repeated expedites can become normal work, consume management attention, and conceal unstable processes. Systematic problem solving includes immediate containment where necessary, followed by direct observation, causal analysis, a tested countermeasure, and confirmation that the problem does not recur. Urgent response and learning are not opposites; the organization needs both, with clear separation between restoring safe operation and eliminating the cause.
Study instruction:
- Review a recurring emergency response. Identify which actions were containment, which addressed root causes, and what evidence would demonstrate sustained correction.
Key point:
- Containment restores control; countermeasures address the cause.
- Do not allow repeated emergency fixes to replace learning and prevention.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Toyota Motor Corporation: Toyota Production System
52. Q: Why should improvement continue after a target condition has been reached?
A: Show answer
A process can regress if the organization stops pursuing a meaningful challenge. Reaching a target condition consistently is a reason to reflect on what was learned and consider the next target, not necessarily a signal to end improvement. The chapter describes the improvement kata as an ongoing cycle: one target condition establishes the foundation for another. The next challenge may emerge from obstacles encountered during the current effort or from a broader direction that remains unmet. Without a continuing challenge, teams may focus only on maintaining the current state and gradually lose the habits of observation, experimentation, and adaptation.
Study instruction:
- After a target condition has been sustained, conduct a reflection that records what changed, what was learned, what remains unstable, and what the next challenge could be.
Key point:
- A reached target condition becomes a platform for the next challenge.
- Continuous improvement requires an ongoing direction and learning routine.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
53. Q: How should a team respond when a target condition is not achieved by the planned date?
A: Show answer
It should examine what was learned from the experiments, what obstacles remain, and whether the target, timing, or approach should be revised. Missing a date is not automatically evidence of poor effort; the team may have discovered that an assumption was false or that the process has a capability limit not previously understood. However, accepting failure without analysis is equally inappropriate. The team should distinguish an informative test result from avoidable delay, assess customer and operational consequences, communicate any revised expectation, and define the next experiment. The chapter emphasizes that failure can be a rich source of learning when it is examined honestly.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a missed-target review. Compare predictions with actual conditions, identify the obstacle that caused the gap, and define the next test and any necessary escalation.
Key point:
- A missed target can generate valuable learning if the causes are understood.
- Learning does not remove the need to manage commitments and consequences.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
54. Q: Why should awards and completed improvements be treated as a beginning rather than an endpoint?
A: Show answer
Recognition can acknowledge effort and results, but it should not signal that the organization has finished learning. The chapter contrasts presentations that end with a trophy or plaque with Toyota's emphasis on the next challenge. Celebrating a result is compatible with continuous improvement if the team also asks what the achievement reveals, which conditions remain difficult, and what target should follow. Treating an award as the endpoint can encourage complacency and focus attention on status rather than process capability. The more useful measure of progress is whether the organization has improved its current condition and strengthened its ability to tackle the next challenge.
Study instruction:
- At the end of an improvement project, prepare a review that recognizes the result and identifies the next challenge, remaining instability, and capability developed by the team.
Key point:
- Celebrate progress without treating it as finality.
- The next challenge sustains learning after a successful improvement.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata and Toyota Kata Practice Guide
