Table 7
Process Improvement Roles and Abnormality Response at Toyota
1. Q: Who should carry out process improvements at Toyota?
A: Show answer
Process improvement is carried out primarily by people whose operating roles include responsibility for improving and managing production processes: team leaders, group leaders, production supervisory staff, superintendents, and manufacturing engineers. Production operators also contribute, especially within their immediate work area, but they are not expected to carry the entire improvement responsibility autonomously while simultaneously meeting production requirements. The chapter rejects three inadequate arrangements: leaving improvement solely to operators, assuming that anyone who notices a problem will respond, or delegating improvement to a separate specialist team. The effective arrangement makes improvement part of normal line management, assigns a clear first responder at each process, and engages operators in improvement under guidance. Responsibility is therefore distributed across the organization but is not vague: each role has defined ownership and support.
Study instruction:
- "Review the distinction between process ownership, first-response responsibility, technical support, and operator participation. Be able to name the roles that carry improvement as part of their regular work."
Key point:
- Improvement is a management and operating responsibility, not a side activity owned by one group.
- Operators contribute, while designated leaders and engineers ensure that improvement is continuous and coordinated.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
2. Q: Why is the idea of fully autonomous operator teams a misconception about Toyota's approach?
A: Show answer
The misconception is that Toyota's continuous improvement is mainly a self-directed activity in which production operators independently identify, investigate, and implement improvements in their own processes. The chapter explains that this is not the operating model described for Toyota. Operators are close to the work and their knowledge is essential, but proximity alone does not provide the time, authority, technical support, or problem-solving capability needed to manage every abnormality and improve the process continuously. Toyota instead uses a defined line organization in which team leaders and supervisory roles support production and coach improvement. Rejecting autonomous teams as the primary mechanism does not reject participation; it rejects the assumption that operators can carry production, problem response, and process improvement simultaneously without structured support.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the popular self-directed-team assumption with the chapter's description of line-based responsibility. Identify which duties require a designated leader or specialist."
Key point:
- Operator involvement is essential, but autonomous operator teams are not the primary improvement structure described in the chapter.
- Empowerment requires support, capability development, and clear responsibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
3. Q: Does limiting operator autonomy mean that operators should not participate in improvement?
A: Show answer
No. Operators should be engaged regularly because they understand the actual work, notice friction in the process, and can identify practical opportunities within their work envelope. The chapter describes operators participating in quality circles, suggestion systems, and improvements to their immediate work in collaboration with the team leader. The distinction is between meaningful participation and transferring the entire improvement burden to operators. A sound system invites ideas, helps operators test and learn, provides coaching and technical assistance, and ensures that production responsibilities remain manageable. Participation should develop people's improvement capability and contribute to better work, rather than become an additional unresourced obligation layered on top of a fully loaded production assignment.
Study instruction:
- "List the ways operators can contribute without becoming solely accountable for the whole improvement system. Consider suggestions, observation, testing, and collaboration with the team leader."
Key point:
- Operator participation is a core part of improvement and learning.
- Participation is most effective when supported by coaching, time, and clear operational ownership.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
4. Q: Why can a fully loaded production operator not reliably make parts and investigate process problems at the same time?
A: Show answer
In a tightly balanced one-piece-flow process, an operator is expected to perform work within the planned cycle time. When an abnormality occurs, investigating its cause requires attention, observation, questioning, and sometimes testing. Those activities compete with the operator's immediate production task. If the operator stops to investigate, output falls behind; if the operator resumes production immediately, the opportunity to examine the event while it is occurring may disappear. The chapter uses this conflict to show why assigning both duties to the same fully loaded operator is structurally unrealistic. It is not a question of motivation. The work design must provide a responder who can attend to abnormalities while operators continue or safely pause production according to the process and safety requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Map the tasks performed during a normal cycle and during an abnormality. Identify which tasks can occur concurrently and which require the operator's full attention."
Key point:
- Production and investigation can compete for the same limited time and attention.
- The operating system must provide capacity for immediate response rather than assume operators can absorb every task.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
5. Q: How can operator-only improvement expectations become disrespectful of people?
A: Show answer
They become disrespectful when an organization expects operators to meet demanding production targets, resolve recurring process problems, and independently redesign the process without providing time, support, training, or authority. Such an expectation shifts a management and work-design responsibility onto people who may not control staffing, equipment, standards, or upstream conditions. It can also create a false choice between meeting output expectations and investigating causes. The chapter's criticism is not that operators lack intelligence or initiative; it is that the system should not impose incompatible responsibilities and then treat failure as an individual shortcoming. Respect for people requires designing roles and support so that employees can contribute to improvement without being set up to fail.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether improvement expectations are compatible with actual workload, cycle time, skills, authority, and available support. Identify any duties that have been added without capacity."
Key point:
- Respect requires a workable system, not simply exhortations to take ownership.
- Improvement responsibilities must be matched with time, capability, and organizational support.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
6. Q: Why does assuming that anyone or everyone will respond to an abnormality often mean that nobody responds?
A: Show answer
When responsibility is expressed as belonging to everyone, no individual may feel specifically accountable for acting. People may assume that another person has noticed the signal, has greater authority, or is already responding. The chapter illustrates this with andon warning lamps that illuminate but receive no response. A visible signal is only an information mechanism; it does not by itself create an owner, response time, decision authority, or escalation route. A reliable system names the first responder for each process and makes clear what that person must do when a signal occurs. Other employees may assist, but assistance does not replace an accountable owner.
Study instruction:
- "Review a real or hypothetical alert process and identify the named owner, backup, expected response, and escalation path. Check whether each alert is acknowledged and closed."
Key point:
- A signal without an assigned responder is an incomplete control.
- Clear individual accountability prevents alerts from being ignored through diffusion of responsibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
7. Q: Why is a separate continuous-improvement team insufficient as the main owner of daily process improvement?
A: Show answer
A specialist team can provide facilitation, technical expertise, training, or support for complex changes, but it cannot be the sole engine of improvement if the objective is to improve every process every day. A separate team is removed from many immediate operating decisions and cannot be present at every process abnormality. If line leaders treat improvement as work delegated to specialists, daily management and improvement become disconnected. The chapter describes improvement kata as embedded in work processes and taught across the organization, rather than assigned to a dedicated group of improvement agents. Specialist support can strengthen the system, but line managers and process leaders must retain ownership of the conditions and results in their areas.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish specialist support from process ownership. Identify which improvement activities require local daily leadership and which genuinely benefit from specialist expertise."
Key point:
- Continuous improvement must be embedded in daily management.
- Specialists can enable improvement, but they cannot substitute for line-level ownership at every process.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
8. Q: What two broad categories of improvement activity does Koichi Shimizu identify at Toyota?
A: Show answer
Shimizu distinguishes voluntary improvement activity carried out by production operators through quality circles, suggestion systems, and similar initiatives from organized improvement carried out by team leaders, production supervisory staff, and engineers as part of their job functions. The categories differ in their organizational purpose and management arrangements. Voluntary activity gives operators opportunities to develop improvement thinking and contribute ideas. Organized improvement is directly integrated into the responsibilities of people who manage production processes and is connected to productivity, quality, and cost objectives. The categories should not be treated as competing alternatives. They are complementary forms of participation within a broader management system.
Study instruction:
- "Read the two categories in Shimizu's paper and create a comparison showing participants, formal responsibilities, typical mechanisms, and intended contribution."
Key point:
- Toyota improvement includes both voluntary operator activity and organized supervisory and engineering activity.
- The two categories serve related but distinct purposes.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
9. Q: How should the chapter's approximately 10 percent and 90 percent improvement figures be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports Shimizu's finding that roughly 10 percent of realized productivity and cost improvement came from voluntary operator activity and roughly 90 percent from improvement performed by team leaders, supervisory staff, and engineers as part of their jobs. These figures challenge the assumption that most realized improvement is generated by autonomous operator teams. They should not be treated as a universal benchmark, a current audited statistic, or a target that every factory must reproduce. Shimizu's paper explains that the 10 percent figure was based on a rough impression reported by a Toyota manager and notes corroborating discussion in another cited work. The figures are best used to understand the relative importance of organized improvement in the case examined, while recognizing the limits of the evidence and historical context.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the figures to the underlying paper and note the author's qualification about their evidential basis. Avoid presenting them as a universal performance standard."
Key point:
- The 10/90 split emphasizes the importance of organized improvement in the Toyota case described.
- The estimate is contextual and should not be generalized as a guaranteed ratio for other organizations.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
10. Q: Why is the main purpose of voluntary operator improvement not limited to the immediate improvement itself?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that voluntary activities such as quality circles and suggestions also develop operators' kaizen mindset and problem-solving ability. Participation gives people practice in noticing work conditions, articulating problems, proposing ideas, and collaborating on changes. It can also help leaders identify employees who demonstrate initiative and may be candidates for team-leader development. This capability-building purpose is important even when the direct productivity or cost contribution of voluntary activity is smaller than that of organized improvement. The benefit therefore includes both the immediate change and the future ability of employees to participate more effectively in process improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an operator suggestion by considering both its immediate process effect and the learning or capability it creates for the people involved."
Key point:
- Voluntary improvement develops people as well as processes.
- The value of an improvement activity should not be judged only by its immediate financial effect.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
11. Q: How do quality circles and suggestion systems contribute to operator development?
A: Show answer
Quality circles and suggestion systems provide structured opportunities for operators to observe work, discuss problems, develop ideas, and participate in changes. They can make local knowledge visible to leaders and create a pathway for people to practice improvement thinking. Their effectiveness depends on whether suggestions receive timely consideration, whether feedback is provided, and whether accepted changes are implemented safely and consistently. A suggestion system that collects ideas but does not respond can weaken trust and discourage participation. These mechanisms should connect to the team's daily management system so that ideas are assessed against the process condition, customer needs, quality, safety, and operational priorities.
Study instruction:
- "Examine a suggestion process from submission to decision and feedback. Identify how the organization communicates acceptance, rejection, testing, and implementation."
Key point:
- Quality circles and suggestions are mechanisms for engagement and capability development.
- A functioning system closes the feedback loop and connects ideas to actual process conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
12. Q: How are operators and team leaders encouraged to work together on improvement?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes reciprocal incentives. Team leaders are responsible for encouraging and obtaining improvement suggestions from team members. Operators, in turn, demonstrate initiative and improvement skill that may contribute to their suitability for promotion to team leader. This arrangement makes improvement a shared working relationship rather than a one-way instruction. The team leader provides guidance and operational coordination, while operators contribute detailed knowledge of the work and practical ideas. For the relationship to function, suggestions must be heard respectfully, the team leader must make time for coaching and follow-up, and operators must understand how their ideas fit the process's target condition and standards.
Study instruction:
- "Describe what each party contributes to a real improvement example. Check whether the operator's input influenced the analysis and whether the team leader provided coaching and follow-through."
Key point:
- Team leaders are expected to solicit and develop operator initiative.
- Operator and team-leader development can reinforce one another through collaborative improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
13. Q: What is the team leader's primary role in Toyota's process-improvement system?
A: Show answer
The team leader is the designated first responder to abnormalities at the process and a central owner of the process work standard, which represents its target condition. The team leader stays acquainted with how the process is operating, recognizes deviations from the standard, responds when problems occur, and supports the team's work. The role also includes encouraging operator suggestions and collaborating on improvements in the immediate work area. The team leader does not necessarily launch a full problem-solving exercise for every abnormality; repeated or significant problems may trigger deeper investigation. The role combines operational support, observation, response, and improvement coaching.
Study instruction:
- "Write a role description for a team leader that separates first response, standard ownership, operator support, escalation, and recurring-problem investigation."
Key point:
- A named team leader provides immediate process-level response.
- The team leader uses the work standard as a reference for detecting abnormalities and supporting improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
14. Q: What responsibilities do group leaders have in establishing and managing process target conditions?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's description, group leaders work with team leaders and team members on target conditions in their processes and on understanding and resolving daily production problems. For a new process or product, the group leader typically establishes the first process target condition with a production engineer. Group leaders also participate in translating broader management direction into practical process-level conditions. Their role connects the daily reality of the production team with broader production objectives and technical planning. They should ensure that the target condition is sufficiently clear for the team to work toward and that problems encountered during implementation are surfaced and addressed.
Study instruction:
- "Map the relationship between group leader, team leader, team members, and production engineer during a process launch and subsequent daily improvement."
Key point:
- Group leaders connect process-level work with technical and organizational direction.
- They participate in setting target conditions and resolving daily production problems.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
15. Q: How do production supervisory staff and superintendents contribute to improvement?
A: Show answer
Production supervisory staff and superintendents help organize improvement across processes rather than focusing only on a single operator's task. The chapter describes superintendents as a level where senior management themes, targets, plans, and initiatives begin to be converted into process target conditions. They ensure that local improvement efforts use improvement-kata thinking, fit together into a flowing value stream, align with organizational targets, and serve customer requirements. Their work therefore includes coordination, prioritization, and checking that local process changes do not optimize one area at the expense of the wider system.
Study instruction:
- "Take one organization-wide outcome target and identify the supervisory decisions needed to translate it into coherent process-level target conditions."
Key point:
- Superintendents connect local process improvement to the value stream and organizational direction.
- Coordination prevents isolated local optimization.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
16. Q: What is the difference between manufacturing engineers and production engineers in the chapter's terminology?
A: Show answer
The chapter cautions that Toyota's use of the term manufacturing engineer differs from the way the term may be used in other factories. Toyota manufacturing engineers are responsible for improvement in shop-floor operations. Toyota production engineers, by contrast, are responsible for developing tools, processes, machines, and equipment. The distinction matters because job titles do not always map consistently between organizations. When designing responsibilities, organizations should define the work to be performed rather than copy a title and assume that it has the same scope. Both functions can support improvement, but their contributions are different: one focuses on improving shop-floor operations and the other on developing production technology and process capability.
Study instruction:
- "Compare job descriptions rather than titles. Identify who owns shop-floor improvement and who designs or develops production equipment and processes in your own organization."
Key point:
- Toyota's terminology distinguishes shop-floor improvement engineering from production engineering.
- Clear functional definitions are more reliable than job-title assumptions.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
17. Q: How is the first target condition established for a new process or product?
A: Show answer
For a new process or product, management sets a target cost and a target date for production. The first process target condition, described as a work standard, is typically established by the process's group leader together with a production engineer and then given to the production team. The team includes the team leader and team members. The target condition provides an initial reference for launching production and for identifying the gap between intended and actual performance. It is a starting condition to be achieved and stabilized, not proof that the process will operate perfectly from the first day.
Study instruction:
- "Follow the sequence from management's cost and timing requirements to the initial work standard and the production team's responsibility for achieving it."
Key point:
- The initial process target condition is established by the group leader and production engineer.
- The production team uses it as the starting standard for launch and learning.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
18. Q: Why does a new process need time to achieve its initial target condition?
A: Show answer
At the start of production, people are learning the new work and equipment or installations may experience breakdowns. The chapter notes that achieving the initial target condition can take several weeks. The gap between the intended condition and actual performance is therefore expected to reveal learning needs and process weaknesses. Leaders should not assume that the initial standard will be achieved immediately or respond to every early gap by blaming operators. They should observe the process, support learning, address abnormalities, and use evidence to determine what needs to change. Once regular production stabilizes, further target conditions can be developed for ongoing improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish launch stabilization from later continuous improvement. Identify which gaps arise from normal learning and which indicate a process design or support problem."
Key point:
- A new process may require a stabilization period before regular production is established.
- The initial standard creates a reference for learning and subsequent improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
19. Q: How do target conditions guide daily work after production stabilizes?
A: Show answer
Once regular production stabilizes, further target conditions or standards are developed for ongoing process improvement. Group leaders, team leaders, and team members focus on the target conditions in their processes and on understanding and resolving daily production problems. A target condition gives the team a defined description of the intended way the process should operate. It helps people compare actual conditions with the intended condition, recognize abnormalities, and choose meaningful next steps. Without a clear target condition, teams can become reactive, chase isolated symptoms, or confuse activity with progress.
Study instruction:
- "Review a process standard and identify the intended condition, observable measures, current gaps, and the problems that should receive attention."
Key point:
- Target conditions give daily improvement a concrete direction.
- Standards make deviations visible and support focused problem-solving.

- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
20. Q: How should senior-management themes and targets be translated into process-level target conditions?
A: Show answer
Senior management announces themes, targets, plans, and initiatives, but these broad statements must be translated into conditions that can be pursued at individual processes. The chapter describes this conversion as occurring through mentor-mentee dialogue and generally beginning at the superintendent level. The translation should preserve the intent of the organizational target while defining what must become true in the process. A broad outcome such as improved cost or quality is not yet a usable process target condition. Leaders must determine which process characteristics, work patterns, constraints, and measures need to change. The process-level condition then provides a basis for experiments and learning.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a broad management target and work downward to specify a process condition that can be observed and tested at the point of work."
Key point:
- Organization-wide targets need translation before they can guide local process improvement.
- Mentor-mentee dialogue supports alignment between strategic intent and process reality.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
21. Q: Why should superintendents ensure that process target conditions fit together across a value stream?
A: Show answer
A local process can meet its own target while creating delays, excess inventory, quality problems, or additional work for a downstream process. The chapter therefore assigns superintendents responsibility for ensuring that individual target conditions and improvement efforts fit together into a flowing value stream, match organizational targets, and serve customer requirements. This prevents local optimization from undermining overall performance. Leaders need to understand upstream and downstream dependencies, the timing and quantity of work, and the quality requirements of the next process. A process target condition should improve the contribution of the process to the wider flow, not merely make one department's metrics look better.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one product or service through several connected processes. Identify where a local improvement could shift cost, delay, or defects to another process."
Key point:
- Process-level improvement must support value-stream performance.
- Customer requirements and downstream effects should constrain local target conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
22. Q: What is a process abnormality, and why should it receive an immediate response?
A: Show answer
A process abnormality is a condition in which actual work deviates from the expected process condition or standard. Examples in the chapter include quality problems, work delays, equipment issues, and failures to achieve the expected production pace. The chapter argues that response should be immediate because the circumstances are easiest to observe while the event is happening, small problems can accumulate, and a timely adjustment may still allow the day's target to be achieved. Immediate response also makes the organization's stated commitment to quality credible. In a tightly coupled value stream, a problem in one process can quickly affect other processes, so waiting for a later summary may allow the impact to spread.
Study instruction:
- "Define the abnormality signals for a process and specify who acknowledges each signal, what immediate containment is required, and when escalation occurs."
Key point:
- Abnormalities should be made visible and addressed promptly.
- Fast response protects learning opportunities, quality, and flow.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
23. Q: Why is the cause of an abnormality easier to investigate while the event is still happening?
A: Show answer
During or immediately after an abnormality, the relevant conditions are still observable: the equipment state, material, work sequence, timing, operator actions, and surrounding circumstances can be examined before they change. If investigation is postponed, the process may return to normal, the evidence may disappear, and people's recollections may become less precise. The chapter describes this as keeping the root-cause trail hot. Immediate observation does not mean jumping to a conclusion or changing several variables at once. It means capturing the facts while they are available, preserving relevant evidence, and deciding whether the issue requires deeper problem-solving.
Study instruction:
- "Practice recording an abnormality at the point of occurrence. Capture time, location, process state, observed deviation, containment, and what remains unknown."
Key point:
- Timely observation preserves information that may be lost later.
- A hot trail improves the quality of subsequent investigation but does not replace disciplined analysis.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
24. Q: Why can delaying a response allow small problems to become large and complicated?
A: Show answer
An unresolved abnormality can recur, combine with other small problems, affect downstream operations, or become harder to isolate as the process continues. Delay may also permit defective output to move forward or make the original conditions difficult to reconstruct. In a closely linked production flow, a local disruption can create shortages, waiting, rework, or missed production targets elsewhere. Immediate response helps contain the problem and preserve the opportunity to learn before secondary effects accumulate. It does not guarantee that every issue will be solved immediately, but it reduces the risk of allowing a known deviation to continue without attention.
Study instruction:
- "Select a recurring minor problem and map how it could affect quality, output, downstream work, and customer delivery if left unaddressed."
Key point:
- Small abnormalities can accumulate into larger operational problems.
- Prompt containment reduces the time and distance over which a problem can spread.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
25. Q: What are the limitations of relying on Pareto charts as the first method for responding to process problems?
A: Show answer
Pareto charts summarize recorded problems and help identify categories that occur frequently or have substantial impact. The chapter warns that they are often too late to serve as the first response mechanism. By the time a problem rises to the top of a chart, it may already have caused considerable damage, become more complicated, and lost the immediate evidence needed to understand what happened. A Pareto chart also aggregates events, which can obscure differences among individual occurrences. The category called “other” may contain many smaller problems that are not being examined. The limitation is therefore not that Pareto analysis is inherently wrong, but that retrospective aggregation cannot replace immediate observation and response at the process.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the information available at the moment an abnormality occurs with the information available in a later Pareto report. Identify what evidence may have disappeared."
Key point:
- Pareto analysis is retrospective and should not replace immediate process response.
- Aggregated categories can conceal specific causes and smaller recurring issues.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
26. Q: When are Pareto charts useful in a process-improvement system?
A: Show answer
Pareto charts can be useful for analyzing accumulated data, identifying patterns across many events, prioritizing improvement themes, and communicating the relative frequency or impact of problem categories. They are most useful when the underlying records are reliable and when the chart is used as an additional analytical view rather than as the sole mechanism for detecting abnormalities. Teams should investigate the actual process conditions behind the categories selected for attention. The chart can help decide where to allocate deeper analysis, but it cannot explain the cause of a specific event by itself. Immediate response and later aggregate analysis therefore serve different purposes and should complement each other.
Study instruction:
- "Build a Pareto chart from a defined period of verified problem records, then trace the leading category back to actual process observations and individual cases."
Key point:
- Pareto charts support prioritization and trend analysis.
- They complement, rather than replace, observation and investigation at the process.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
27. Q: Why should the first response to a process abnormality normally come from someone other than the production operator?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that operators in a tightly loaded process must resume production once an immediate issue is addressed, while the best opportunity to understand the event may exist at that same moment. If operators are expected both to restore output and investigate causes, the system creates conflicting duties. Toyota's described arrangement places a team leader at the process as the designated first responder. The team leader can attend to the abnormality, help restore the process, and preserve observations while operators perform their assigned work or follow safe stop procedures. This is not a rule that operators should remain passive; they should report and explain what they observed. It is a design choice that provides a specific response role.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a responsibility matrix for operator, team leader, group leader, and technical support during an abnormality. Check that production duties do not prevent response and learning."
Key point:
- The team leader is the designated first responder in the chapter's model.
- Operators remain essential sources of information and participants in improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
28. Q: Does every response by a team leader require a full problem-solving project?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter states that team leaders respond to every abnormality, but each response does not automatically trigger a full problem-solving activity. A response may consist of acknowledging the signal, checking the condition, containing the immediate issue, restoring the process when appropriate, recording what happened, and monitoring for recurrence. Deeper problem-solving is usually initiated for repeating problems or issues that warrant structured investigation. This distinction prevents the organization from treating every small deviation as a large project while still ensuring that no abnormality is ignored. The response system should define criteria for escalation, such as recurrence, severity, safety implications, customer risk, or inability to restore the process.
Study instruction:
- "Create escalation criteria that distinguish routine first response, repeated-problem analysis, and urgent safety or quality escalation."
Key point:
- Every abnormality needs an appropriate response, but not every event needs a full improvement project.
- Recurrence and severity help determine when deeper problem-solving is warranted.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
29. Q: How does the team leader use the work standard or target condition to identify abnormalities?
A: Show answer
The team leader owns the process work standard, which represents the target condition, and uses it as a reference for understanding how the process should operate. By knowing the expected sequence, timing, quality requirements, and other relevant conditions, the team leader can recognize when actual work departs from the intended state. Without a clear standard, an observation may be interpreted inconsistently because there is no shared reference for what normal work should look like. The standard is not merely a document to enforce; it is a practical baseline for seeing the process, identifying gaps, and deciding what should be examined.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the written or visual standard with actual work at the process. Identify which deviations are visible, measurable, and actionable."
Key point:
- The target condition provides the reference for recognizing abnormalities.
- A standard is useful when leaders know the real work and observe it at the process.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
30. Q: What is the difference between monitoring a process and policing its failures?
A: Show answer
Monitoring means staying familiar with how the process is actually working so that deviations can be recognized and support can be provided quickly. Policing treats observation primarily as a search for mistakes or individual fault. The chapter explicitly describes the team leader's purpose as becoming acquainted with the process, not policing its failings. A monitoring leader asks what condition is occurring, what has changed, what obstacle is present, and what support is needed. A policing approach encourages concealment, defensive reporting, or superficial compliance. Effective monitoring is fact-based and directed toward process stability, learning, and improvement while still maintaining clear standards and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a process and record the questions asked by the leader. Determine whether they focus on process conditions and learning or on assigning blame."
Key point:
- Monitoring is intended to understand and support the process.
- Blame-oriented policing can discourage transparency and hide abnormalities.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
31. Q: What does the one-piece-flow cell example reveal about the conflict between production and investigation?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's example, a cell has a counter showing actual output and another showing the target quantity as takt-time intervals pass. When an operator encounters a problem, the cell stops. The operators attempt to fix the issue so that production can resume, while the target counter continues to advance. Once the issue is cleared, they naturally try to catch up. The moment the problem occurred was the best time to understand why it happened, but the operators are pulled back into production. The example shows that a process can expose an abnormality without having enough role capacity to investigate it. A designated team leader provides that missing response capacity.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the example using a timeline: abnormality, line stop, immediate fix, target-counter movement, restart, and lost investigation opportunity."
Key point:
- A visible abnormality does not guarantee that anyone has time to investigate it.
- A designated response role resolves the conflict between production recovery and process learning.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
32. Q: Why is it often fruitless to investigate a production problem long after operators have resumed work?
A: Show answer
Once production resumes, the physical and operational conditions that produced the abnormality may have changed. The equipment may have been reset, the material moved, the work sequence resumed, and the people involved returned to normal duties. Later interviews and summaries may preserve some information, but they are less reliable than direct observation at the time. The chapter argues that the root-cause trail becomes cold and that the organization loses a learning opportunity. A later investigation may still be necessary for serious or recurring issues, but it should use evidence captured during the immediate response whenever possible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which facts should be captured before the process is reset or restarted. Establish a simple record that preserves evidence without delaying necessary safety or containment actions."
Key point:
- The original process conditions may disappear after recovery.
- Immediate evidence capture improves later analysis and corrective action.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
33. Q: How should an organization balance restoring production with understanding the cause of a problem?
A: Show answer
The organization should separate immediate containment and recovery from deeper causal analysis while ensuring that the first response preserves useful evidence. The team leader or designated responder can assess the abnormality, protect people and product, capture key observations, and decide whether production can safely resume. Operators should not be expected to abandon production responsibilities for an extended investigation when the process design does not provide that capacity. If the problem repeats or presents serious risk, a structured investigation should follow with the appropriate leaders and technical support. The balance is not speed at any cost; safety, quality, and controlled recovery take priority, while learning is deliberately protected.
Study instruction:
- "Design a response sequence with separate steps for safety, containment, evidence capture, restart authorization, escalation, and deeper analysis."
Key point:
- Recovery and investigation are related but distinct activities.
- A sound response protects safety and quality while preserving the opportunity to learn.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
34. Q: Why does Toyota's use of team leaders support staffing a process with the correct number of operators?
A: Show answer
A fast-response system gives the process access to help when abnormalities occur, reducing the perceived need to add extra operators as a buffer against every disruption. The chapter argues that extra operators can conceal problems by absorbing interruptions, workarounds, or unevenness rather than exposing the actual process condition. With a designated team leader available to respond, the process can be staffed with the number of operators required by the work design while abnormalities remain visible and manageable. This does not mean that staffing should be cut without analysis. The staffing level must be safe and realistic, and the response system must be capable of handling the problems that the process exposes.
Study instruction:
- "Examine whether excess staffing is compensating for recurring process problems. Verify that safety, workload, takt, and response capacity are understood before proposing staffing changes."
Key point:
- Correct staffing can expose problems that excess capacity might conceal.
- Lean staffing depends on a reliable support and response system, not on indiscriminate labor reduction.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
35. Q: Why can an additional indirect role such as a team leader be an enabler of lean operations?
A: Show answer
An additional support role may appear inconsistent with a narrow interpretation of lean staffing, but the chapter argues that the team leader enables immediate response and improvement at the process. The role helps the organization handle more abnormalities, preserve root-cause evidence, and learn from problems rather than allowing them to remain hidden. The team leader's presence can also permit the process to operate with the correct number of production operators instead of adding operators to absorb disruptions. The relevant question is therefore not whether a position is direct or indirect labor in isolation. It is whether the role improves the total system's ability to deliver quality, flow, productivity, and learning with less waste.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a support role by its contribution to process performance, problem visibility, and improvement capacity rather than by its classification as indirect labor alone."
Key point:
- Lean should be evaluated at the system level, not by counting roles in isolation.
- A response role can enable better flow and fewer hidden problems.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
36. Q: How can a fast-response system contribute to productivity improvement over time?
A: Show answer
Fast response limits the time abnormalities remain uncontrolled and makes it possible to learn while conditions are visible. Repeated problems can then be investigated and removed through improvement activity. As the process becomes more capable and stable, it may require fewer workarounds and less compensating labor. The chapter describes a reinforcing sequence: designated response enables correct operator staffing; visible problems create opportunities for learning; improvement raises productivity; and improved productivity can reduce the number of operators needed. This is a long-term system effect, not an immediate promise that adding a team leader will automatically reduce labor or increase output.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the cause-and-effect chain from response capacity to problem visibility, learning, process improvement, and staffing needs. Identify measures that would test each link."
Key point:
- Response capacity helps expose and address process weaknesses.
- Productivity improvement is cumulative and depends on solving problems, not merely adding a role.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
37. Q: What risks arise from cutting indirect labor or flattening the organization chart without examining process needs?
A: Show answer
Simple directives to reduce indirect labor or remove management layers can eliminate the people who respond to abnormalities, coach operators, maintain standards, and coordinate improvement. If those responsibilities are not reassigned with adequate time and authority, alerts may go unanswered, root-cause evidence may be lost, and operators may be forced to absorb conflicting duties. Extra operators may then be added to compensate for disruptions, or problems may be hidden by workarounds. The chapter warns that apparently efficient structural changes can produce a static system that is less able to improve. Organizational design should therefore be evaluated by its effect on response, learning, quality, and total productivity rather than by headcount alone.
Study instruction:
- "Before proposing a staffing reduction, list the response, coaching, coordination, and technical functions that would be affected and show how each would continue to be performed."
Key point:
- Reducing support roles without preserving their functions can weaken the improvement system.
- Headcount reduction is not equivalent to system-wide productivity improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
38. Q: How does the improvement system help avoid hidden problems and workarounds?
A: Show answer
When a process has no reliable responder, operators may adapt locally to keep output moving. Extra staffing can also absorb interruptions and make unstable work appear acceptable. These workarounds can hide the true frequency and causes of abnormalities, preventing the organization from learning. A team leader who monitors the process against its standard and responds to deviations helps make the actual condition visible. Problems can then be contained and, when recurring, investigated. The purpose is not to eliminate every buffer regardless of risk; it is to understand whether buffers are necessary and whether they are concealing avoidable process weakness.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a workaround in a process and determine what abnormality it masks, who knows about it, and what evidence would reveal its true operational impact."
Key point:
- Workarounds can keep output moving while concealing instability.
- Visible problems are a prerequisite for deliberate improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
39. Q: How should an organization distinguish a suggestion from an operationally owned improvement?
A: Show answer
A suggestion is an idea or observation offered by an employee; it may be valuable but is not yet a verified process change. An operationally owned improvement has an accountable process owner, a defined problem or target condition, a test or implementation plan, appropriate review, and follow-up to determine whether the change works. The chapter's distinction between voluntary operator activity and organized improvement shows why collecting ideas is not enough. Leaders must evaluate suggestions, involve the people who do the work, coordinate technical or safety requirements, and ensure that changes are integrated into the standard where appropriate. This prevents an idea log from being mistaken for a functioning improvement system.
Study instruction:
- "Follow one suggestion from submission through assessment, testing, approval, standardization, and follow-up. Identify where ownership changes or remains with the process leader."
Key point:
- Ideas become operational improvements only through disciplined evaluation and follow-through.
- The process owner remains accountable for the resulting condition and standard.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
40. Q: What should happen when an andon signal is activated but no one responds?
A: Show answer
The organization should treat the absence of response as a system failure, not simply as an operator's failure to call for help. The first step is to protect people and product according to the applicable safety and quality procedures. The team should then determine whether the signal was visible, whether the designated responder was available, whether the response expectation was clear, and whether escalation worked. If no one is explicitly assigned, responsibility should be assigned by process and shift, with a backup for absence or competing demands. The event should be reviewed to prevent recurrence. An alerting device is effective only when it reliably connects an abnormality to a capable person who can act.
Study instruction:
- "Run a tabletop test of an unanswered andon signal. Verify primary and backup ownership, response expectations, escalation, and the record of disposition."
Key point:
- Andon is a call for attention to an abnormality, not a substitute for response ownership.
- The system must connect a visible signal to a named and available responder.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
41. Q: What should a team do when the same abnormality occurs repeatedly?
A: Show answer
The team leader should respond to each occurrence and ensure that the immediate condition is contained. Recurrence is a reason to consider deeper problem-solving because it suggests that the underlying cause has not been removed or that the process remains vulnerable. The team should review the captured observations, compare the actual condition with the work standard, identify patterns, and involve the group leader or relevant engineering support when needed. The analysis should distinguish a repeated symptom from its cause and test whether proposed changes prevent recurrence without creating new problems. Once a change is effective, the process standard and training may need to be updated.
Study instruction:
- "Use a recurring-problem log to connect each event to its immediate response, observed conditions, suspected causes, test, result, and standard update."
Key point:
- Repeated abnormalities warrant more than repeated temporary fixes.
- A verified improvement should reduce recurrence and be reflected in the way work is performed.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
42. Q: How can the effectiveness of process-improvement responsibility be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should examine whether abnormalities are acknowledged promptly, whether first responders are available, whether recurring problems are investigated, and whether effective changes improve the process. Useful evidence includes response records, recurrence rates, quality defects, delays, downtime, attainment of process target conditions, and the quality of operator participation. Measures should be interpreted together: a high number of reported abnormalities may reflect improved visibility rather than deteriorating performance. Likewise, fast closure is not evidence of a good response if the cause remains and the issue recurs. Leaders should combine process data with direct observation and review whether the responsibility structure supports learning and customer outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Create a small review dashboard that combines response, recurrence, process performance, and improvement follow-through. Explain what each measure can and cannot demonstrate."
Key point:
- Measure both the quality of the response system and the resulting process performance.
- Activity counts alone do not prove that the process is improving.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
43. Q: What is the relationship between immediate abnormality response and Toyota's jidoka principle?
A: Show answer
Toyota describes jidoka as building quality into the process by detecting abnormalities and stopping equipment or work when necessary so that defects do not continue downstream. The chapter's team-leader response arrangement complements this principle by ensuring that a person is available to attend to a problem when it is signaled. Jidoka is not simply a lamp or stop cord; the signal must lead to appropriate action, investigation where needed, and prevention of recurrence. The precise response sequence depends on the process and its safety and quality design, but the underlying logic is to make abnormalities visible and prevent defective work from flowing forward.
Study instruction:
- "Review Toyota's explanation of jidoka and map how detection, signaling, human response, containment, and recurrence prevention connect in a process."
Key point:
- Jidoka combines abnormality detection with action to protect quality.
- A response system gives practical meaning to the signal generated by the process.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
44. Q: How does Just-in-Time make rapid response important across connected processes?
A: Show answer
Just-in-Time links processes in a coordinated flow, producing what is needed, when it is needed, and in the amount needed. Because processes are closely connected, a disruption in one area can quickly affect the next process through delay, shortage, quality problems, or interrupted flow. The chapter uses this coupling to explain why abnormalities should not be left unattended. Rapid response limits the time a problem can propagate and helps the organization maintain reliable flow. It also makes the downstream customer relationship concrete: each process must provide the next process with acceptable output at the required time and quantity.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical abnormality through at least two downstream processes. Identify the first point at which rapid containment prevents wider disruption."
Key point:
- Tightly connected flow increases the importance of prompt problem response.
- Local abnormalities can create downstream consequences beyond the process where they began.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
45. Q: How should a company adapt the chapter's model if it does not have Toyota's exact organizational structure?
A: Show answer
It should adopt the operating principles rather than copy titles, reporting layers, or staffing ratios. The essential design requirements are clear process ownership, a named first responder, sufficient capacity to respond without creating incompatible production duties, standards that make abnormalities visible, operator participation, and access to technical or supervisory support for recurring problems. A smaller organization may assign these responsibilities differently, but it must still specify who acts, when, and how escalation occurs. The design should be tested in the actual work environment and adjusted based on observed response gaps, safety requirements, workload, and process performance.
Study instruction:
- "Translate each Toyota role into the functions performed in your organization. Confirm that every process and shift has a clear owner and a practical backup."
Key point:
- Copy the management logic, not necessarily Toyota's job titles or staffing structure.
- Local adaptation must preserve accountability, response capacity, and operator engagement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
46. Q: What minimum elements should a daily process-abnormality response system define?
A: Show answer
A practical system should define the process standard, abnormality signals, primary responder, backup responder, immediate containment actions, decision authority for stopping or restarting work, evidence to capture, escalation criteria, and method for tracking recurring issues. It should also specify how operators notify the responder and how the team receives feedback after an event. Safety and quality requirements take precedence over output recovery. The system should be simple enough to use during actual work, but complete enough to prevent alerts from being ignored or closed without understanding. It should be reviewed through observation at the process rather than relying only on written procedures.
Study instruction:
- "Audit one process against this list. Test the response in a controlled exercise and identify any ambiguity in ownership, authority, or handoff."
Key point:
- A response system requires more than an alert device.
- Clear roles, safe containment, evidence capture, and escalation make the system actionable.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
47. Q: How can leaders avoid treating improvement as an occasional project rather than part of daily management?
A: Show answer
Leaders should connect target conditions, standards, abnormality response, operator involvement, and recurring-problem analysis to the normal rhythm of running the process. The chapter describes improvement as embedded in every work process and notes that team leaders and supervisory staff devote substantial time to process improvement because it is part of how they manage. If improvement is left to occasional workshops or a central project team, the organization may improve selected areas while daily abnormalities remain unmanaged elsewhere. Daily management makes improvement continuous by ensuring that people see the current condition, respond to deviations, and learn from repeated obstacles.
Study instruction:
- "Review a team's daily management routine and identify where process observation, abnormality response, operator suggestions, and target-condition review actually occur."
Key point:
- Improvement is sustained when it is integrated into daily work.
- Projects and workshops can help, but they cannot replace everyday process ownership.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
48. Q: What is the danger of judging a factory's improvement system only by its number of suggestions or kaizen events?
A: Show answer
Suggestion counts and event counts measure activity, not necessarily the quality or effect of improvement. A factory may collect many ideas while failing to address recurring abnormalities, or run many workshops without changing the process condition. Conversely, a smaller number of well-selected improvements may produce significant and sustained benefits. The chapter's distinction between voluntary activity and organized improvement also shows that direct idea counts do not capture all the improvement work performed by leaders and engineers as part of their jobs. Evaluation should therefore include process outcomes, capability development, response quality, and whether changes are sustained in the standard way of working.
Study instruction:
- "Compare activity measures with outcome measures for one improvement area. Explain how each measure could be misleading when used alone."
Key point:
- Activity counts do not show whether process conditions improved.
- A balanced evaluation includes process results, learning, and sustained changes.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
49. Q: How can a manager investigate whether excess staffing is concealing process problems?
A: Show answer
The manager should observe the work directly and compare actual staffing with the process requirements, takt, workload balance, and standard work. The review should identify whether additional people are compensating for recurring equipment stops, uneven work, material shortages, quality defects, or unclear methods. It should also examine whether operators are using workarounds that keep output moving while concealing the underlying issue. Any staffing change must be assessed for safety, ergonomic demands, realistic workload, and response capacity. The chapter's argument is not to remove people indiscriminately; it is to avoid using excess staffing as a substitute for a process that can expose and address abnormalities.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a process walk and document the purpose of each person's work. Identify which activities add value, provide necessary support, or compensate for an unresolved process weakness."
Key point:
- Excess staffing may hide instability, but staffing decisions must remain safe and evidence-based.
- The aim is to improve the process rather than simply reduce the number of people.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
50. Q: What should be considered before removing a team-leader or supervisory position?
A: Show answer
Before removing a role, management should identify the functions currently performed by that person: first response to abnormalities, standard ownership, operator coaching, coordination with group leaders, escalation, and support for improvement. It should determine who will perform each function, whether that person has capacity and authority, and whether response coverage will remain available on every shift. It should also examine likely effects on problem visibility, quality, recurrence, production staffing, and learning. If these functions disappear or become informal, the organization may reduce visible overhead while increasing hidden operating costs and risk.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a function-by-function impact assessment before any structural change. Require evidence that every critical response and improvement responsibility has a new owner."
Key point:
- A role should not be removed without preserving its essential functions.
- Structural efficiency must be tested against operational performance and learning capacity.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
51. Q: How does operator promotion relate to improvement capability in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter states that an operator's promotion to team leader is determined in part by the improvement initiative and skill the operator demonstrates. This signals that Toyota values the ability to recognize problems, contribute ideas, and help improve work as part of leadership development. It does not mean that promotion is based on suggestion counts alone or that every strong operator should become a team leader. The relevant capability is demonstrated improvement thinking and the ability to work constructively with others. The chapter presents voluntary improvement activity as one way to develop and identify that capability.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the behaviors that demonstrate improvement capability, such as careful observation, clear problem definition, testing, learning, and collaboration. Distinguish these from simply proposing many ideas."
Key point:
- Improvement initiative is one factor in identifying potential team leaders.
- Capability is demonstrated through useful problem-solving behavior, not activity volume alone.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
52. Q: What does the chapter teach about the relationship between cost reduction, quality, and productivity?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents process improvement as a way to improve productivity and quality while reducing cost. These outcomes should not be treated as independent targets that can be optimized separately. A cost reduction that creates defects, unsafe work, excessive strain, or downstream delays is not a sound improvement. Likewise, a productivity gain that depends on hidden rework or overburdened operators may not represent a better process. Toyota's official description of the Production System links waste elimination, high quality, low cost, and faster delivery to customers. The chapter's role structure supports these aims by ensuring that people with process responsibility continuously respond to problems and improve the work.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed improvement against quality, safety, productivity, cost, and downstream customer effects rather than a single local metric."
Key point:
- Improvement should strengthen the overall process, not shift waste or risk elsewhere.
- Quality, productivity, cost, and customer delivery must be considered together.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview
53. Q: What are the most important lessons to remember from Chapter 7?
A: Show answer
Chapter 7 argues that process improvement at Toyota is neither left solely to autonomous operator teams, left to chance, nor delegated to a separate continuous-improvement group. It is embedded in daily management and carried primarily by team leaders, group leaders, supervisory staff, and engineers, with operators actively involved under guidance. Target conditions connect management direction to process work, while the team leader provides immediate response to abnormalities and owns the work standard used to recognize deviations. Immediate response preserves evidence and limits the spread of problems; deeper problem-solving is generally reserved for recurring or significant issues. The chapter also warns that removing support roles or adding excess operators without understanding the process can hide problems and weaken learning. The central lesson is that continuous improvement requires both engaged people and an organizational system that gives clear responsibility, time, and support.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the chapter's operating model from roles and target conditions through abnormality response, deeper problem-solving, and sustained productivity improvement. Explain why each element depends on the others."
Key point:
- Improvement must be embedded in daily work with clear process-level ownership.
- Operator engagement, leader response, and system-level coordination work together to create continuous improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Who Carries Out Process Improvement at Toyota?
- Koichi Shimizu, “Reorienting Kaizen Activities at Toyota” (2004)
- Toyota Production System: Official Toyota Overview

Table 8
Coaching Kata: Developing Problem-Solvers Through Mentoring and A3 Practice
1. Q: If you were Tina what would you do next? (After Paul reports an 8 percent increase in trim-line scrap cost over two months and sets a 30-day recovery target.)
A: Show answer
Start by establishing the facts for Tina’s own area rather than issuing a general instruction to reduce scrap. Review the recent scrap records and cost reports, compare them with the earlier period and the required baseline, and identify which parts, processes, and defect types account for the largest increase. Check that the data uses consistent definitions and time periods. Then select one clearly defined priority problem for investigation. The 30-day target gives urgency and direction, but it does not identify the cause or justify a broad set of countermeasures. Tina should create a focused starting point that can be investigated at the actual process.
Study instruction:
- Practice turning an overall performance gap into a ranked list of specific, evidence-based problem areas before selecting one focus.
Key point:
- Begin with the actual performance gap and reliable data, not assumptions or action lists.
- Narrow the initial focus so cause and effect can be understood.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
2. Q: In what step of Practical Problem Solving is Tina? (After ranking the group’s scrap records and selecting the side panel interior trim piece as the priority.)
A: Show answer
Tina is in Step 1: Pick up the problem. She has used the records to identify where the group’s scrap frequency and cost are concentrated and has chosen a priority part for attention. She has not yet established the detailed conditions that create the defect, located the point of cause, or tested a causal explanation. Selecting a problem is a disciplined narrowing decision, not a diagnosis. The next work is to understand how the selected problem appears in the actual process and how the team leader understands it.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish problem selection from situation analysis and cause investigation; identify what evidence is sufficient for each stage.
Key point:
- Step 1 establishes which problem deserves focused attention.
- A priority problem is not yet a proven root cause.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
3. Q: What mentoring/coaching behaviors is Tina using? (When she ranks scrap by frequency and cost and focuses on Dan’s side panel trim process.)
A: Show answer
Tina uses a “show me” orientation: she prefers records, observable conditions, and direct evidence to unsupported opinions. She also needs to see the situation herself rather than relying entirely on summaries from the five team leaders. Her decision to focus on the largest contributor is a single-focus discipline. If she changed several processes at once, the resulting variables would make it difficult to see which change affected scrap. The purpose is not simply to reduce a number quickly; it is to help the team learn how its work system produces the result. Tina should therefore stay close to the process while coaching the team leader to investigate it.
Study instruction:
- Review a real performance report and identify the facts that justify selecting one priority process without assuming that the report explains the cause.
Key point:
- Facts and direct observation are stronger than opinion alone.
- One focused investigation preserves the ability to learn cause and effect.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
4. Q: If you were Tina what would you do next? (After selecting the side panel trim as the priority and before Dan has described what he knows about the process.)
A: Show answer
Tina should meet Dan and ask him to describe the current situation, the observed scrap pattern, and what he has noticed at the process. The purpose of this first dialogue is to reveal Dan’s current understanding and problem-solving habits, not to give him a checklist of actions. Tina should listen for concrete observations, missing information, assumptions, and whether he has recognized the abnormal condition. She should avoid simply telling him to observe the process and report back, because that would conceal how he approaches the problem and leave her without a shared firsthand understanding.
Study instruction:
- Prepare open, neutral questions that reveal a learner’s current understanding before prescribing the next learning activity.
Key point:
- First assess how the mentee is thinking.
- Coaching should respond to the learner’s actual capability and the actual situation.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
5. Q: In what step of Practical Problem Solving are Tina and Dan? (After Dan reports higher scrap, operator complaints about harder installation, and more andon calls, but cannot explain what is happening.)
A: Show answer
They are at the threshold of Step 2: Grasp the Situation. Dan has offered useful signals, but they are not yet a sufficiently precise description of the condition or the mechanism that produces the scrap. Increased andon calls and operator comments are clues that guide observation; they are not proof of a supplier defect, an operator error, or any other cause. Tina should help Dan move from impressions to direct evidence at the place where the work and defect occur.
Study instruction:
- Classify each piece of information in a problem case as an observed fact, reported perception, hypothesis, or confirmed cause.
Key point:
- Early clues guide investigation but do not establish causation.
- Step 2 requires a detailed picture of what is actually happening.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
6. Q: What mentoring/coaching behaviors is Tina using? (When Dan describes the problem vaguely and Tina refrains from telling him what to do.)
A: Show answer
Tina leads with questions because her responsibility is to develop Dan as a problem solver, not to solve the scrap problem on his behalf. His response shows her what he notices, what he assumes, and what he does not yet understand. A useful question is genuine inquiry into his thinking; it is not a disguised attempt to steer him toward a solution Tina has already selected. The mentor may know the improvement routine and the standards for good investigation, but should remain open about what the specific cause and countermeasure will prove to be.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite leading questions that contain a preferred answer as neutral questions that reveal the learner’s reasoning and evidence.
Key point:
- Questions are diagnostic coaching tools, not persuasion devices.
- The mentee must develop the analysis and solution through guided practice.

7. Q: If you were Tina what would you do next? (After Dan reports the warning signs but has not yet grasped the defect condition.)
A: Show answer
Tina should propose that she and Dan go together to the scrap area and examine the actual rejected parts. At the parts, she can ask Dan to describe what he sees and compare his observations with the defect records. If his experience is limited, Tina should not send him away to investigate alone and then rely on his report. Being present allows her to see the condition herself, evaluate his observations, and choose the next coaching step. The purpose of the visit is to learn what is happening, not to interview operators for a ready-made explanation.
Study instruction:
- Plan a short gemba observation that identifies the object, process location, evidence to inspect, and questions to ask while observing.
Key point:
- Go and see the condition with the mentee when the mentee needs close guidance.
- Firsthand observation protects the quality of both problem analysis and coaching.
