8. Q: What mentoring/coaching behaviors is Tina using? (When she accompanies Dan to inspect scrap and redirects him after he proposes contacting Incoming Inspection.)
A: Show answer
Tina is appropriately directive about the next investigative step without dictating the eventual solution. She has heard Dan’s proposal and then takes him to inspect the physical evidence more carefully. This is not a guessing game in which the mentee must infer a hidden answer from the mentor. It is guided learning: the mentor chooses a useful next step based on the learner’s response and what the mentor can see firsthand. Tina also models observation rather than relying on interviews as a substitute for understanding the process. Conversations with operators may later add context, but they do not replace seeing the defect and work sequence directly.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish a directive about the next method of investigation from a directive that prematurely specifies the solution.
Key point:
- A coach can guide the next step while leaving the solution with the learner.

Incoming Inspection should be involved when the evidence indicates a possible incoming-material issue, not simply because the first visible defect looks like a part-quality problem.
Study instruction:
- Practice responding to an early causal hypothesis by identifying the next observation that could confirm, reject, or refine it.
Key point:
- Do not escalate a hypothesis as though it were an established cause.

20. Q: In what step of Practical Problem Solving are Tina and Dan? (After the small trial shows that keeping the driver running while locating the nut causes stripped threads, and they identify a missing instruction in the standard.)
A: Show answer
They are in Step 4: Develop and Test Countermeasure. The trial provides evidence that the nut must be located on the stud with the driver off before the driver is activated. They have also checked the work standard and found that it does not specify this sequence. The proposed response can now address the demonstrated mechanism: revise the standard, explain the correct sequence, and verify that the work is followed. The change should be checked in practice rather than assumed effective because it has been announced.
Study instruction:
- Trace a countermeasure back to the verified cause and identify the test evidence and standard change that support it.
Key point:
- Step 4 turns a tested cause explanation into a controlled process change.
- The standard must reflect the method that prevents recurrence.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Toyota Indonesia: Toyota Production System
21. Q: What mentoring/coaching behaviors is Tina using? (When she asks Dan to conduct small-scale tests and revise the work standard based on the findings.)
A: Show answer
Tina reinforces two disciplines. First, test on a small scale before making a broad change, so the team can see whether the proposed method produces the expected result. Second, return to the work standard and make sure the new learning is captured in the prescribed process. The experiment is not simply a demonstration; it creates evidence that supports a change in the work method. Tina also extends learning beyond Dan’s team by asking him to share findings and examine other processes that may have the same vulnerability. This is controlled learning, not automatic rollout of an unverified solution.
Study instruction:
- Design a small test with a stated prediction, limited scope, observable result, and decision rule for the next action.
Key point:
- Test the proposed method before wider adoption.
- Update the standard and share verified learning so it can be sustained and transferred.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
22. Q: If you were Tina what would you do next? (After the test supports the driver-off alignment method and Dan proposes explaining it to the team.)
A: Show answer
Accept the evidence-based countermeasure and ensure the work standard is revised to state the sequence clearly: locate the nut on the stud with the driver off, then activate the driver to tighten it. Have Dan explain the finding to the affected team members and verify their work by direct observation. Extend the check to the other shift and review similar processes where the same mechanism could occur. Record the expected result and track scrap after implementation. The goal is not to add a device automatically, but to ensure the chosen control is adequate for the process and is consistently followed.
Study instruction:
- Build an implementation and verification plan that identifies the revised standard, people to brief, shifts to check, and performance measure to monitor.
Key point:
- Convert verified learning into a clear standard and observable practice.
- Adoption must be checked across shifts and related processes.
Source links:
- Toyota Indonesia: Toyota Production System
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why the A3 Process Involves More than Filling in Boxes
23. Q: Why did Tina insist on three months of follow-up tracking on the trim scrap?
A: Show answer
The three-month tracking period provides evidence that the corrective action has been adopted and that assembly-damaged side panel trim has not recurred. A short-term improvement immediately after a meeting could reflect temporary attention rather than elimination of the cause. Sustained monitoring tests whether the revised work method remains in use across time and shifts and whether the defect returns under normal operating conditions. Dan’s direct observation of operators complements the scrap data: the records show the outcome, while observation checks whether the process follows the revised standard. Follow-up therefore tests both effectiveness and adherence.
Study instruction:
- Design a follow-up check that combines outcome measures, direct process observation, time period, shift coverage, and a response if recurrence occurs.
Key point:
- A countermeasure is not proven sustainable by an immediate improvement alone.
- Verify both process adoption and defect performance over time.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: Avoiding Failure in Problem-Solving Projects
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
24. Q: How Did You Feel as You Read Through the Case?
A: Show answer
A reader may feel impatient because Tina repeatedly sends Dan back to observe, clarify, and test before allowing a countermeasure. That impatience exposes a common management habit: equating progress with visible action. In this case, the repeated investigation is productive work because it develops a reliable understanding of the process and Dan’s problem-solving capability. Moving quickly to training or a broad instruction could have changed attention without correcting the driver sequence. The case invites readers to notice their own urge to solve before the situation is sufficiently understood and to recognize that careful observation can reduce total problem-solving time by preventing ineffective action.
Study instruction:
- Note where you felt tempted to jump to a solution and identify what additional fact or observation was still missing at that point.
Key point:
- Apparent slowness can be disciplined learning rather than delay.
- The quality of understanding determines the quality of the countermeasure.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Start the A3 Problem-Solving Process
25. Q: How Long Do You Think the Story in the Case Took?
A: Show answer
The chapter says the author does not know the actual elapsed time, but suggests that most of the work probably occurred within one shift. The important lesson is not a guaranteed one-shift duration; it is the short turnaround between observations, coaching reviews, and next steps. If the mentor waits for a weekly meeting, the process may change and pressure to act may cause the team to skip investigation. Coaching cycles should be frequent and short, with the next step reviewed as soon as practical, often within minutes or hours. The time required will depend on the real process and evidence available.
Study instruction:
- Map a current problem-solving cycle and identify avoidable waiting time between observation, next step, test, and review.
Key point:
- The case’s one-shift duration is an informed estimate, not a documented fact.
- Rapid learning cycles depend on timely checks, not lengthy status meetings.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
26. Q: What Would Have Happened If Tina Had Stayed in Her Office Instead of Going to Observe the Process Herself?
A: Show answer
Tina would have depended on Dan’s interpretation without being able to compare his account with the actual work. She could not reliably judge whether he had noticed the relevant defect, understood the work sequence, or proposed an appropriate next observation. That would weaken her ability to coach and would turn the relationship into a remote review of reports. Going to the process is not about taking over Dan’s investigation; it gives Tina enough firsthand understanding to guide his learning and assess what he needs to practice next. Data and written reports remain useful, but they do not replace seeing the work.
Study instruction:
- Compare what can be learned from a process report with what becomes visible through direct observation at the work location.
Key point:
- A mentor cannot guide effectively from a distance without understanding the actual condition.
- Go and see supports both problem diagnosis and development of the mentee.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Go to the Gemba
27. Q: How Was Tina Teaching Dan?
A: Show answer
Tina taught Dan through one-to-one guidance while they worked on a real production problem at the actual process. She used questions to understand his thinking, accompanied him to observe the scrap and installation work, directed him toward useful next observations when needed, and allowed him to develop and test the explanation. She did not substitute a classroom lecture or a ready-made answer for practice. The improvement activity and the learning activity were integrated: Dan was expected to make a genuine process improvement while developing the routine for solving future problems. This is why the mentor’s behavior is part of daily management rather than a separate training event.
Study instruction:
- Design a workplace coaching session around a real process challenge, specifying the learner’s task, the coach’s observation role, and the next review point.
Key point:
- Teach the routine through guided work on actual problems.
- The immediate process result and the learner’s capability are developed together.

28. Q: What Do You Think of the Countermeasure Dan Developed?
A: Show answer
In the case context, Dan’s countermeasure is acceptable because it links the verified cause to a revised work standard, instruction to the affected operators, observation of adoption, and follow-up tracking. It is not necessarily the most fail-safe design imaginable, but the process is actively managed by team leaders who observe work against the standard each shift. A poka-yoke device may be appropriate where risk, process capability, or control reliability requires it; the chapter’s caution is against adding devices automatically without considering maintenance and the operating system. The mentor should accept a countermeasure that meets the target condition and is supported by evidence rather than replacing it merely because the mentor has a more elegant idea.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a proposed countermeasure against cause linkage, effectiveness, risk, maintainability, standardization, and verification before deciding whether additional error-proofing is needed.
Key point:
- The best immediate answer is one that reliably meets the condition and is sustained in the actual management system.
- Do not confuse “more elaborate” with “more effective”; assess the risk and process context.

- Lean Enterprise Institute: Avoiding Failure in Problem-Solving Projects
29. Q: What is the Coaching Kata?
A: Show answer
The Coaching Kata is a repeatable pattern of mentor-mentee dialogue used to teach the Improvement Kata. The mentor asks about the target condition, actual condition, obstacles, next step, and when the pair will check what was learned. Those questions are not intended to produce a scripted conversation or force the learner toward a predetermined answer. They help the coach understand the learner’s reasoning and select the next useful coaching input. Repeated practice makes scientific problem-solving behavior more accessible and consistent. The routine is most effective when it is connected to a real process challenge and direct observation rather than treated as a classroom exercise or a checklist detached from work.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the five coaching questions and practice using them to understand a learner’s thinking without supplying the solution.
Key point:
- Coaching Kata teaches the Improvement Kata through repeated dialogue and guided practice.
- The questions reveal how the learner is thinking so coaching can be situational.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
30. Q: How does the Coaching Kata differ from the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The Improvement Kata is the learner’s routine for working toward a challenging target condition through observation, obstacle identification, and iterative experimentation. The Coaching Kata is the mentor’s routine for teaching and developing that behavior in the learner. The two routines are linked: the learner works on the process while the coach helps the learner think and act scientifically. The coach does not replace the learner’s improvement work, and the learner’s improvement work is not left unsupported. Confusing the two can lead to a manager either solving the problem personally or asking questions without understanding the improvement routine being practiced.
Study instruction:
- Draw two parallel sequences showing what the learner does and what the coach does during one improvement cycle.
Key point:
- Improvement Kata is the problem-solving practice; Coaching Kata is the teaching practice.
- Sustainable improvement depends on developing both routines together.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How Kata Fits In
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
31. Q: Why is developing people the primary task of a leader in the Coaching Kata approach?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents people’s improvement capability as a source of organizational strength. Processes improve repeatedly only when people can recognize current conditions, investigate obstacles, test ideas, and learn from results. A leader who solves every problem personally may obtain a local result but leaves the organization dependent on that leader. A leader who develops others creates more people capable of improving processes and adapting to changing conditions. This does not mean that results are unimportant; it means results are pursued by building the capability that can generate further results. The leader’s daily work therefore includes observing, coaching, and evaluating how people apply the improvement routine.
Study instruction:
- Review a manager’s calendar and identify how much time is spent developing problem-solving capability at the work location rather than only reviewing outcomes.
Key point:
- Organizational strength grows when more people can improve processes effectively.
- Leaders develop capability through daily work, not only formal training events.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Lean Leader Program
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
32. Q: Why does Toyota emphasize training while working on real processes?
A: Show answer
Real work exposes the learner’s actual habits, assumptions, and skill gaps in a way that a lecture or simulation may not. The coach can observe how the learner defines the problem, uses evidence, notices abnormalities, and responds when an experiment produces an unexpected result. The learner also experiences the operational consequences of each step and must connect the improvement routine to the customer and process. Classroom instruction can provide concepts and awareness, but it cannot by itself demonstrate that someone can apply the routine consistently under real conditions. Training while working integrates practice, feedback, and process improvement into the same activity.
Study instruction:
- Identify a real, bounded process problem that can be used to practice a specific problem-solving behavior under safe supervision.
Key point:
- Capability is demonstrated through application, not attendance alone.
- Real-process coaching reveals what the learner needs to practice next.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
33. Q: What are the limitations of classroom training and simulations when developing Improvement Kata capability?
A: Show answer
Classroom training can explain terminology, demonstrate a sequence, and create initial awareness. A simulation can allow low-risk practice. Neither automatically proves that a person can recognize a real abnormality, collect reliable facts, work backward to a point of cause, or adapt when an experiment produces an unexpected result. Those behaviors are context-sensitive and must be observed in actual work. A learner may perform well in a classroom exercise yet revert to habitual solution-jumping under production pressure. Formal instruction should therefore prepare and support workplace practice, not replace coaching at the process.
Study instruction:
- For an existing training course, specify which competencies can be assessed in class and which require observation during actual work.
Key point:
- Awareness is not the same as reliable workplace capability.
- Use classroom learning as a foundation for coached application, not as proof of mastery.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Lean Leader Program
34. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize that everyone at Toyota has a mentor?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes coaching as a continuing need rather than a remedial activity for beginners. Experienced employees still have blind spots, habits they may not notice, and new situations that require learning. A mentor provides an external view, asks questions that expose assumptions, and helps the learner practice the improvement routine. The relationship also provides continuity: the mentor is responsible for helping the mentee progress, while the mentee performs the actual work. As capability grows, a person may become a mentor to someone else while continuing to receive coaching from a more experienced person.
Study instruction:
- Identify a skill you perform regularly and list the blind spots that an experienced observer could help you detect.
Key point:
- Coaching remains useful at every level of experience.
- Development is continuous and can operate through nested mentor relationships.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Deploy the Improvement Kata
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
35. Q: Why are mentor-mentee relationships not necessarily tied to the formal organizational hierarchy?
A: Show answer
A useful mentor is selected because of relevant experience and the learner’s current development needs, not solely because of reporting lines. A direct manager may be the appropriate coach, but another experienced person may be better placed to guide a specific activity or skill. This flexibility allows mentoring to follow the work and the learning requirement. It also means that the organization must clarify roles: the mentor provides guidance and feedback, while formal managers retain their defined authority for staffing, performance, safety, and operational decisions. An informal coaching relationship should complement, not obscure, accountability structures.
Study instruction:
- Map a team’s reporting relationships and identify where a separate technical or kata mentor could add value without creating conflicting authority.
Key point:
- Mentoring should match development needs and relevant experience.
- Coaching relationships need clear boundaries with formal management responsibilities.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Deploy the Improvement Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Lean Leader Program
36. Q: What is the division of responsibility between the mentor and the mentee?
A: Show answer
The mentee is responsible for doing the work: observing the process, developing the analysis, proposing the next step, conducting the experiment, and learning from the result. The mentor is responsible for guiding the mentee through the improvement routine, understanding the situation well enough to judge the next coaching step, and supporting the outcome. The chapter describes an overlap of responsibility: the mentor should not take over the solution, yet cannot treat the result as someone else’s problem. This shared commitment creates a working bond and prevents coaching from becoming detached advice or a simple transfer of tasks.
Study instruction:
- For a current improvement activity, write down which decisions and actions belong to the learner and which coaching and oversight duties belong to the mentor.
Key point:
- The mentee owns the problem-solving work; the mentor owns the quality of coaching and remains invested in the result.
- Shared responsibility does not mean taking the task away from the learner.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: It Takes 2 (or More) to A3
37. Q: How should a mentor use questions without steering the mentee toward a predetermined solution?
A: Show answer
The mentor should ask questions to reveal what the mentee sees, how the mentee interprets evidence, and what the mentee believes the next step should be. Questions should be open enough to allow a genuine answer, including “I do not know.” If the mentor already has a favored solution, questions can become a guessing game and the mentee learns to please the mentor rather than investigate. The coach may be firm about the method of inquiry, the need for facts, and the discipline of PDCA, while remaining open about the specific answer that the evidence will support. The quality of coaching is judged by the learner’s development, not by agreement with the coach’s opinion.
Study instruction:
- Audit a set of coaching questions and mark which ones invite evidence and which ones reveal the coach’s preferred answer.
Key point:
- Ask to understand the learner’s thinking, not to disguise instructions.
- A genuine “I do not know” is a valid starting point for going to see.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
38. Q: Why might a mentor begin with a deliberately vague assignment or challenge?
A: Show answer
A broad opening challenge gives the mentor an opportunity to see how the mentee approaches an unfamiliar situation before providing detailed guidance. The mentee’s first response may reveal whether they jump to solutions, rely on assumptions, seek facts, understand the process, or recognize the need to clarify the problem. The mentor can then choose an appropriate next question or observation. Vague does not mean permanently ambiguous: as the dialogue progresses, the problem, current condition, and target condition must become specific. The initial openness is diagnostic; it is not permission to leave expectations unclear.
Study instruction:
- Create a bounded challenge and list the different types of learner responses that would indicate different coaching needs.
Key point:
- An open challenge can reveal the learner’s starting pattern of thinking.
- The mentor must then help convert the challenge into clear, evidence-based work.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
39. Q: When should a mentor be directive, and what should the direction cover?
A: Show answer
A mentor may be directive when the learner’s response shows that a particular next investigative step is needed, especially when the learner is inexperienced or the risk of an unsupported conclusion is high. The direction should concern how to understand the situation, such as going to the process, checking a standard, examining a defect, or running a controlled test. It should not prematurely prescribe the final countermeasure. The mentor must avoid turning the routine into a guessing game, but also should not leave a novice without the support needed to make progress. The right balance is structured guidance about the method and learner ownership of the solution.
Study instruction:
- For a coaching scenario, write one directive about the next evidence-gathering step and one example of an inappropriate directive that gives away the solution.
Key point:
- Be clear about the next learning step, not predetermined about the final answer.
- Direction should match the mentee’s capability and the process risk.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
40. Q: What does it mean to keep a mentee “in the corridor” of the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
Keeping the mentee in the corridor means guiding the learner to follow the disciplined improvement routine while allowing them considerable freedom to develop the specific solution. The mentor helps maintain direction, focus, and a scientific sequence: clarify the condition, examine the actual situation, identify obstacles, choose a next step, and check what was learned. The mentee is not free to skip observation and testing simply because a quick answer seems attractive. At the same time, the mentor does not dictate every action or impose a preferred countermeasure. The corridor combines alignment with initiative: common method, locally developed understanding and action.
Study instruction:
- Identify which parts of an improvement activity are method requirements and which decisions should remain with the learner.
Key point:
- The mentor controls the learning process without taking ownership of the solution.
- A shared routine can align behavior while preserving initiative.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How Kata Fits In
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Deploy the Improvement Kata
41. Q: Why should a mentor allow small mistakes during guided practice?
A: Show answer
Small, bounded mistakes reveal how the mentee is applying the routine and where further coaching is needed. If the mentor prevents every error by supplying answers in advance, the learner may never discover the reasoning behind the correct method. A manageable error can prompt observation, reflection, and a better next experiment. This principle has a strict boundary: the mentor should not permit a mistake that could harm a customer, create an unacceptable safety or quality risk, or cause uncontrolled disruption. Experiments should be limited, supervised, and designed so that learning is gained without exposing people or customers to avoidable harm.
Study instruction:
- Classify proposed learning experiments by risk and define the containment, approval, and stop conditions required before trying them.
Key point:
- Learning can come from small errors when risk is controlled.
- Customer, safety, and quality protection take priority over experimentation.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: It’s About Best Practicing
- Toyota Indonesia: Toyota Production System
42. Q: Why should “I do not know” be an acceptable answer in a coaching dialogue?
A: Show answer
If a mentee feels compelled to invent an answer, the coach receives misleading information and the learner may reinforce unsupported assumptions. Accepting “I do not know” keeps the dialogue honest and identifies a gap that can be addressed through observation or a small test. The mentor can then ask what evidence is missing and where the pair can go to obtain it. This response also reduces the pressure to guess what the coach wants to hear. A learning culture treats uncertainty as a reason to investigate, not as a personal failure or a reason to fabricate confidence.
Study instruction:
- Practice responding to an uncertain answer with a question about what is unknown and the smallest safe way to learn more.
Key point:
- Honest uncertainty is more useful than a confident guess.
- “I do not know” should trigger investigation and observation.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Starter Kata for Coaching
43. Q: Why must the mentor go and see the process rather than rely only on the mentee’s report?
A: Show answer
Firsthand observation gives the mentor an independent view of the actual condition and the details that may be missing from a report. It allows the mentor to assess whether the learner has noticed the relevant work sequence, compared actual work with the standard, and interpreted the evidence appropriately. Without that understanding, the mentor may be unable to select a useful next coaching step and may become a passive approver of the mentee’s proposals. Going to see does not mean the mentor takes over the work; it means the mentor remains close enough to the real situation to guide learning responsibly.
Study instruction:
- Compare a written report with a direct observation and list the important process details that were absent or distorted in the report.
Key point:
- The mentor needs firsthand understanding to coach effectively.
- Go and see is a management responsibility, not merely a data-collection technique.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Go to the Gemba
44. Q: What is the difference between observing a process and interviewing people about a process?
A: Show answer
Observation reveals what actually happens: sequence, timing, movement, equipment condition, workarounds, variation, and deviations from the standard. Interviews reveal what people remember, believe, experience, or think may be happening. Both forms of information can be valuable, but they are not interchangeable. In the case, Tina does not begin by asking operators for theories about the scrap; she and Dan inspect the parts and watch the installation. Once the actual condition is understood, respectful questions to operators can clarify context and help test explanations. The discipline is to avoid treating opinions as proof of the process mechanism.
Study instruction:
- For one process issue, prepare an observation checklist and a separate set of interview questions, then identify which claims require direct verification.
Key point:
- Observe the work to establish facts about the process.
- Use interviews to add context, not as a substitute for seeing the work.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Go to the Gemba
45. Q: Why should the work standard or target condition be reviewed before observing a process?
A: Show answer
The standard establishes the expected method or condition against which actual work can be compared. Without that reference, an observer may see an action but cannot reliably identify whether it is normal, abnormal, or inconsistent with the intended sequence. In the case, the standard describes when the part and nut driver should be picked up and how the nuts are installed, but it omits the critical instruction to locate the nut with the driver off. Comparing the standard with actual work helps reveal both operator variation and gaps in the standard itself. The standard is therefore a diagnostic reference and a document that may need improvement.
Study instruction:
- Bring the current standard to a process observation and record each difference between the prescribed sequence and actual work.
Key point:
- A standard makes deviations visible and gives observation a reference point.
- The standard itself may be incomplete and require revision after learning.
Source links:
- Toyota Indonesia: Toyota Production System
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
46. Q: What is the “point of cause,” and how should a team locate it?
A: Show answer
The point of cause is the place or process step where the condition that creates the problem occurs, which may be different from the location where the defect is discovered. The team should begin with a precise description of the defect, inspect where it is found, and trace the process backward through the relevant steps. At each step, compare the condition of the item and the work performed. In the trim case, damaged threads were found in scrap, but the team had to check line-side parts and observe the nut-installation sequence before identifying the likely cause. Locating the point of cause prevents the team from correcting the wrong department or process stage.
Study instruction:
- Choose a defect and map the flow from detection back to the earliest process step where the defect could be created; specify the evidence needed at each point.
Key point:
- The detection point and point of cause may be different.
- Trace the evidence through the process before assigning responsibility.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Go to the Gemba
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
47. Q: Why should a team avoid involving another department before the evidence supports it?
A: Show answer
Premature escalation can create unnecessary investigation, interrupt other teams, and spread an unverified explanation. It can also shift attention away from the actual process where the defect is generated. In the case, calling Incoming Inspection after seeing broken clips would have been premature because the later evidence showed damaged threads associated with the installation method. This does not mean departments should be isolated or that escalation is undesirable. Once the point of cause or a cross-functional dependency is supported by evidence, the relevant function should be involved with a clear description of the condition and the information needed.
Study instruction:
- Before escalating a process issue, document the evidence linking the other department to the suspected cause and define the specific assistance required.
Key point:
- Escalation should be evidence-led and purposeful.
- Avoid creating work for other functions based on an untested assumption.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Lean Enterprise Institute: It Takes 2 (or More) to A3
48. Q: Why does the chapter favor single-factor experiments over multiple simultaneous countermeasures?
A: Show answer
Changing one factor at a time makes it easier to interpret the result and understand cause and effect. If a team changes training, equipment settings, inspection frequency, and work sequence simultaneously, an improvement cannot be confidently attributed to any one change. The team may also introduce new variation that obscures the original problem. A focused test isolates the suspected mechanism, defines the expected result, and checks what actually happens. This does not mean every complex problem can be understood with a simplistic one-variable design; it means the team should control the scope and interpretability of experiments so learning is possible.
Study instruction:
- Convert a multi-action proposal into a sequence of small tests, each with one principal variable and an observable prediction.
Key point:
- Controlled experiments make cause and effect easier to see.
- A list of simultaneous countermeasures can create confusion rather than learning.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Start the A3 Problem-Solving Process
49. Q: Why should a mentor focus on the work system rather than blame an individual operator?
A: Show answer
The process determines the conditions in which people perform work. If the standard is incomplete, the tool or layout makes correct execution difficult, or the sequence allows a defect to occur, blaming the operator will not remove the system condition. The case shows that the new team members’ experience level coincided with higher scrap, but the mechanism was their use of a running driver while locating the nut. Retraining alone could temporarily increase attention while leaving the method unchanged. A system-focused investigation asks what in the work makes the error possible and what standard or process change will prevent it. Individual coaching may still be necessary when a genuine skill gap is demonstrated.
Study instruction:
- Reframe a blame-based problem statement into one that describes the process condition, standard, and observable behavior.
Key point:
- Investigate how the process produces the outcome before attributing fault.
- Training is appropriate when a real capability gap is established, not as a substitute for diagnosis.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
50. Q: How should a team distinguish correlation from a verified root cause?
A: Show answer
Correlation means two conditions vary together; it does not by itself show that one produces the other. In the case, scrap increased when new team members were assigned, which justified closer investigation. The team then observed a difference in the nut-driver technique and tested whether that technique caused thread damage. The controlled trial supplied stronger evidence for a specific mechanism. A robust investigation should also consider production volume, part mix, shift, equipment condition, changes in supplier material, and other factors that may coincide with the event. The cause explanation is stronger when it predicts an observable result that a test can confirm or reject.
Study instruction:
- For a suspected cause, list alternative explanations and design an observation or test that discriminates between them.
Key point:
- Correlation identifies a lead; mechanism and testing support causation.
- Consider other variables before concluding that one factor explains the result.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Start the A3 Problem-Solving Process
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why A3 Thinking Is the Ideal Problem-Solving Method
51. Q: What role does PDCA play in the mentor-mentee dialogue?
A: Show answer
PDCA gives the mentee a disciplined way to move from an expected result to an action, observation, and adjustment. The mentor helps the learner state what is expected before acting, then checks what actually happened and what was learned. Each cycle should be small enough that the result can be examined and the next step adapted to the new condition. The mentor is not asking for a long list of future actions because the result of one experiment may change what should happen next. The Coaching Kata makes this learning cycle visible through repeated questions and timely review.
Study instruction:
- Write a PDCA cycle for a real process issue, including a prediction before the test and a comparison of expected and actual results afterward.
Key point:
- PDCA is a learning cycle, not merely a sequence for completing tasks.
- The next action should reflect what the previous step revealed.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How Kata Fits In
52. Q: Why should coaching cycles be frequent, short, and conducted face-to-face at the process?
A: Show answer
Frequent short cycles keep the mentor and mentee close to the changing condition and allow the next step to be adjusted quickly. Long gaps can make observations stale and increase pressure to jump to a solution. Face-to-face coaching at the process allows both people to examine the same evidence, clarify what happened, and avoid turning the review into a lengthy discussion detached from work. A cycle should end when one clear next step and a time to check its result are agreed. It should not become an open-ended meeting or a review of a large action list.
Study instruction:
- Schedule a short coaching check at the work location and define the single next step, owner, expected result, and review time.
Key point:
- Short feedback loops support rapid learning and adjustment.
- The next step and check time should be specific and close to the process.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
53. Q: How should a coach use the five Coaching Kata questions in a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The coach should use the questions as the main headings of a dialogue: What is the target condition? What is the actual condition now? What obstacles are preventing progress, and which one is being addressed? What is the next step? When can the coach and learner go and see what was learned? The questions are prompts for further inquiry, not a rigid script that excludes clarification or feedback. The coach should listen carefully to the answer, compare it with direct observation, and choose the next question or guidance based on what the learner needs. Once one next step is clear, the cycle can end and the experiment can begin.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a mock coaching cycle and record the learner’s answers, the coach’s follow-up questions, and the agreed next observation time.
Key point:
- The five questions structure the dialogue and reveal the learner’s thinking.
- One clear next step is more useful than a premature list of actions.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: The Five Coaching Kata Questions
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Example Coaching Cycle
54. Q: What is the purpose of an A3 document in mentor-mentee dialogue?
A: Show answer
An A3 is a concise written representation of the learner’s thinking that supports the dialogue between mentor and mentee. Preparing it forces the learner to clarify observations, current condition, target condition, proposals, plans, and learning points. It helps the mentor see how the learner is approaching the problem and what guidance is needed next. It also gives both people a shared reference for discussion, consensus, and process checks. The chapter emphasizes that the value is not the paper itself; it is the iterative thinking and dialogue used to develop it. A completed form without that process can become a bureaucratic artifact rather than a learning tool.
Study instruction:
- Review an A3 and identify which statements are supported by observations, which are hypotheses, and which are proposed actions.
Key point:
- A3 supports thinking and coaching; it is not a substitute for either.
- The document should make the problem-solving story visible and discussable.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why the A3 Process Involves More than Filling in Boxes
- Lean Enterprise Institute: A3: Tool or Process? Both
55. Q: What information should a useful A3 contain?
A: Show answer
The format varies with the purpose and theme, but the chapter identifies common content: a summary of observations or current condition, a target condition, proposals, plans, and key points from reflections. A3 sections should build on one another. A clear theme frames what matters; the current condition provides evidence; the target condition describes the desired future state; analysis explains the gap and obstacles; proposals and plans connect action to the analysis; and reflection records what was learned. The one-page story should be concise and visual, with additional pages available for supporting data where necessary. The exact headings should serve the problem, not dictate it.
Study instruction:
- Draft a one-page outline for a process problem and check that each section is supported by the section before it.
Key point:
- A3 content should form a logical story from current facts to learning and action.
- Adapt the format to the purpose while keeping the reasoning traceable.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why the A3 Process Involves More than Filling in Boxes
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Start the A3 Problem-Solving Process
56. Q: How should an A3 be developed between mentor and mentee?
A: Show answer
The A3 should be built through repeated back-and-forth review rather than completed in one sitting and submitted for signature. The mentor should have the mentee work on one section at a time, review the evidence and logic, and return the document for clarification or deeper investigation when necessary. Earlier sections may need revision as understanding improves. Much of the benefit lies in this iterative process because it forces the learner to gather facts, reconsider assumptions, and connect each part of the story. The final document is a record of thinking that has been developed through dialogue, not a form that creates sound thinking by itself.
Study instruction:
- Practice reviewing an A3 one section at a time and write feedback that asks for evidence or clarification rather than supplying the answer.
Key point:
- Iteration and pushback are essential parts of A3 learning.
- A signature is not evidence that the reasoning is sound.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why the A3 Process Involves More than Filling in Boxes
- Lean Enterprise Institute: It Takes 2 (or More) to A3
57. Q: What are common ways organizations misuse A3 documents?
A: Show answer
Common misuse includes treating the A3 as a template to fill in, requiring a completed report without iterative discussion, copying a format without understanding its purpose, or using the document as a substitute for going to the process. These practices can generate paperwork without improving problem-solving capability. Another misuse is turning the A3 into an email attachment passed between offices, so the written record replaces direct coaching. A3 should help organize evidence and keep dialogue focused, but it cannot establish that the observations are correct or that a countermeasure works. The process of thinking, observing, testing, and reviewing remains essential.
Study instruction:
- Audit an A3 process for evidence of actual gemba observation, iterative review, learner ownership, and follow-up checks.
Key point:
- Completing boxes does not equal A3 thinking.
- The document must support real investigation and coaching rather than replace them.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Why the A3 Process Involves More than Filling in Boxes
- Lean Enterprise Institute: A3: Tool or Process? Both
58. Q: How can a written A3 support coaching without replacing face-to-face communication?
A: Show answer
The A3 should be prepared before or between coaching cycles so that the learner arrives with a concise account of the evidence and current thinking. The mentor and mentee should then discuss it together, ideally at or near the process, and verify important claims against direct observation. The document can reduce unfocused conversation and preserve key decisions, but it should not encourage remote email reviews or replace going to see. If the A3 contains no data, there may be little basis for a meaningful discussion. Written and verbal methods should reinforce each other: the document organizes thinking, while face-to-face dialogue tests and develops it.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page coaching brief and identify which facts must be verified in person before agreeing on the next step.
Key point:
- Use the A3 as a shared focal point for dialogue.
- Keep observation and coaching close to the actual work.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: It Takes 2 (or More) to A3
- Lean Enterprise Institute: No Really, Go to the Gemba
59. Q: How can an organization verify that a countermeasure has been adopted and sustained?
A: Show answer
Verification should combine process observation with outcome tracking. The process observation checks whether people follow the revised method and whether the standard is understood; the outcome measure checks whether the defect, delay, or cost problem has actually reduced. The organization should define the period, frequency, shifts, sample, and responsible reviewer before implementation. If performance worsens or the old condition reappears, the team should return to the process, compare actual work with the standard, and investigate what changed. Sustaining a countermeasure is an ongoing management activity, not a one-time communication or training event.
Study instruction:
- Build a sustainment plan with a process-adherence check, a performance measure, review dates, owner, and escalation trigger.
Key point:
- Verify both whether the new method is used and whether it produces the intended result.
- Recurrence should trigger renewed investigation rather than blame or automatic retraining.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Avoiding Failure in Problem-Solving Projects
- Toyota Motor Asia: Toyota Production System
60. Q: How can an organization build Coaching Kata capability across multiple management levels?
A: Show answer
Build capability through a deliberate coach-learner structure in which leaders first practice the Improvement Kata themselves, then learn to coach others under the guidance of more experienced mentors. Start with a manageable number of real processes so coaches can observe the work and conduct frequent cycles. Define who coaches whom, what routine is practiced, and how capability will be demonstrated. As people become capable, some can support learners at the next level while continuing to receive coaching themselves. Do not treat the rollout as a one-time course or measure success only by the number of trained people. The organization must assess whether people can apply the routine and improve real processes over time.
Study instruction:
- Draft a staged deployment plan that identifies focus processes, mentor assignments, practice frequency, capability evidence, and reflection points.
Key point:
- Coaching capability must be practiced and developed, not merely announced or taught once.
- A scalable system connects mentors, learners, real work, and ongoing capability checks.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute: How to Deploy the Improvement Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Lean Leader Program

Table 9
Developing Improvement Kata Behavior Across an Organization
1. Q: How can other companies develop similar routines and thinking in their organizations?
A: Show answer
They develop them by practicing the Improvement Kata and Coaching Kata in their own work rather than copying Toyota's visible tools. Leaders clarify a long-term direction, study actual processes and current management behavior, establish measurable target conditions, and use short experiments to discover a workable path. Coaches reinforce the desired thinking through frequent dialogue at the process. Because organizations differ in people, processes, history, and constraints, the details must be learned locally. The aim is to make scientific improvement and coaching part of daily management, not to run a temporary implementation campaign.
Study instruction:
- "Compare copying techniques with developing repeatable thinking routines; identify one real process where the organization can begin practicing."
Key point:
- Develop behavior through repeated practice on real work, not imitation of tools alone.
- The path must be discovered and adapted to the organization's own conditions.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
2. Q: What is the actual organizational challenge described in Chapter 9?
A: Show answer
The challenge is to help people throughout the organization think and act according to the Improvement Kata at every process, every day. This reaches beyond introducing lean methods or adding an improvement activity to existing management. It changes how managers lead, how employees understand problems, how teams make decisions, and how learning is reinforced. A few successful workshops or trained specialists do not demonstrate organization-wide adoption. The desired condition is a daily management pattern in which people understand current conditions, pursue target conditions, experiment, and learn as part of normal work.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the desired behavior at process, team, and leadership levels; identify current routines that would need to change."
Key point:
- The target is a different way of managing and learning, not an extra project.
- Organization-wide adoption requires daily practice across roles and processes.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
3. Q: Why does developing Improvement Kata behavior involve organizational culture?
A: Show answer
Culture is expressed through recurring ways people think, act, react, and respond to problems. If employees are expected to experiment scientifically and leaders are expected to coach rather than prescribe solutions, those behaviors must become habitual. Posters, terminology, policies, or formal structures cannot establish the habits by themselves. The chapter presents the effort as a shift in organizational culture, often an adjustment to existing culture rather than a complete replacement. Repeated practice, feedback, and reinforcement gradually make the desired behavior more natural.
Study instruction:
- "Observe how a team actually reacts to problems and compare its behavior with its stated values and improvement principles."
Key point:
- Culture is visible in habitual behavior, not merely formal statements.
- Repeated practice is central to embedding a different management pattern.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
4. Q: Is there a universal formula for introducing Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
No single rollout formula fits every organization. Starting capabilities, process conditions, leadership habits, workforce experience, and operating constraints differ. A framework can guide the work, but it cannot replace direct observation and experimentation in the local environment. The chapter emphasizes that organizations must develop their management systems to suit their own situations. The route is initially uncertain, so leaders should treat proposed tactics as hypotheses, test them, examine results, and adjust the teaching approach rather than copy another company's countermeasures without understanding their own current and target conditions.
Study instruction:
- "List the local factors that could affect adoption and identify assumptions that need to be tested rather than accepted."
Key point:
- Use the kata as a framework, not a rigid universal rollout plan.
- Local experimentation is necessary because the route depends on actual conditions.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
5. Q: What does it mean for an organization to become an experimenter?
A: Show answer
It means using deliberate, small tests to learn how to move from a current condition toward a target condition. It does not mean launching a separate innovation program or testing whether the Improvement Kata works. The organization applies the routine to its own development: observe current behavior, define a desired behavior, try a practical step, check what happened, and adjust. Process improvement, employee development, and culture change can therefore occur through the same work. Unexpected obstacles are treated as information about the next learning need rather than as reasons to abandon the effort.
Study instruction:
- "Design one small experiment to improve the way managers coach and specify what observable evidence would indicate learning."
Key point:
- Experimentation is the means of discovering the path, not a separate initiative.
- Use PDCA to develop both processes and management behavior.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
6. Q: How are process improvement and development of organizational behavior connected?
A: Show answer
They reinforce each other. People learn the Improvement Kata by applying it to actual processes, while process results provide evidence about whether coaching and practice are effective. A learner's capability is visible in how the person studies the process, sets a target condition, selects experiments, and responds to evidence. Process work also gives the development effort a meaningful operational purpose. This connection prevents training from becoming abstract and disconnected from performance. Coaches should review both the learner's behavior and the process outcome rather than treating either one as a complete measure by itself.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a focus process and define one operational measure and one behavior the learner should demonstrate while improving it."
Key point:
- Real process work is both the improvement objective and the learning environment.
- Review behavior and process outcomes together.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
7. Q: Why is classroom training insufficient to create lasting Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
Classroom instruction can explain concepts and create initial awareness, but understanding does not automatically create new habits. The Improvement Kata includes behaviors that may feel unfamiliar, such as carefully grasping current conditions, setting a specific target condition, and testing one next step rather than immediately listing solutions. Learners need repeated practice in real situations, feedback from a coach, and opportunities to see the consequences of their actions. Without that follow-through, classroom knowledge can fade and familiar habits return. Training should prepare people for practice, not serve as proof of capability.
Study instruction:
- "Compare what a learner can explain in a classroom with what the learner actually does at a work process."
Key point:
- Awareness is not the same as demonstrated skill.
- Classroom instruction should lead quickly to coached application.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
8. Q: What role should classroom training play in a Kata development program?
A: Show answer
Classroom training can introduce the purpose, vocabulary, and basic sequence before learners begin hands-on practice. It creates a shared starting point and explains why participants will observe processes and conduct experiments. The chapter cautions against long sessions or treating course completion as evidence of behavior change. Classroom work is most useful when it is short, informational, and delivered to people who are about to practice with a coach. The coach then helps learners translate the concepts into actions in their actual work environment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a short orientation agenda and specify the practical coaching activity that follows each topic."
Key point:
- Use classroom sessions to establish awareness and prepare learners for practice.
- Confirm capability through application, not attendance.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
9. Q: Why are workshops insufficient for developing daily Improvement Kata habits?
A: Show answer
Workshops are generally designed to produce a point improvement within a limited period. They can solve a local problem or introduce methods, but they do not necessarily establish a recurring way of managing every process. The chapter notes that results can recede after a workshop ends. A workshop may be a useful countermeasure for a particular condition, but it is not a substitute for daily ownership, coaching, and follow-up. Process owners must continue observing the work, pursuing target conditions, and learning through experiments after the event team has left.
Study instruction:
- "Review a completed workshop and identify the process owner, daily routine, and coaching checks that remained afterward."
Key point:
- A point improvement is not the same as a sustained behavior pattern.
- Daily ownership and repeated coaching help prevent regression.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
10. Q: What should external consultants contribute to Kata development?
A: Show answer
External consultants can provide early coaching, technical perspective, and help the organization begin experimenting. Their role should be to accelerate internal capability, not to perform all coaching or own the improvement effort indefinitely. If consultants do the work on behalf of managers and employees, the organization may gain short-term results without learning to sustain the routine. The chapter places responsibility for culture change with the organization, particularly senior leadership. An external coach should work alongside internal people, make the reasoning visible, and progressively help them coach others.
Study instruction:
- "Define the capability to be transferred, the internal people who will practice, and evidence that they can continue without the consultant."
Key point:
- Consultants should build internal capability rather than replace it.
- Ownership of daily improvement must remain inside the organization.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
11. Q: Why can metrics, incentives, and motivators fail to create Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
Metrics and incentives can influence attention, but they cannot teach people how to observe a process, establish an appropriate target condition, conduct experiments, or learn from obstacles. When rewards attach to easily counted activities, employees may optimize the score rather than develop capability. A high number of completed workshops or coaching sessions can coexist with little process improvement. Measures should help people see actual conditions and learn, not substitute for coaching or become the purpose of the work. The organization must connect measures to demonstrated behavior and meaningful process outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect an improvement scorecard and identify measures that reward activity volume rather than capability or process change."
Key point:
- Measurement can support learning but cannot replace it.
- Beware of incentives that encourage point chasing instead of target-condition achievement.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
12. Q: Why is reorganizing the company not a reliable route to continuous improvement?
A: Show answer
Changing reporting lines, departments, or value-stream structures does not automatically change how people respond to problems. The chapter argues that behavior, rather than formal organizational shape, is decisive. A traditional functional structure can support continuous improvement if people develop the required habits and coaching routines. Structural changes may sometimes be useful countermeasures when actual work reveals a need, but they should not be treated as the target condition or proof that culture has changed. Leaders should clarify the behaviors needed and let evidence inform any redesign.
Study instruction:
- "Separate a proposed structural change from the behavior it is intended to enable; identify evidence that would justify the change."
Key point:
- Structure is a possible countermeasure, not a substitute for new behavior.
- Do not reorganize in the hope that desired habits will appear automatically.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
13. Q: How do repeated practice and reinforcement change organizational habits?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes habit development as repeated practice of desired behavior, supported by periodic experiences of achievement. Repetition makes the routine more familiar and can make the response increasingly automatic. In the Improvement Kata, people repeatedly practice observing current conditions, setting target conditions, experimenting, and reflecting. Coaches reinforce the pattern through questions, procedural guidance, and recognition of useful learning. Over time, learners can devote more attention to process details because the basic routine becomes more natural. This development is gradual and requires deliberate practice rather than a one-time announcement.
Study instruction:
- "Select one behavior to practice repeatedly and define how a coach will observe, reinforce, and review it over several cycles."
Key point:
- Habits develop through repeated behavior, feedback, and reinforcement.
- A new routine becomes reliable through practice over time.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
14. Q: Why should training and doing take place together at actual work processes?
A: Show answer
Applying the katas to actual work allows coaches to see what learners can really do and lets learners practice with real conditions, constraints, and data. The work is not a simulation detached from operational responsibility. The process provides evidence about the quality of observation, target setting, experimentation, and follow-up. It also helps the organization judge whether teaching is improving the process. Separating training from work can conceal gaps that only appear when a learner faces uncertainty, competing demands, and actual operating conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Select a process suitable for repeated practice and identify what the coach should observe during live application."
Key point:
- Practice should occur where the work is performed.
- Actual process performance reveals learning needs and coaching effectiveness.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
15. Q: Why is relying on only one change factor ineffective?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses the need for a coordinated effort rather than relying on urgency, coaching, or explanation of the Improvement Kata in isolation. Urgency without a defined practice pattern can generate activity in many directions. Coaching without a clear routine has no consistent behavior to develop. Explaining the kata without coaching and a meaningful reason to apply it will not change habits. The practical lesson is to combine a clear desired pattern, guided practice, and a reason to change how work is managed. The chapter's figure is not reproduced in the text source, so this explanation follows the surrounding discussion rather than assigning unsupported labels to the figure.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether the desired routine, coaching support, and reason for change are all explicit and mutually reinforcing."
Key point:
- No isolated pressure, instruction, or coaching activity is sufficient.
- People need a pattern to practice, guidance, and a meaningful reason to apply it.

- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
16. Q: Why should an organization use the Improvement Kata to develop Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
The Improvement Kata provides a disciplined way to work through an uncertain path toward a desired condition. Developing a new management culture is itself such a challenge: the organization cannot know every obstacle or the exact sequence of actions in advance. Leaders can grasp current management behavior, define a measurable target condition, and use short PDCA cycles to test ways of teaching and coaching. This keeps the development effort grounded in facts and learning. It also models the very behavior the organization wants people to adopt, making the routine both the subject and the means of development.
Study instruction:
- "Write a target condition for one aspect of management behavior and identify the next experiment that could move toward it."
Key point:
- Use the Improvement Kata as the method for developing its own organizational practice.
- Culture development requires repeated PDCA, not a fixed blueprint.

17. Q: How can the Improvement Kata be applied at different organizational levels?
A: Show answer
The Improvement Kata can be used at the process level and at higher levels where the work is to develop coaching capability or organizational behavior. A production team may improve a work sequence, a manager may improve coaching cycles, and an advance group may improve the teaching approach. The subject changes, but the logic remains: understand the current condition, define a target condition, and use short experiments to move toward it. This nested application connects local learning with broader development without turning culture change into an abstract corporate program.
Study instruction:
- "Map one process-level improvement and one management-development activity using the same current-condition and target-condition logic."
Key point:
- The same routine can improve work processes and the system that develops people.
- Higher-level improvement should remain connected to process-level learning.

18. Q: What should leaders investigate when process results do not improve as expected?
A: Show answer
Leaders should examine the quality of teaching and coaching rather than assume that the number of cycles proves effectiveness. They can review whether the learner understood the current condition, whether the target condition was clear, and whether experiments were designed and checked appropriately. The coaching approach may need adjustment, including the questions asked, procedural guidance, frequency of checks, or the coach's understanding of the process. The chapter treats disappointing results as information for improving instruction. This does not mean blaming the coach or learner; it means investigating the development system through PDCA.
Study instruction:
- "When a target condition is missed, review a coaching cycle and identify one teaching behavior to test differently."
Key point:
- Weak results should trigger examination of coaching and learning conditions.
- Treat setbacks as information for adjustment rather than blame.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
19. Q: Why should coaches practice the Improvement Kata before teaching it?
A: Show answer
A coach needs firsthand experience to recognize whether a learner is observing the process carefully, setting an appropriately challenging target condition, and selecting useful experiments. Without that experience, the coach may only approve or praise a proposal without diagnosing its reasoning. Personal practice gives the coach a basis for asking better questions and offering procedural guidance that develops scientific thinking. The chapter therefore recommends that coaches gain practical experience before teaching others. Coaching skill is not simply knowledge of five questions; it depends on understanding the work and the thinking pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Before assigning someone as a coach, observe that person independently applying the Improvement Kata and handling obstacles through experiments."
Key point:
- Credible coaching depends on practical experience with the routine being taught.
- A coach must evaluate reasoning, not merely encourage activity.
