- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
20. Q: How can an organization overcome the initial shortage of experienced coaches?
A: Show answer
The chapter recognizes that few people may have enough experience to coach at the outset. The organization should develop a small number of capable practitioners early, give them repeated practice on real processes, and attach potential coaches to the work so they learn through observation and guided participation. Senior leaders should practice rather than delegate the learning entirely. External mentoring may accelerate development, but internal people must progressively assume the coaching role. Expansion should not outpace the ability to provide meaningful coaching, because weak coaching can turn practice into a superficial ritual.
Study instruction:
- "Estimate learner demand and coaching capacity, then define a realistic first group and a plan for developing additional coaches."
Key point:
- Build a capable internal coaching base before scaling broadly.
- Expansion should match the organization's ability to coach effectively.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
21. Q: Who should practice first when an organization is changing its management culture?
A: Show answer
Senior managers should begin practicing the Improvement Kata ahead of the broader organization when the aim is cultural change. Middle and frontline managers will watch what senior leaders actually do before deciding whether the change is a genuine priority. If executives continue managing in the old way while asking others to adopt new behaviors, the message is inconsistent. Their personal practice helps them understand the routine and credibly guide the next management levels. The chapter distinguishes this situation from an organization where the kata is already established and everyone continues practicing.
Study instruction:
- "Review senior leaders' routines and identify where they can visibly practice the kata before asking other levels to adopt it."
Key point:
- In a culture-change effort, senior leaders must go first through visible practice.
- Actions communicate priority more convincingly than announcements.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
22. Q: Why do middle managers often wait before adopting a new management routine?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a broad pattern: a small group welcomes change, a small group resists it, and most people wait to see what actually happens. Middle managers may hesitate because they are uncertain whether senior leaders are committed, whether the initiative will last, and what adoption means for their roles. Their behavior is influential because they translate leadership priorities into daily management. Criticizing hesitation without examining the signals managers receive misses the conditions shaping their response. Consistent senior practice and practical coaching help reduce uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Ask managers what evidence they need before changing their routines and compare their answers with current leadership behavior."
Key point:
- The majority's behavior is strongly shaped by observed leadership commitment.
- Understand waiting behavior before treating it as resistance.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
23. Q: Why can development of Improvement Kata behavior not be delegated by senior leaders?
A: Show answer
Culture change depends on how leaders and managers behave, not simply on a program assigned to specialists. Senior leaders determine priorities through decisions, time allocation, and responses to problems. If they do not personally practice and learn the routine, they may be unable to enlist and guide the managers who will coach others. Delegation can signal that the change is peripheral. Senior leaders can assign support tasks, but they cannot outsource responsibility for modeling, reinforcing, and sustaining the desired management behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Separate executive responsibilities from support tasks that can be assigned to the advance group."
Key point:
- Senior leaders own the behavioral change and must demonstrate it.
- A specialist group cannot compensate for absent leadership commitment.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
24. Q: What is the purpose of establishing an advance group?
A: Show answer
An advance group develops early familiarity with the Improvement Kata, practices it on selected processes, learns what the routine requires in the organization's circumstances, and helps refine the teaching approach. It is a learning group rather than a permanent central improvement department. Direct process work gives members evidence about operating conditions and the capability of potential coaches. The group can then help prepare a wider plan based on what has actually been learned. Its continuing role is to monitor and improve the organization's teaching approach while staying connected to real conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Define the group's learning objectives, initial processes, mentoring arrangement, and expected outputs before it begins."
Key point:
- The advance group is a learning and teaching-development mechanism.
- Its work should be grounded in direct process practice.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
25. Q: How large should the initial advance group be, and who should participate?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends an initial group of no more than about five people and includes a senior executive. A small group can learn together, observe the same processes, and refine its approach without a large coordination burden. Executive participation connects the effort to leadership and provides firsthand understanding. The group is not intended to represent every function or take over improvement for the organization. Its composition should support rapid learning, access to real processes, and development of internal coaching capability.
Study instruction:
- "Select a small group with process access and senior leadership involvement; document why each member is needed."
Key point:
- Start small and include senior leadership.
- The advance group should enable learning, not become a substitute operating structure.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
26. Q: Why should an advance group not become a centralized lean department?
A: Show answer
A centralized lean department can remove responsibility from managers and leaders who own actual processes. The chapter warns that a parallel staff group may lack the authority and daily connection needed to change behavior across the organization. It can also reinforce the belief that improvement is a specialist activity. The advance group should develop the teaching approach, assist with coaching where useful, and maintain visibility of actual conditions. Local managers and leaders remain responsible for improvement and coaching in their own areas.
Study instruction:
- "Compare central support responsibilities with those of process-owning managers and remove arrangements that transfer daily ownership."
Key point:
- Process owners and local leaders must retain improvement responsibility.
- Central support should build capability, not centralize all improvement work.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
27. Q: What role should an external mentor play in supporting the advance group?
A: Show answer
An external mentor can help the advance group learn the routine, challenge assumptions, and develop an effective coaching approach. The mentor should not perform all coaching or solve every process problem. Members should increasingly conduct the work, explain their reasoning, and coach others while receiving feedback. This protects the organization's ability to continue after external support ends. The chapter's principle is that the organization must develop its own management system and internal capability through experience.
Study instruction:
- "Define the mentor's work around capability transfer, observed practice, feedback, and reduced dependence over time."
Key point:
- External expertise should accelerate internal learning.
- A consultant who replaces internal coaches can create dependency.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
28. Q: Where should the advance group begin practicing the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests beginning with a few real production or assembly processes and, where appropriate, a pacemaker process. A focused process provides a concrete setting for grasping current conditions, assessing stability, establishing a target condition, and testing experiments. Beginning with a small number allows the group to learn deeply instead of spreading attention too widely. Members should reflect on what they are learning about the process, people, and organization. The process should be accessible enough for repeated observation and coaching.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one or a few important, observable processes and explain why they are suitable for repeated practice."
Key point:
- Begin with a manageable number of real processes.
- Selection should support direct observation and frequent learning.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
29. Q: How can process stability be examined during the advance group's initial work?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives an example of timing and graphing approximately 20 to 40 successive cycles at several points in a process. The purpose is to see actual variation and identify what prevents the process and operators from working with a stable cycle. Observations should be made at the process rather than inferred from distant reports. The data do not automatically reveal the cause; they help the team formulate questions and identify obstacles for further investigation. This also provides practice in disciplined observation before setting a target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Collect successive cycle-time observations at defined process points, graph them, and identify questions requiring direct follow-up."
Key point:
- Use repeated observations to understand variation and stability.
- Data should guide investigation rather than be treated as an automatic explanation.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
30. Q: How long may the initial advance-group learning period take?
A: Show answer
The chapter indicates that initial efforts can occupy the advance group for roughly two to six months. This allows members to practice on real processes, understand the routine beyond its terminology, and develop a grounded view of current conditions. The duration is not a universal deadline; it reflects the time that may be needed for meaningful firsthand understanding before wider planning. Rushing through this stage can produce a shallow plan based on assumptions. The group should use the period to build competence and identify the next learning needs.
Study instruction:
- "Create a review of process observations, coaching lessons, unresolved questions, and evidence of growing capability."
Key point:
- Allow enough time for firsthand learning before broad rollout.
- The two-to-six-month period is chapter guidance, not a mandatory rule.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
31. Q: How should potential internal coaches be involved in the advance group's work?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes attaching two or three potential coaches to the advance group in addition to its core members. These trainees participate in shop-floor application so they can observe and practice in real conditions. They need not attend every advance-group activity, particularly planning discussions that do not match their current learning stage. This creates a practical path toward coaching capability and increases the internal coach pool. Progress should be judged by what trainees demonstrate at the process, not by how many meetings or courses they complete.
Study instruction:
- "Specify which shop-floor activities trainees will join and what observable competencies they must demonstrate."
Key point:
- Develop coaches through real process practice.
- Match training exposure to the learner's current capability and role.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
32. Q: What is a coaching cycle in the development of Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle is a short, repeatable interaction in which a coach uses the five questions with one learner at the process being improved. It begins after a target condition has been established and helps the coach understand both the process condition and the learner's thinking. It is not a general status meeting or lengthy problem-solving workshop. It is a focused check that helps the coach judge capability and determine an appropriate next step. Repeated cycles condition the desired thinking pattern and make coaching part of daily work.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a coaching cycle at the worksite and identify how each question reveals process facts or learner reasoning."
Key point:
- A coaching cycle is a focused learning routine at the process.
- It develops the learner's thinking while keeping the coach connected to actual conditions.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
33. Q: What are the five questions used in a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The five questions are: What is the target condition? What is the actual condition now? What obstacles do you think are preventing you from reaching the target condition, and which one are you addressing now? What is your next step or experiment, and what do you expect? When can we go and see what we have learned from taking that step? Together, they guide the learner from the desired condition through current facts and obstacles to a specific experiment and planned check. Their value lies in the dialogue, not in mechanically reciting words.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the five questions and practice asking them in sequence without turning the exchange into solution giving."
Key point:
- The questions connect target, current condition, obstacle, experiment, and learning check.
- The fifth question schedules observation of learning.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
34. Q: What are the main purposes of a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three purposes: quickly grasping the current condition of the process and learner so the coach can judge an appropriate next step; providing a routine for conditioning the desired behavior; and recognizing the learner's efforts. These purposes reinforce one another. The coach needs to understand the situation to give useful procedural guidance, the learner needs repetition to develop skill, and recognition helps sustain engagement. A coaching cycle is therefore both a process check and a capability-development interaction, not merely a progress report.
Study instruction:
- "After observing a cycle, assess whether it clarified the process, revealed learner thinking, and produced a useful next step."
Key point:
- A cycle supports process understanding, behavior practice, and recognition.
- It is more than a status report or inspection.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
35. Q: How frequently should coaching cycles take place at a focus process?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes striving to conduct a coaching cycle at each focus process at least once per shift. Frequent practice helps make the routine part of normal work and gives the coach timely information about progress. The exact cadence should be adapted to the process and circumstances, but cycles should not be so infrequent that obstacles remain unexamined for long periods. The chapter also encourages checking early in the workday rather than postponing every discussion until the shift ends. The schedule should be explicit enough that coaching receives real priority.
Study instruction:
- "Set a practical cadence for a focus process and compare planned cycles with actual cycles over several shifts."
Key point:
- Frequent scheduled practice helps establish the routine.
- Do not let coaching become an end-of-shift administrative activity.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
36. Q: How long should a coaching cycle take, and what makes it efficient?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes aiming for about 15 minutes in many cases once coach and learner gain experience. Efficiency comes from staying at the process, following the five questions, focusing on one target condition, and identifying one next step. Novice coaches may let the discussion expand into many issues and lengthy debate. That weakens the cycle's role as frequent practice. The aim is not to rush meaningful thought but to prevent the routine from becoming a broad meeting. More extensive analysis can be arranged separately when needed.
Study instruction:
- "Time a cycle and identify discussion that could be handled separately without weakening the five-question routine."
Key point:
- A focused cycle can often be completed in about 15 minutes.
- Conciseness comes from one target condition and one next step, not from rushing.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
37. Q: Why should a coaching cycle end with one next step rather than a list of actions?
A: Show answer
Once coach and learner agree on a single next step, the coaching cycle is complete. A long action list can encourage speculative planning instead of learning through experiments. One next step keeps attention on the immediate obstacle and creates a clear opportunity to observe what happens. The step may be very small, such as collecting additional data or observing a process more closely. Small steps are acceptable when they increase understanding. The next action should be taken promptly and followed by another coaching check.
Study instruction:
- "Reduce an overlong action list to the next experiment that will produce the most useful learning."
Key point:
- Choose one next step that can be tested and checked.
- A small learning step can be more valuable than a long speculative list.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
38. Q: What should the fifth coaching question accomplish?
A: Show answer
The fifth question asks when coach and learner can go and see what they have learned from the next step. It establishes a timely observation point so the experiment produces learning rather than disappearing into an action list. New coaches sometimes assume the next step must be a solution, but the chapter explains that it may simply be deeper investigation. The learner may need to verify a measurement, observe a cycle, or test an assumption before choosing a countermeasure. The check should be scheduled around the expected learning.
Study instruction:
- "For each next step, state what is expected to be learned and set a specific time to observe the result."
Key point:
- The next check is about learning, not merely confirming task completion.
- Further observation may be appropriate before choosing a solution.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
39. Q: Why should a coach generally work with one mentee and one target condition at a time?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises coaching one target condition at a time, generally with one mentee. Each learner has a distinct process situation, capability level, and development need. When a coach tries to handle several mentees in one dialogue, the discussion can become general and individuals may be less willing to disclose difficulties. Focus allows the coach to understand the learner's reasoning and provide guidance relevant to the specific process. Group learning may support awareness or reflection, but it should not replace focused coaching when developing individual capability.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a multi-person coaching discussion and determine whether each learner receives guidance tied to an individual target condition."
Key point:
- Focused one-to-one coaching supports candor and specificity.
- Do not dilute coaching by combining unrelated target conditions.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
40. Q: Why should a beginner's first target conditions have a short time horizon?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends limiting a learner's first few target conditions to about one week. A short horizon lets the learner experience the complete improvement routine more frequently, encounter the check-and-adjust stage, and build a practice rhythm. It also reduces the temptation to create a detailed long-range plan before the learner understands the process. As capability grows, the horizon can be lengthened; the chapter gives roughly one to four weeks as a possible later range. These periods are guidance, not universal rules, and should reflect the actual process.
Study instruction:
- "Set a one-week target condition for a novice and define observations that will show whether the full routine was practiced."
Key point:
- Short horizons help beginners practice the full cycle and build momentum.
- Lengthen the horizon as experience and understanding improve.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
41. Q: Why should coaching checks occur early in the workday rather than only at the end of a shift?
A: Show answer
An early check creates time to influence the work while the shift is still underway. If coaching is repeatedly postponed until the end of the day, it may signal that the target condition is not specific or coaching is not treated as a priority. The chapter suggests that time of day can be part of a coaching target condition. An early check also helps the learner take the next step promptly and gives the coach an opportunity to return later that day to see what was learned.
Study instruction:
- "Review cycle timing and test an earlier check that allows the next experiment and learning review to occur during the same shift."
Key point:
- Schedule checks when they can influence the work.
- A target condition can specify when coaching should take place.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
42. Q: How should coaches use the five questions when they approach any process?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends using the five questions whenever approaching a process because the routine teaches both the mentee and the coach. Repeated use helps the coach maintain a disciplined view of target conditions, actual conditions, obstacles, experiments, and learning. It also reduces the tendency to jump to a solution or rely on assumptions formed away from the work. The questions should be grounded in direct observation and adapted to the learner's situation, not asked as a mechanical checklist. Consistent practice strengthens the coach's own scientific thinking while helping others develop it.
Study instruction:
- "Use the five questions to review a process you do not directly manage and note how the routine changes your assumptions."
Key point:
- The coach is also a learner and should practice the same thinking pattern.
- Consistency at the process reinforces the routine for both participants.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
43. Q: How can recognition and a sense of achievement support behavior development?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that success should be recognized throughout the Improvement Kata routine, not only when the final target condition is achieved. Learners can make progress by grasping a current condition more clearly, identifying an obstacle, designing a sound experiment, checking a prediction, or adjusting their understanding. Recognizing these intermediate achievements reinforces the behavior the organization wants to develop. If praise is reserved only for completed solutions or numerical results, people may avoid experiments that reveal unexpected information. Recognition should reinforce disciplined learning without overstating operational results.
Study instruction:
- "Identify intermediate learning achievements in a recent effort and decide how a coach could recognize them accurately."
Key point:
- Reinforce useful thinking and learning throughout the routine.
- Success is not limited to reaching the final target condition.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
44. Q: When should the organization create a broader plan for developing Improvement Kata behavior?
A: Show answer
After the advance group has spent a few months learning through application at selected processes, it can begin planning wider development. The plan should be based on firsthand understanding rather than assumptions made before practice. The chapter recommends that the first plan's horizon should not exceed 12 months because the organization is still on a steep learning curve and its understanding may change. Planning should not stop experiments; the advance group should keep practicing while the plan is developed so it remains connected to actual work.
Study instruction:
- "Confirm that the advance group has sufficient process experience before drafting a wider plan and limit the first horizon to 12 months."
Key point:
- Plan expansion after initial learning, not before it.
- A shorter first plan allows for substantial learning and adjustment.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
45. Q: Why should the organizational development plan be created iteratively through an A3-style process?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an iterative process in which the advance group presents its planning to a mentor through coaching cycles and develops one section at a time. Each section frames the next, while earlier sections can be revised before the plan is finalized. The value lies not only in the finished document but in repeatedly gathering facts, testing assumptions, and thinking more deeply. The chapter notes that this planning can take about two months. Process experiments should continue during this period so the plan remains grounded in actual conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Draft one A3 section at a time, record its evidence, and bring unresolved assumptions to the next mentor review."
Key point:
- The iterative planning process is itself a learning mechanism.
- Keep testing ideas while the plan is being developed.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
46. Q: What should the theme of an organizational development plan express?
A: Show answer
The theme should express the desired development of managers' and leaders' behavior toward the Improvement Kata pattern while remaining connected to continuous process improvement. The chapter cautions against introducing the kata merely for its own sake. Process improvement and learning the routine should occur simultaneously, with operational performance such as cost and quality remaining part of the purpose. A theme focused only on training numbers or culture language can lose its connection to the work. A useful theme links desired management behavior to the organization's longer-term direction.
Study instruction:
- "Write a one-sentence theme that links leader behavior development to a meaningful process or customer-related improvement direction."
Key point:
- The theme must connect behavior development with real process improvement.
- The kata is a means of improving work, not an end in itself.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
47. Q: Why does the development plan need a clear long-term vision?
A: Show answer
A long-term vision provides an overall direction so people do not move toward unrelated local objectives when they encounter obstacles. The chapter cautions against lengthy debates that produce vague statements protecting competing interests. A useful vision should be succinct and meaningful without prescribing every detail of the route. For manufacturing operations, the chapter cites one-piece flow at the lowest possible cost as an example of a broad direction. Organizations should use a vision that fits their context and should not confuse it with a detailed implementation plan. The vision guides target conditions while experimentation reveals the path.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether the current vision gives teams direction without dictating countermeasures or prematurely narrowing the route."
Key point:
- A vision provides direction when the path is uncertain.
- Keep the vision concise, useful, and connected to the organization's work.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
48. Q: What should be included when describing the current condition in the development plan?
A: Show answer
The current-condition section should summarize what the advance group has learned by applying the Improvement Kata at process level. At minimum, it should describe current manager and leader behavior and how process improvement is currently handled. Other relevant factors may be included when they affect development. Some aspects should be measurable so progress can later be evaluated. The description should be evidence-based and specific enough to expose gaps between existing routines and desired behavior. It should not be a generic judgment that the organization is or is not lean.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a current-condition statement using observed examples, process data, and measurable indicators of existing coaching and improvement behavior."
Key point:
- Describe actual leadership behavior and actual process-improvement practice.
- Include measurable facts that can be compared with future conditions.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
49. Q: How should the target condition for organization-wide development be defined?
A: Show answer
The target condition should describe a specific, measurable future state based on facts and data. The chapter identifies two dimensions: process-improvement activity and leader or coaching behavior. Process measures may include the number of processes managed through the Improvement Kata and measurable improvement such as stability. Behavior measures may specify who will reach particular capability levels, who will practice the Improvement or Coaching Kata, how frequently, and at how many processes. The target should be challenging but realistic in light of the current condition and available coaching capacity.
Study instruction:
- "Define at least one process outcome and one capability or coaching measure for the same future date."
Key point:
- Measure both operational improvement and development of people.
- A target condition should be specific, measurable, and grounded in facts.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
50. Q: Why should an organization avoid selecting too many focus processes at the beginning?
A: Show answer
The Improvement Kata is intended as a daily management approach, so a process does not leave the system when a temporary project ends. As the organization applies the routine to more processes, the number of ongoing focus processes accumulates. If too many are selected initially, coaches and leaders may be unable to provide sufficient attention and practice quality may decline. The chapter recommends beginning with fewer rather than more processes. A smaller scope makes it easier to observe conditions, learn the routine, and develop coaches before expanding responsibly.
Study instruction:
- "Compare proposed focus processes with available coach time and choose a scope that can receive consistent attention."
Key point:
- Start with a manageable number of processes.
- Expansion should follow demonstrated coaching capacity, not enthusiasm alone.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
51. Q: What capability levels can be used to describe progress in Improvement Kata development?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives three example levels. Level A, awareness, means a person has a basic understanding of what the Improvement Kata is and how it works. Level I means the person can effectively carry out the Improvement Kata. Level C means the person can effectively carry out both the Improvement Kata and a coaching routine. These labels help define desired capability. They should not be awarded automatically after a course or fixed number of practice sessions; actual performance must be observed at the process.
Study instruction:
- "Write observable criteria for A, I, and C in your context and identify evidence required to confirm each level."
Key point:
- Awareness, independent practice, and coaching are distinct capability levels.
- Capability is demonstrated through behavior, not attendance or tenure.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
52. Q: How should the organization move people from the current condition to the target condition?
A: Show answer
The advance group should involve people at the next organizational level, who will become mentees, in planning how to reach the target condition. If the advance group designs the entire route alone, it resembles traditional top-down instruction and misses an opportunity to develop ownership. Mentors may establish a target and sometimes a target condition, but learners should participate in working out how to get there. The plan should specify who will practice, when, where, and how, including the coaching cycles needed.
Study instruction:
- "Involve next-level managers in designing their practice schedule and record which parts of the plan they helped develop."
Key point:
- Mentees should participate in planning the route to the target condition.
- Practice plans should specify who, when, where, and how.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
53. Q: How do training activities relate to the A, I, and C capability levels?
A: Show answer
The chapter links awareness-level learning with a short classroom course and shop-floor exercises. The next stage is repeated Improvement Kata practice. Once a person demonstrates the ability to carry out the Improvement Kata effectively, that person can move into Coaching Kata practice. Progression from I to C is a capability gate, not a reward for time served or a fixed number of exercises. This structure keeps training aligned with demonstrated skill and prevents people from coaching others before they can reliably practice the routine themselves.
Study instruction:
- "Define the practical assessment a learner must pass before progressing from Improvement Kata practice to Coaching Kata practice."
Key point:
- Training should progress from awareness to practice and then coaching.
- Advancement depends on demonstrated capability rather than elapsed time.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
54. Q: How can the Dreyfus model help interpret different skill levels within Kata training?
A: Show answer
The chapter points to the Dreyfus model as a useful way to understand that people within the same broad capability level may have different degrees of skill. It identifies five stages: novice, competence, proficiency, expertise, and mastery. Two people who can both perform the Improvement Kata may still need different levels of guidance. Training should therefore respond to demonstrated capability, not simply assign everyone in a category the same practice. The model is a lens for understanding skill development; it does not replace direct observation of performance in the actual process.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two learners at the same broad level and identify how their experience changes the guidance each needs."
Key point:
- People within one capability category may have different skill levels.
- Coaching should adapt to the learner's actual development stage.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
55. Q: Why can experienced practitioners coach the next group while continuing to learn?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes experienced people coaching the next group as they progress through the organization. This transfers capability while keeping experienced practitioners connected to actual conditions and the current abilities of those they develop. Coaching is not merely a way to distribute knowledge downward; it also exposes the coach to real process conditions and reveals whether the teaching approach is working. The arrangement can create a chain of practice and coaching across levels, although real organizations will not progress in a perfectly orderly sequence. The structure should adapt to actual capability and context.
Study instruction:
- "Map how practitioners at each capability level could support learners at the next level while receiving feedback on their coaching."
Key point:
- Coaching others can strengthen the coach's understanding of current conditions.
- Capability development is a continuous flow, not a one-time handoff.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
56. Q: What two categories of metrics should be monitored in a Kata development effort?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes coaching-process metrics from process-improvement metrics. Coaching measures may include cycle start and stop times, the number of processes coached, who conducts coaching, how often cycles occur, and whether the next step from the fifth question was taken. The second category measures whether focus processes are improving, using indicators drawn from their target conditions. The categories must be examined together. A program can meet coaching activity targets while process performance remains unchanged, suggesting that coaching quality or target conditions need closer examination.
Study instruction:
- "Build a paired scorecard with coaching measures and process measures, then review their relationship."
Key point:
- Track both the practice of coaching and results at focus processes.
- Activity completion alone does not demonstrate improvement effectiveness.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
57. Q: What should managers investigate when coaching-cycle targets are met but process improvement is not occurring?
A: Show answer
Managers should examine how coaching is being done rather than assume the number of cycles proves effectiveness. They should review whether the coach understands the process, whether the target condition is meaningful, whether the learner is grasping current conditions, and whether experiments are checked for learning. They should also consider whether the dialogue has become a checklist or a solution-giving session. The chapter's logic is that coaching activity and process improvement must be connected. If progress is absent, the organization should adjust its teaching approach through PDCA.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a process with high coaching compliance but weak results and identify one specific coaching adjustment."
Key point:
- A high coaching count is not proof of effective coaching.
- Use process outcomes to diagnose the quality of the teaching approach.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
58. Q: How should data for coaching and process metrics be collected?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends obtaining information simply and, where feasible, going to the process to collect it. The learner should not routinely prepare metrics in an office and present them later if the facts can be observed at the worksite. Direct collection makes the interaction more like a pull system: coach and learner go where the work occurs and obtain information needed for the next decision. Simple paper-based recording may be sufficient. The important issue is that data accurately represent the process and are available for timely learning.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the source of each metric and move collection closer to the process where this improves accuracy and timeliness."
Key point:
- Collect facts at the process whenever practical.
- Keep measurement simple enough to support frequent learning.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
59. Q: Why should organizations be cautious about point systems and activity-based awards?
A: Show answer
Point systems can cause employees to focus on earning rewards rather than achieving target conditions or developing capability. Activities such as attending courses, completing forms, or conducting a set number of coaching cycles are easy to count, but they do not prove that a person can apply the Improvement Kata effectively. The chapter recommends recognizing demonstrated capability and achievement of target conditions rather than simply counting activity. Measures and recognition should reinforce intended behavior, not encourage superficial compliance or gaming.
Study instruction:
- "Review existing awards and identify whether each rewards activity, demonstrated skill, or meaningful target-condition achievement."
Key point:
- Do not confuse completed activities with demonstrated capability.
- Recognition should reinforce learning and results rather than point accumulation.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
60. Q: Why should reflection sessions be built into the organizational development plan?
A: Show answer
The organization will encounter unforeseen obstacles and learn that assumptions or tactics need adjustment. Scheduled reflection creates an opportunity to review what is going as planned, what is not, and what should change. The chapter suggests that the advance group might reflect every two weeks. These sessions are PDCA checks on the development effort itself and use information from more frequent coaching cycles. Without planned reflection, teams may continue executing a plan even after evidence shows it is not producing the desired behavior or process improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Schedule recurring reflection and define the evidence participants will bring from coaching cycles and focus processes."
Key point:
- Reflection is a planned part of executing and adjusting the strategy.
- Use learning from frequent cycles to inform periodic PDCA reviews.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
61. Q: How should an advance-group reflection session be structured?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends beginning by restating the overall theme and reminding participants why they are experimenting. This calibrates the group's thinking before it reviews progress. Participants can then use the five questions and record what is going as planned and what is not. Inputs should come from coaching cycles and direct process observations. The purpose is to understand obstacles and improve the approach, not to evaluate individuals or defend missed actions. A simple evidence-based format helps the group stay focused on learning and identify the next adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reflection board with the theme, reason for experimenting, five questions, and observations of expected and unexpected results."
Key point:
- Start with shared purpose and the reason for experimentation.
- Use reflection to learn from evidence, not assign personal blame.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
62. Q: Why must organizational reflections be depersonalized and data-based?
A: Show answer
When participants feel that a reflection is a personal performance evaluation, they may defend themselves and hide problems. That inhibits PDCA because the group becomes concerned with justification rather than learning. A depersonalized reflection treats unexpected results as information about the approach and remaining obstacles. Participants can discuss what was planned, what happened, and what should change without turning the review into blame. Data and direct observations keep discussion grounded. Openness does not remove accountability; it creates conditions in which problems can be seen and addressed honestly.
Study instruction:
- "Review language from a recent reflection and replace blame-oriented statements with factual descriptions of expected and observed conditions."
Key point:
- The purpose is to learn from the experiment, not judge the individual.
- Openness and evidence make useful adjustment possible.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
63. Q: What common obstacle arises when people create action lists too quickly?
A: Show answer
People often feel more comfortable listing actions than carefully studying a situation and selecting one next experiment. A long action list can create the appearance of progress while concealing untested assumptions. It also shifts attention away from learning whether a proposed step is appropriate. The chapter recommends finding one next step through the coaching questions and then seeing what is learned. The step may be additional observation rather than a countermeasure. Managers should reinforce clear reasoning, small experiments, and timely checks rather than the volume of actions.
Study instruction:
- "Take an action list from an improvement meeting and identify which items are evidence-based and which are premature assumptions."
Key point:
- A task list is not a substitute for an experiment.
- Select one evidence-based next step and schedule the learning check.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
64. Q: Why do senior leaders sometimes struggle to internalize the five coaching questions?
A: Show answer
Senior leaders may be accustomed to making decisions, setting targets, and directing actions, whereas the five questions require them to examine current conditions, explore obstacles, and help another person think through an experiment. The questions can feel slow when a leader expects to provide an immediate answer. The chapter identifies difficulty internalizing the questions as a common obstacle. Leaders need repeated practice on real processes and must experience how the questions reveal facts and develop capability. They should not treat them as a script while continuing to prescribe solutions.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a senior leader's coaching and note whether the questions develop the learner's reasoning or merely precede a prescribed answer."
Key point:
- Senior leaders must practice the questioning routine, not merely endorse it.
- The questions require a shift from directing solutions to developing thinking.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
65. Q: Why is checking and adjusting often neglected in improvement work?
A: Show answer
Many organizations are more comfortable taking action than examining whether the action produced the expected result. The chapter lists a tendency to do but not check and adjust as a common obstacle. Without checking, the team cannot compare its prediction with what happened or update its understanding. It may continue implementing an ineffective countermeasure or miss an unexpected result that reveals a better direction. Coaches should make the learning check explicit when agreeing on the next step and return at the planned time to observe the result. PDCA is incomplete without this discipline.
Study instruction:
- "For a recent action, reconstruct the prediction, actual result, and adjustment; identify any missing check."
Key point:
- Doing without checking prevents reliable learning.
- Every experiment needs an expected result and a timely review.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
66. Q: What is the risk of jumping to solutions before observing the current condition?
A: Show answer
Premature solutions are often based on assumptions rather than a sound understanding of the process. When teams skip observation and analysis, they may address symptoms, overlook constraints, or create countermeasures that do not fit the actual cause. The chapter identifies this tendency as a recurring obstacle. The routine requires people to grasp the current condition and define a target condition before testing next steps. Coaches should redirect solution-first thinking toward facts, process observation, and a clear explanation of what is preventing progress.
Study instruction:
- "Take a proposed countermeasure and identify the observations that support the problem definition and choice of action."
Key point:
- Understand the process before selecting a solution.
- Connect countermeasures to observed obstacles and testable expectations.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
67. Q: Why do mentors and mentees sometimes misunderstand Toyota-style coaching?
A: Show answer
Both parties may assume that the mentee is expected to guess the solution the mentor already has in mind. This turns coaching into a test of agreement rather than a process for developing the learner's thinking. The mentor may ask questions but still judge answers against a hidden preferred solution. The chapter explains that coaching should help the learner understand the situation and identify an appropriate next step, which may be further investigation. Mentors should make their purpose clear, listen to reasoning, and provide procedural guidance without disguising a predetermined answer as a question.
Study instruction:
- "After a coaching session, ask the learner what they believed the coach expected and compare it with the purpose of the questions."
Key point:
- Coaching is not a guessing game about the mentor's preferred solution.
- The dialogue should develop understanding and the next experiment.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
68. Q: Why is uncertainty about the path to a target condition uncomfortable?
A: Show answer
People often prefer a detailed plan that predicts every action in advance. The Improvement Kata accepts that the route to a target condition is not fully known and must be discovered through experiments. This can feel uncomfortable because people must act without the reassurance of a complete solution. The chapter emphasizes that a plan is a prediction, not a guarantee. Coaches should help learners focus on the next evidence-based step and the learning it should produce. Repeated practice makes it more natural to navigate uncertainty without pretending the future is fully predictable.
Study instruction:
- "Identify where a team treats a plan as certainty and rewrite the next phase as a testable prediction with a scheduled check."
Key point:
- The path is discovered through experiments rather than fully specified in advance.
- A plan is a prediction that must be checked and adjusted.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
69. Q: Why is repeating or revisiting earlier steps necessary in Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
People may interpret a request to repeat an observation or reconsider a target condition as evidence that they made a mistake. The chapter explains that iteration is essential because each pass can reveal deeper understanding. New information may show that the current condition was incomplete, the target condition poorly defined, or an obstacle misunderstood. Repeating a step is not automatically failure or wasted effort; it is part of scientific learning. Coaches should explain why revisiting the work is useful and focus attention on what new evidence has emerged.
Study instruction:
- "Find an improvement effort that revisited an earlier step and document what additional understanding the repetition produced."
Key point:
- Iteration deepens understanding and is part of the method.
- Repeat a step when new questions or evidence justify it, not as a ritual.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
70. Q: How can leaders prevent Kata development from being treated as an extra project?
A: Show answer
Employees may initially see the effort as additional work placed on top of normal duties. Leaders should demonstrate that the kata is intended to change how daily management is conducted, not create a parallel activity. Coaching cycles should take place at actual processes and be integrated with work priorities. The development plan should connect practice to meaningful operational target conditions. If the routine appears only as occasional training or project meetings, people may not experience it as a new management habit. Integration requires leaders to make time for practice and use it in ordinary decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Review the team's calendar and identify where kata practice can improve or replace existing management routines rather than add an unrelated meeting."
Key point:
- The kata should become part of daily management, not an extra project layer.
- Leaders must align time, priorities, and actual work with the desired routine.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
71. Q: How can coaching cycles be kept from becoming long, burdensome meetings?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises conducting cycles standing at the process, focusing on one target condition, asking the five questions, and ending when one next step is clear. Novice coaches may allow discussion to expand into many factors and lengthy debate. Process information and target-condition evidence should be available at the worksite so the conversation remains concrete. A cycle may often take about 15 minutes after participants gain experience. More extensive analysis can be handled separately, but it should not displace frequent short cycles. The coach should keep the dialogue purposeful and take the next step promptly.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a cycle and identify what can be shortened or moved to a separate analysis session without weakening the routine."
Key point:
- Keep the cycle short, process-based, and focused on one next step.
- Avoid turning coaching into an open-ended meeting.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
72. Q: What should an organization do if the planned development approach is not producing expected behavior?
A: Show answer
The organization should use PDCA to examine the teaching and coaching approach. It should compare intended behavior with what people actually do, study coaching observations, and identify where learners revert to familiar habits. The group can test a specific adjustment, such as changing the practice setting, coaching frequency, mentor guidance, or capability gate. The chapter argues that when process improvement is not developing as expected, the coaching may still be incorrect. The gap should prompt investigation and improvement of the development system rather than automatic repetition of the same training.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one gap between intended and observed behavior, form a testable explanation, and adjust one element of the teaching approach."
Key point:
- Use evidence of a capability gap to improve the development system.
- Do not respond to weak results by repeating the same training automatically.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
73. Q: Why is Improvement Kata practice a lifelong activity, even for experienced leaders?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents kata training as an ongoing pursuit of mastery. Experienced engineers, managers, and executives still need to practice routines learned as beginners because processes, products, and conditions continue to change. Practice also helps people detect and correct bad habits that can reappear under pressure. The value is that learning occurs while meaningful work is being done: people improve capability and contribute to real target conditions at the same time. There is no final point at which an organization can declare continuous improvement and adaptation complete.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal practice plan that includes regular coaching, observation of real work, and reflection on habits that need correction."
Key point:
- Mastery requires continuing practice, feedback, and adaptation.
- Practice remains valuable because work and conditions keep evolving.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
74. Q: How can an organization distinguish genuine capability from superficial compliance?
A: Show answer
Genuine capability is visible when people can independently grasp a process's current condition, define a meaningful target condition, identify obstacles, conduct small experiments, and use what they learn to adjust. Coaches should observe the quality of reasoning rather than simply check whether questions were recorded. Process measures should also show whether focus processes are moving toward target conditions. Superficial compliance is more likely when employees complete forms or scheduled cycles but cannot explain their predictions, evidence, or learning. Combine direct observation, capability assessment, and process outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a learner during live process work and record evidence of independent reasoning rather than document completion."
Key point:
- Capability is demonstrated in reasoning and behavior at the process.
- Use both observed skill and process progress to distinguish learning from compliance.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
75. Q: How should an organization balance standard routines with adaptation to local conditions?
A: Show answer
The kata provides a consistent pattern of thinking and coaching, while target conditions, obstacles, experiments, and teaching tactics must respond to local facts. The chapter discourages copying another organization's countermeasures without understanding current and target conditions. Standard questions create a shared way to learn, but they should not force identical solutions across different processes. The organization should preserve the discipline of the Improvement Kata while allowing experiments to reveal what is appropriate in each setting. This balances consistency in learning with flexibility in action.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which elements of the routine should remain consistent and which decisions must adapt to process-specific evidence."
Key point:
- Standardize the learning routine while adapting experiments to actual conditions.
- A common method does not require identical countermeasures everywhere.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- Lean Enterprise Institute: Kata
76. Q: What should leaders do if they endorse Improvement Kata publicly but continue managing through old habits?
A: Show answer
Compare leaders' stated commitment with observable behavior: how they review performance, respond to problems, allocate time, and coach managers. The chapter makes clear that middle managers watch senior leaders' actions to determine whether the change is real. Leaders should begin practicing on meaningful processes and use the five questions in management interactions. The advance group can support this learning but cannot substitute for executive practice. If leaders continue prescribing solutions, rewarding activity counts, or avoiding checks, those behaviors will undermine the desired culture regardless of announcements.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a leadership review and document behaviors that reinforce or contradict the intended kata pattern."
Key point:
- Visible leadership practice is more persuasive than verbal endorsement.
- Resolve contradictions between the announced change and daily management behavior.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
77. Q: What should an organization do if it wants to scale the kata before it has enough capable coaches?
A: Show answer
It should slow expansion and focus on developing a small internal coaching base. Select a manageable number of processes, give potential coaches repeated practice, and assess their capability through observation before assigning them to teach others. Senior managers should continue practicing, and an external mentor may accelerate internal development without taking over. Scaling too quickly can create nominal coaching cycles that neither develop learners nor improve processes. Expansion should be based on demonstrated capacity to support learners, not the number of people who attended an introductory course.
Study instruction:
- "Compare rollout size with the number of coaches who can demonstrate effective practice and set a capability-based expansion gate."
Key point:
- Scale only as fast as coaching capability can support.
- Build depth before breadth to protect practice quality.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
78. Q: What should a manager do when learners complete coaching cycles but still jump directly to solutions?
A: Show answer
Observe the dialogue and determine whether the coach reinforces observation and experimentation or rewards quick answers. The learner may not understand the target condition, may lack access to process facts, or may believe the mentor expects a particular countermeasure. The coach should revisit the current condition, ask what evidence supports the proposed solution, and use the fifth question to schedule a learning check. If the next step remains unclear, additional observation may be more appropriate than choosing a solution. The objective is to improve reasoning, not simply complete a script.
Study instruction:
- "Review a solution-first discussion and identify the question or observation that would have helped test assumptions before acting."
Key point:
- Redirect solution-first behavior toward facts, obstacles, and experiments.
- Examine learner thinking, not just whether the cycle occurred.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- AME: Kata Resources
79. Q: How can an organization maintain alignment between culture development and process performance?
A: Show answer
The development theme and target conditions should remain connected to measurable process improvement. The chapter warns against introducing Improvement Kata behavior as an end in itself. Organizations should track whether focus processes become more stable or otherwise improve in ways that matter to operational objectives, while also monitoring the coaching behavior intended to produce those results. This dual view prevents the effort from becoming a training program disconnected from performance and prevents short-term results from overshadowing capability development. Alignment comes from improving real work while building the ability to keep improving it.
Study instruction:
- "For each focus process, connect its target condition to a cost, quality, delivery, or stability measure and pair it with a coaching measure."
Key point:
- Capability development and process performance should advance together.
- The routine exists to improve real work and the ability to keep improving it.

- Toyota Kata, Chapter 9: Developing Improvement Kata Behavior in Your Organization
- McGraw Hill: Toyota Kata
